I have heard that said of every Speaker since I can remember!” To which I re-answered, “Yes; but this time they are right.”
Which reminds me of how a modern poet, spared to middle age in spite of the wrath of God, famous for that he could neither scan nor rhyme﻿—let alone think or feel﻿—once made a speech in which he carefully set out those things which had been said against Swinburne when first that meteor flamed across the heaven of English verse.

===Chinese===

每个人与所有人的关系都得记住；辩论事项的性质，某人因任职经历或个人劳绩而应得的关照，得记住；另一个人（正蒙受某种不公的失败）的诉求，得承认；第三个人爱重复啰嗦，是隐患；第四个人是个疯子。同时，发言权要求大小之间极细微的差别、辩论的时长、乃至每位发言者过度的沉闷，都得权衡。在这之上，还得掂量每场辩论的重要性（就其可称重要而言——但对参与者来说它们重要得荒唐）。再在这之上，还有党鞭们提出、议长掌握的辩论总安排。
如今在这桩复杂得难以置信的差事上——我估计一天要作成百上千个内在决断——劳瑟先生从未失误过一次。他那个时代的议院是一件乐器，他对它有完全的掌控，凭借一种完全的理解和一种迅捷而公正的决断力，我在任何指挥领域都不曾见过可与之比肩的。
今天的人惯于使用超出场合的强词，而“天才”是个很强的词；但劳瑟先生——当我日复一日注视着他——展现的是天才，对此我不比怀疑莫扎特之于音乐、乌敦之于雕塑有天才更多一分怀疑。它令人惊叹，令人满意，从不停歇。
那些年我常与一位乡绅老先生共餐，他对那个仍是贵族制的英格兰的俱乐部里的人与事知之甚深。我初次入选那庄严的议会之后不久，就在他自己的餐桌上热情地告诉他，我对这项成就的评价。他倦怠地答道：“哦！自我记事以来，每一位议长我听人这么说过！”我回敬道：“是；可这一回他们说对了。”
这又让我想起一位现代诗人——他顶着上帝的震怒活到了中年，出名的是既不会格律也不会押韵——更别提思考与感受——有一次发表演说，仔细列举了当年斯温伯恩这颗流星初掠英国诗的天空时，人们说过的那些反对之词。

===Original===

The modern poet next read to this assembly the things that had been written against himself when he first blurred into the murk of our evening. He triumphantly concluded: “What was said against Swinburne they have said against me!” Then there arose an aged writer of reviews, a man whose hair, whose voice, and whose aura were all three of delicate silver. He said: “Yes! But in what they said of him they were wrong; in what they said of you, they were right.”
This was not an epigram, it was a paving-stone; and it reminded me of that line in the epic of Roncesvalles, “Christians are right and pagans are wrong.”
So in the matter of Speakers. I have seen the action of four; but of none would I say even that they had talent, saving in this case alone, where I have used another word.﻿ ﻿…
So we drifted down the narrow entry and out into the open sea; and all that afternoon, under a wind now slightly lifting, now falling again, we crept eastward and a little south, making more way as the sun declined, because the wind was shifting westward on to our quarter; and of that I was glad, for I desired to look into Port Madoc, which I had not seen since I was a child. I had vivid memories of it during a wonderful journey overshadowed by that air wherewith the Creator blesses childhood, lending to everything an active flavour of the divine; which is in three things, Clarity, Magnitude, and Multiplicity of strong emotion.
For the divine reveals itself in a special multiplicity, in an infinite variety. All that there is in colour and in music, and in line and in affection, and to these added other raptures innumerable, such as we know not of nor can conceive﻿—that is to be at last our beatitude: that is the fullness of being. In childhood our innocence permits us some little glimpse of such things; but with the passage of the years they are lost altogether. The light in the lantern goes out, and the living thing within us fails, and is stupefied, and dies.
So Musset and old Pythagoras and Mr. Tupper. If any man doubts the Fall of Man (and I see from the papers that two belated bishops and one dean are still shaky on it, through some Victorian influence upon their minds, not only of bones but of chipped stones), let him consider this decay of heaven within ourselves as the maturity of our manhood develops.

===Chinese===

接着这位现代诗人向听众念了当年他自己初在我们黄昏的昏暗中模糊出现时，人们写下的反对之词。他得意洋洋地总结道：“他们反对斯温伯恩的话，也都说到了我头上！”这时站起来一位年迈的评论作家，一个头发、嗓音和气场全都是纤柔银色的男人。他说：“是啊！可他们说他的那些话，他们错了；说你的那些话，他们说对了。”
这不是一句隽语，这是一块铺路石；它让我想起《龙塞斯瓦列斯史诗》里的那句诗：“基督徒是对的，异教徒是错的。”
议长们也是如此。我见过四位的行事；除这一位我不曾说哪怕他们有才干，唯独在这一位身上，我用了另一个词。……
于是我们漂下那条狭窄的水道，出到外海；那个下午，风忽而稍起，忽而又落，我们缓缓向东、略偏南地爬行，日头西斜时船速渐增，因为风转向西，落到我们尾舷上来了；对此我很高兴，因为我渴望去看看波特马多克，自童年以后我再没见过它。我对它有鲜活的记忆，那是一趟奇妙的旅程，笼罩着造物主赐予童年的那种气息，它赋予一切一种神性的活泼滋味；神性就在三样东西里：清澈、宏大，以及强烈情感的繁盛。
因为神性以一种特殊的繁盛、一种无限的多样启示自己。色彩中、音乐中、线条中、爱恋中的一切，再加上无数我们不知也无法想象的别的狂喜——那终将成为我们的至福：那是存在的圆满。童年时，我们的纯真允许我们瞥见这些东西的一斑；但随着岁月流转，它们便全然失去了。灯笼里的光熄了，我们里面的活物衰败、麻木、死去。
缪塞如此，老毕达哥拉斯如此，塔珀先生也如此。谁若怀疑人的堕落（我从报上看到，两位过时的主教和一位座堂主任牧师在这个问题上仍摇摆不定，出于某种维多利亚时代对他们头脑的影响——不仅有骨头的影响，还有打制石器的影响），就让他看看，随着我们成年期的成熟，我们里面的天国是怎样衰败的吧。

===Original===

The more we are of this world and the more we know of it, the further are we drifting from the shores of the Blessed. Which shores also the poet had in mind when he wrote, sang, crooned, or whatnot:

> “Sing to me of the islands, oh, Daughter of Cahoolin, sing,

Sing to me of the West,

Sing to me of the girth loosened and the lax harpstring

And of rest.
> “Beyond the skerries and beyond the outer water,

There lies the land.

Sing to me of the islands, oh, Daughter of Cahoolin, oh, High King’s Daughter,

And of the over-strand.
> “I desire to be with Brandan and his companions, in the quiet places

And to drink of their spring.

Sing to me of the islands, oh, Daughter of Cahoolin, and of the blessed faces.

Daughter of Cahoolin, sing.”

But if you tell me that that is not the way to spell “Cuchulin,” I have two answers, each of them stronger than Hercules.
The first is that the poet spelt it as he felt inclined﻿—that is, by ear; for poets excel not, as is commonly believed, in speech but in ears; as you may see by their profiles or sidehead views; and as it is his spelling your action lies against him, not me.
And the second answer is, that one ought never to be bothered with the pedantry of quaint alphabets and the spelling tricks of outer men. Cuchulin is, or was, or should be, pronounced “Cahoolin,” and there is an end of it. But as to whether he ever had a daughter, or if he had, whether she could sing, or even whether that Irishman existed at all, I hold all these things to be perfectly indifferent.﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===

我们越是属于这个世界、越是了解它，就离蒙福者的海岸越远。诗人写下、唱出、低吟或随便怎样吟出下面这些诗句时，心中想着的也正是那片海岸：

> “为我唱唱那些海岛吧，哦，卡胡林之女，唱吧，

为我唱唱西方，

为我唱唱松开的腰带与松弛的琴弦，

还有安息。
> “越过礁岛，越过外海的水，

那里便是那片陆地。

为我唱唱那些海岛吧，哦，卡胡林之女，哦，至高王之女，

还有那漫过的沙滩。
> “我愿与布兰丹及其同伴同在，在那些安静的地方，

饮他们泉中的水。

为我唱唱那些海岛吧，哦，卡胡林之女，还有那些蒙福的面容。

卡胡林之女，唱吧。”

可你若告诉我“库丘林”（Cuchulin）不是这样拼的，我有两个回答，每一个都比赫拉克勒斯更有力。
第一个是：诗人想怎么拼就怎么拼——也就是凭耳朵；因为诗人的过人之处，世人普遍以为在言语，其实不然，在耳朵；看看他们的侧脸便知；而且既然这拼法是他的，你要找磴也该找他，不是我。
第二个回答是：外族人那些古怪字母的学究气与拼写把戏，永远不该来烦人。库丘林现在是、曾经是、也应该是读作“卡胡林”的，如此而已。至于他究竟有没有过一个女儿，若有，她会不会唱歌，甚至那个爱尔兰人究竟存在过没有，我认为这些全都无关紧要。……

===Original===

We cast anchor in the very midmost of that solemn bay with its half-circle of huge mountains looking down upon an empty sea. The giants were dim in the haze, but the more enormous, and I revered and worshipped them.
We so cast anchor because I had to wait for the tide. I could not run up the long, winding channel through the sands of Port Madoc until the flood should be with me, and that would not be till the gloaming, between eight and nine o’clock that night.
Therefore did we lie thus in bo, gazing at the great hills of Wales. There is no corner of Europe that I know, not even the splendid amphitheatre standing in tiers of high Alpine wall around Udine, which so moves me with the awe and majesty of great things as does this mass of the northern Welsh mountains seen from this corner of their silent sea.
Few can recall it, for few visit that corner of the salt. It leads nowhere but to the harbour of Port Madoc. No man beaches a boat today under Harlech; no man today sets out from that shore for Ireland beyond. The halls are in ruin. There is no more harping. No flight of sails comes up eastward out of the sea like birds. Even the sailors have forgotten Gwynnedd. Today the only sailors familiar with the solemnity of which I speak are those who ply in the small boats of the slate trade, or who, like myself, have employed a curious leisure in searching out new things here in the seas of home. For all my life I have made discoveries close at hand, and have found the Island of Britain to be infinite. But who in our times knows where to look for vision?
Indeed, this lack of fame applies to perhaps half the greater visions, even of the modern over-frequented and travelled earth. Men know half of them to satiety, but the other half they never see. Everyone has wearied of the Bay of Naples, repeated a thousand times, but what of that lonely field by the flat Adriatic sand whence you may see the dark eastern Fall of the Gran Sasso, tragic, with storms about it, dominating a deserted shore? Everybody has his bellyful of Gavarnie, but what of the valley of Araxas, which proclaims so terribly the glory of God? Where are the pictures of that? Who has drawn it? Yet it is but half a day from Gavarnie.

===Chinese===

我们在那庄严海湾的正中央抛了锚，半环的巨山俯瞰着一片空海。群巨人隐在雾霭里，却愈显庞大，我敬畏它们，崇拜它们。
我们这样抛锚，是因为我必须等潮。潮不涨上来，我没法沿波特马多克沙洲间那条长长的弯水道上行，而那要等到黄昏，当晚八九点之间。
于是我们就这样泊着，凝望威尔士的群山。我所知道的欧洲没有一个角落——连乌迪内周围那座层层高墙般的壮丽阿尔卑斯圆形剧场也不算——能像从这片沉静之海的这一角望见的北威尔士群山那样，以伟大事物的敬畏与庄严打动我。
能回忆起它的人寥寥，因为很少有人造访那片咸水的角落。它哪儿也不通，只通波特马多克港。今天再没有人把船泊在哈勒赫下面；再没有人从那片海岸启程去往爱尔兰。厅堂已成废墟。竖琴声绝。再没有船帆像群鸟一样从海上向东涌来。连水手们都忘了圭内斯。今天，熟悉我所说的这种庄严的水手，只剩那些在板岩贸易的小船里谋生的人，或如我一般，怀着古怪的闲暇在本乡的海域寻访新事物的人。我一生都在近处做发现，发现不列颠岛是无穷无尽的。可在我们这个时代，谁知道去哪里寻找异象呢？
说真的，这种默默无闻，大约也适用于半数的伟大景观，即便是现代这个人满为患、游踪遍及的大地上。人们对其中一半熟腻到厌倦，另一半却从不曾见。人人都厌倦了被重复千遍的那不勒斯湾，可亚得里亚海平坦沙滩旁那片孤寂的原野呢——从那里可以望见格兰萨索黑暗的东壁，悲怆，周身绕着风暴，君临一片荒废的海岸——又有谁提起？人人都看饱了加瓦尔尼，可阿拉克萨斯山谷呢——它那样骇人地宣告上帝的荣光？画它在哪儿？谁画过它？它离加瓦尔尼不过半日路程。

===Original===

So it is with this awful parade of the great mountains standing on guard over the northern corner of Cardigan Bay, seen from the silence and the flat of ocean, towering above its glass; and all that late afternoon and evening I adored them until, with the last of the light, and a westerly air which was but the suggestion of a breeze, we groped north anxiously for the opening to Port Madoc channel. How I should make it, even upon the flood, in the darkness, I knew not; for the sands there are miles wide, and this channel (which I had never yet made) shifts continually. But God sent me a pilot.
He hailed us out of the half-darkness from a small boat, and asked us in the dialect which I will call “Anglo-Welsh” (after Anglo-Catholic, Anglo-German, Anglo-Indian, Anglo-African, and the rest), “Whether we would not have a pilot, ah?”
Nor was he a pilot, as the event shall show; but at any rate he belonged to that shore, and would have more knowledge than I. So I gave him the helm, and went up forward to look out over the bows, as the *Nona* glided slowly along the flood into the channel which they call “The Water of the Smooth Lake” (“Afon Glaslyn”). For half an hour of that very slow gliding all went well. The darkness had quite fallen. There was no moon.
The gliding stopped; there was a slight thrill. She had hit Wales: an underwater, advance guard of Wales. The man at the helm was not apologetic, he was not humble, but he was at least subdued; and he said, “Her will float soon, so her will!” I forbore to reproach him, not from kindness, but from cowardice.
The flood lifted her foot, she swung off, and we went on again up the darkness, with the least of little airs to give steering-way.
So for a quarter of an hour, covering perhaps a third of a mile: then again, rather sharper this time, came the honest thrust into the sand, and, what was more, she had run up against it so high this time that she was careening a little; she was taking it very hard, as the highbrows used to say of their beloved Prussia after the Armistice.
This time it was a good half-hour before she floated, and all that time the culprit was silent, nor did I reproach him. He at least had some vague memory of where the channel might be, and that I certainly had not.

===Chinese===

卡迪根湾北角上这群岿然守卫的巨山，从大洋的寂静与平坦之上、从它镜面的高处望去，也是如此；那天傍晚直到入夜，我一直崇拜着它们，直到最后一线光里，在一股只算得上微风之影的西风中，我们焦急地摸索着向北找波特马多克水道的入口。即便乘着涨潮，在黑暗里我该怎么进去，我不知道；那片沙洲宽达数英里，而这条我从没走过的水道又不停改移。但上帝给我派来了一位引水员。
他从半暗里的一条小船上朝我们喊话，用一种我愿称之为“盎格鲁-威尔士语”的方言（仿“盎格鲁-公教”“盎格鲁-德意志”“盎格鲁-印度”“盎格鲁-非洲”等例），问我们“要不要来个引水的，啊？”
他也不是什么引水员，后文自见分晓；但至少他是那一带岸上的人，总比我懂得多。于是我把舵交给他，自己到船头去瞭望，诺娜号乘着涨潮缓缓滑进那条他们唤作“平滑湖之水”（Afon Glaslyn）的水道。这样缓慢滑行了半个钟头，一切顺利。夜色已经全黑。没有月亮。
滑行停了；轻轻一震。她撞上了威尔士：威尔士的一支水下先头部队。掌舵的人没有歉意，也不谦卑，但至少蔫了；他说：“她很快就会浮起来，就会的！”我没有责备他，不是出于善良，而是出于怯懦。
涨潮抬起了她的脚，她一摆滑脱，我们又继续在黑暗里上行，靠一丝最小的微风维持着舵效。
这样走了一刻钟，大约三分之一英里：然后又一次，这回更干脆地，诚实地顶进沙里；而且这回她冲得那么高，船身都有些侧倾了；她吃这个吃得够呛，如同高知们在停战之后谈论他们心爱的普鲁士时常说的那样。
这回足足过了半个钟头她才浮起，这期间那位罪人一言不发，我也没有责备他。他至少对水道可能在哪儿还有些模糊的记忆，而我肯定一点也没有。

===Original===

As we lay there, waiting for the rising water to lift us, a clear sound of hammering rang from the black shore, far away, over the vast expanse of sand. It meant something to the pseudo-pilot, for he called out in loud, singsong tones five or six words in the Welsh language, wherein I distinguished the word “afon,” which means “water”; and his very distant Kymric fellow, there unperceived in the night, answered with a distant cry. What they said I know not; perhaps the unjust man at the helm was asking at what hour she would have water enough to lie higher up.
She floated again (it was now deep into the night, and the dim stars, through the haze overhead, watched our misery). The *Nona* glided on.

---

===Chinese===

我们躺在那里等上涨的水把我们抬起时，一声清晰的锤击声从黑暗的岸上远远传来，越过茫茫沙滩。这声音对那位假引水员有意义，因为他用响亮的、唱歌似的调子喊出了五六个威尔士语词，其中我分辨出一个“afon”，意思是“水”；他那远得几乎看不见的同族凯尔特人，在夜色里无人察觉之处，遥遥应了一声。他们说了什么我不知道；也许掌舵的那个不义之徒正在问几点钟水才够她往更上游去。
她又浮起来了（这时夜已深，朦胧的星隔着头顶的雾霭注视着我们的悲惨）。诺娜号继续前行。

===Original===

To be coming thus into a very shoal fairway, after dark, and to be in the hands of a pilot who was quite clearly one of God’s Three Welsh Fools﻿—one of the Triad, one of the Three Great Fools of Britain﻿—was a strain to the temper, a strain to breaking point. It was no good my taking the tiller, for I had no idea of the channel, and only saw now and then, straining my eyes forward, a little blob on the darkness that would be a drum-headed buoy slowly drifting past as we lifted on the young flood.
I used to think that the irritation against fools was irrational and purposeless. Where it is written in Holy Writ that one should tolerate fools even with gladness, I thought that this was a general rule of conduct. But now I know it to be a counsel of perfection and, indeed, like so many things in the Old Testament, a counsel generally to be avoided. For the strong exasperation against fools is, I am now sure, an instinct implanted in us (or, as fools themselves would say, “evolved”) whereby we defend ourselves against such accidents as this perpetual bumping which the *Nona* was undergoing on the sands of Port Madoc. Our anger against fools is a natural faculty of conservation, like the sensitiveness of the nerves of the skin; and in those countries where fools are too much tolerated (of which England) many little disasters proceeding from such tolerated fools combine to make a great one at last and to break down the state.
It is high time that a new book were written on Fools, like those of the sixteenth century, which are so excellent, and which you have in Latin, in German, in French, and in English: *The Praise of Folly*, *The Ship of Fools*, *The Fool’s Paradise*, and others. If I had the leisure, it is one of the many hundred books which I should like to write myself: *The Fool, Sociological, Biological, and Pathological, with Many Portraits and an Appendix on the Newly Discovered Giant Fool of Aphasia*. As I have not the leisure (and never had it) I bequeath the idea to other and younger and wealthier men, just as some years ago I bequeathed them the idea of a *Cads’ Encyclopaedia*.
Among other chapters in this book I would have one on the Fool and the Label. For I maintain that labels are the great support and sustenance of the Human Fool.
Labels help the fool to do what little dull thinking he manages to get through.

===Chinese===

天黑之后就这样进一条极浅的航道（fairway），又落在一位明明白白是上帝“威尔士三愚人”之一——三题诗里、不列颠三大愚人之一——的引水员手里，对脾气是种考验，考验到断裂的边缘。我去掌舵也没用，因为我根本不知道水道在哪儿，只能时而极目前方，看见黑暗中一小团东西——那是一只鼓形浮标，在我们乘着初涨的水缓缓抬起时慢慢从旁边漂过。
我从前以为，对蠢人的恼怒是非理性、无目的的。《圣经》里写着要甘心容忍愚人，我曾以为这是一条一般的行为准则。如今我明白那是一条成全的劝谕，而且实在像《旧约》里的许多话一样，是一条通常应当避开的劝谕。因为对蠢人的强烈愤懑，我现在确信，是一种植于我们体内的本能（或者照蠢人自己的说法，“进化”出来的），借此我们保卫自己，免遭诸如诺娜号在波特马多克沙洲上这没完没了的磕碰之类的事故。我们对蠢人的怒火是一种天然的保全机能，如同皮肤神经的敏感；在那些过分容忍蠢人的国度（英格兰为其一），被容忍的蠢人酿出的无数小灾小难终会汇成大难，把国家压垮。
早该有人写一本新的《愚人书》了，像十六世纪那些精彩的著作那样，拉丁文的、德文的、法文的、英文的都有：《愚人颂》《愚人船》《愚人天堂》，等等。我若有闲暇，这是我自己想写的几百本书之一：《愚人：社会学、生物学与病理学研究，附多幅肖像及论新发现之失语巨愚的附录》。我既然没有闲暇（从来也没有），就把这个想法遗赠给别的更年轻更有钱的人，正如几年前我把《下流佬百科全书》的想法遗赠给他们一样。
这本书里除了别的章节，我要写一章《愚人与标签》。因为我坚持认为，标签是人类愚人的伟大支柱与营养。
标签帮愚人完成他那一点点可怜的、勉强做得完的沉闷思考。

===Original===

He knows whom to respect and whom to despise and what wine to choose, and so on﻿—all by the label. And then again, labels are very useful to the fool by getting him taken for more than he is worth. Any number of my friends have said to me at one time or another, when I was having my laugh at the politicians: “A man does not get to a position like that (Secretary of State for Drainage, or Prime Minister, or whatnot) without abilities.” Now this error shows how useful the label is to the fool. For a matter of fact I can testify, if any living man can, that the politicians who get their share of the swag are of all degrees in the matter of intelligence. Some have first-rate abilities, especially among the lawyers; many are of the ordinary second-rate, fifty-eight percent, β *minus* or Wandsworth intelligence; and quite a large proportion are Plumb Stuffed Fools, true Fools Absolute and of the Nadir; Rooted Fools. I knew one Fool Secretary of State who, in his Whitehall office, used to look at his official documents with a sort of tragic stare, as men look on the dead, and slowly wag them up and down in front of his face with a hopeless gesture. Then he would call upon his permanent official, who would explain to him what they meant, whereupon he would use one after another of the great Fool Phrases which are the furniture of the Fool’s Mind, such as, “Yes,” “I see,” “Quite,” “Precisely,” and then again “Yes.” Having done this, he would sign his name to a number of papers which meant no more to him than so many Chinese tracts, and with a groan he would go off to spend his hundred pounds a week at Brighton.
Even in those trades where you would think there must be activity of brain for a man to advance at all, your fool drifts forward somehow. You find him quite high up in armies, and as for his number in the great cheating professions, notably share-shuffling, it is prodigious. Half the wealthy share-shufflers I have known have been fools animal, fools crass, fools to be driven blindfold or led about on a halter; but to do that great modern profession justice, there is a minority of share-shufflers as keen as needles and as wide-awake as ferrets. They have no more luck, however, than their duller brethren, for Fate rules them all, and alternate wealth and poverty is as much the rule with them as with the stupidest men.

===Chinese===

该尊敬谁、该鄙视谁、该选哪种酒，等等——全凭标签。再者，标签对愚人还大有用处，能让人把他估得比他本身的身价高。我的朋友里不知有多少人，在我取笑政客的当口，对我说过：“一个人能爬到那样的位置（排水事务大臣，或首相，或诸如此类），不会没有能力的。”这个错误正说明标签对愚人多有用。凭事实我可以作证——倘若世上还有人有资格作证的话——分得赃款的政客们，智力上什么档次都有。有些有一流的能力，尤其在律师出身者中间；许多是平平的二流，百分之五十八、乙等减、万兹沃斯智力；还有相当大一部分是铅灌到底的愚人，真正的、坠入天底的绝对愚人；扎根的愚人。我认识一位愚人国务大臣，他在白厅的办公室里习惯用一种悲剧性的凝视看他的公文，像人看死者一样，然后绝望地拿着它们在脸前慢慢上下晃。然后他叫来他的常任文官，由文官向他解释这些文件是什么意思，于是他把“愚人语录”——愚人头脑里的家什——一句接一句用上：“是”“我明白”“不错”“正是”，然后又是“是”。做完这些，他就在一沓文件上签下名字，那些文件对他不比一沓中文小册子更有意义，然后哼一声，动身去布莱顿花他每周一百镑去了。
即便在那些你会以为脑子不活络就根本升不上去的行当里，愚人也能莫名其妙地往上漂。军队高层里能见到他；至于在伟大的行骗行当里——尤其是股票洗牌业——他的数目更是惊人。我认识的殷实的洗牌客，一半是血肉愚人、冥顽愚人、蒙着眼也能赶着走、套上笼头牵着走的愚人；但公道地说，这个伟大的现代行业里，也有一小撮洗牌客，尖利如针，警醒如雪貂。不过他们的运气并不比他们迟钝的同侪更好，因为命运统治着他们全体，贫富交替之于他们，正如之于最蠢的人一样，是铁律。

===Original===

I will bargain that if you were to take samples of share-shufflers out of three sacks, the House of Lords, Monte Carlo, and the gaols, you would find the same percentage of fools in each sample.
Which leads me on to say that never was an old proverb of less accurate modern application than the proverb about the fool and his money. You will have heard it said that the fool and his money are soon parted. That was true enough for the yokel at the fair, but in this modern city life of ours it is just the other way. The Fool of Inherited Money (not the Gambling Fool) holds on to it with fishhooks. It is the intelligent man, with many interests and (by some freak of nature) a generous heart, who squanders his hoard. But your fool gets his legs round the moneybag and crosses his ankles on the other side, clasps it higher up with both arms right round it and fingers tightly interlocked beyond; digs his teeth into the mouth of the same and, screwing up his eyes, clutches it all over desperately with the rigour of death, making himself wholly one with the beloved object. From this attitude nothing can move him save the lure of a plausible fellow promising to increase his wealth. I have seen a clot of fools thus hanging on to moneybags, all pressed up together like bats in a steeple, when a repulsive, overdressed young adventurer would slither by singing a light song of great wealth to be made in some scheme of his devising; and, behold, the fools would turn their round heads ungummed from their moneybags and listen in rapture to that song, and many of them would even hand over their bags to the young adventurer, never to see their gold again. But I say that, save when they are thus tempted by their blind avarice, fools are not easily parted from their money.
However, this harbour or pilot fool of mine was not, I think, troubled in this regard, for had he been a Fool of Wealth he would not have been blundering to get the *Nona* up-channel in the dark.
Five separate times did we touch and wait with increasing anger each time till the flood released us, but at last we came to the pool, and to reasonable deep water, and to a quay.
There did we put out our warping ropes, and springs, and the fenders alongside, and there did we give money to the fool as a reward for all the pain his folly had given us.

===Chinese===

我敢打赌，倘若你从三只口袋——上议院、蒙特卡洛和监狱——里各抓一把洗牌客抽样，三份样本里愚人的比例会一模一样。
这就引得我说：没有哪句老谚语比“愚人与其钱”那句更不适用于现代了。你一定听过人说，愚人与他的钱很快分手。这话对集市上的乡巴佬够真实，可在我们现代的城市生活里恰恰相反。继承财产的愚人（不是赌博的愚人）用鱼钩把钱钩得死死的。倒是那个兴趣广泛、（天生异禀地）心肠大方的聪明人，把家财散尽。而你的愚人，两腿盘住钱袋，脚踝在另一侧交叠，双臂整个环抱上去再往上扣紧，十指在袋外死死相扣；牙齿咬进袋口，眯起眼，全身上下死死攥住它，带着死亡的僵硬，让自己与心爱的物件完全合一。能让他挪窝的，只有一个花言巧语的家伙许诺让他的财富增值的诱饵。我见过一簇愚人这样挂在钱袋上，挤作一团，像钟楼里的蝙蝠；这时来了一个招人嫌的、衣着过盛的年轻冒险家，哼着轻快的歌走过，歌唱他设计的某个行当里可以赚到的大钱；看哪，愚人们便把他们圆圆的脑袋从钱袋上撕下来，狂喜地听那歌，其中不少甚至把钱袋递到年轻冒险家手里，从此再不见他们的金子。可我要说，除非这样被盲目的贪心诱惑，愚人的钱是不容易分手的。
不过我这位港口引水愚人，我想在此事上并无烦恼，因为他若是个财富愚人，就不会摸黑引着诺娜号在水道里乱撞了。
我们一共搁浅了五次，一次比一次愤怒地等着涨潮放行，但终于到了那片深水潭，到了像样的深水，到了一座码头。
在那里我们放出倒缆、系缆，把碰垫靠船边放好；也在那里，我们把钱付给那愚人，作为他愚蠢带给我们全部痛苦的报酬。

===Original===

For also this is the rule in human life; that when you suffer anything you must pay a good price for the entertainment.
It is true of bad cooking, and of bad wine, and of a painful illness, started or continued by doctors, and of all the intolerable noisy hotels, full of insolence and maddening bells. It is true of all the bad things of the world. Nor does it follow that all cheap things are good. Far from it! But all good things are cheap (except caviar), and the way to get good things is to look for them as you would look for a penny which you had dropped in the straw of the (once again!) dear old omnibuses of my youth which were pulled by horses, and had standing at their door a guardian of the poorest of the people, foul in speech, grimy of skin, unshaven, bloodshot, from the gutter﻿—but (oh, believe me!) a better citizen of a better time than ours.
*Nona*, cruising and voyaging *Nona*, wanderer over the seas of Britain, how in the solitude of your companionship my mind does lead me from one thing to another!
But that’s all one. It is time for sleep.

---

===Chinese===

人间的事也通这条规矩：你受了什么罪，还得为这份消遣付个好价钱。
糟糕的厨艺如此，糟糕的酒如此，被医生们发起或维持的痛苦疾病如此，所有那些充斥傲慢与逼疯人的铃声的嘈杂不堪的旅馆也如此。世间一切坏东西皆然。但这并不反过来意味着一切便宜东西都是好的。差得远！但一切好东西都便宜（鱼子酱除外），得到好东西的办法，是像找一枚掉在（又是它！）我青年时代那些马拉的亲爱的老公共马车干草里的一便士那样去找它们——那车门边总站着一个最穷人阶层的看守，言语污秽，皮肤油腻，胡子不刮，眼睛通红，出身阴沟——但是（哦，请相信我！）他是比我们更好的时代里更好的公民。
诺娜号，巡航与远航的诺娜号，在不列颠诸海上漂泊的流浪者，在你相伴的孤寂里，我的心绪怎样把我从一件事牵到另一件事啊！
不过这都一样。该睡了。

===Original===

The new day having come, we got the half-ebb a little before six o’clock, and threaded away down the Channel for the open sea.
I ought, I suppose, to have stopped in Port Madoc, and renewed the memories of my childhood. But a fig for the memories of my childhood, at six o’clock in the morning: at six o’clock of a May morning, and a nice little leading breeze, all cold and merry! The memories of childhood and the contemplation of the divine are for the evening; they go with candlelight, and with a wine I know, and with friends of twenty years. But, so help me He that made me, when I find the morning wind blowing well for the salt and myself freshly roused from a good sleep, I am full of nothing but the coming of the course and an eagerness for the line of the sea against the sky and the making of a further shore.
It ought to be more dangerous to float down on the ebb without a local trickster, than to come up upon the flood. But fortune served, and the swirl of the ebb plainly marked the channel under that heartening light, with the glory of the new day shooting over the tops of the great and solemn mountains eastward, by the land.
Therefore, without misadventure, we came to the last marking buoy and took the sea; running easily with the wind nearly aft, but a little on the port quarter, so that all was well.
The great field or marsh before Harlech stretched there to landward; and, on its splendid defiant height, the old castle watched us as we went; the place where they hid poor Henry the Saint when the true Plantagenet was pacing for his blood.
Here is a landscape inhabited everywhere by things not of this world; by the gods of these hills whom our God has ousted; by the memory of great men dead and their wraiths, I suppose; and by the troops of the Empire, marching up the inland road, and by the chieftains, and by the very saintly men who cast their spell fifteen hundred years ago over the islands and the rocks of Lyonesse.
So we sailed on and on, heading over to the Atlantic southern corner of Wales. Then, far away in the midst of the sea, we heard the tolling of a bell.﻿ ﻿…
The tolling of the bell was not only mournful (and at the same time terrible), but had in it an odd quality of message; as though it were not of the world through which we moved.

===Chinese===

新的一天来了，六点差一点，我们乘上半落的潮水，顺水道穿行而下，出了海。
照理我本该在波特马多克停一停，重温童年的记忆。可让童年的记忆见鬼去吧，在这清晨六点：五月的早晨六点，一缕小小的引导风，又冷又欢畅！童年的记忆与对神圣的观想是留给黄昏的；它们与烛光相配，与一种我认识的酒相配，与二十年交情的朋友相配。可是，造我的主在上，每当早晨的风正好吹向大海、我又从酣睡中初醒，我心里便只有即将开始的航程，只有对天际海线的渴望，只有对抵达更远海岸的渴盼。
乘落潮漂下去而没有本地滑头带路，本该比乘涨潮上来更危险。但运气照应，那令人振奋的光线下，落潮的漩流清清楚楚标出了水道，新的一天的光辉沿着陆地、从东面那些庄严大山的山顶上射过来。
就这样，我们没出事故，到了最后一座标志浮标，出了海；风几乎从船尾吹来，略偏左舷尾，一切顺遂。
哈勒赫前面那片广大的滩野在陆上一侧铺展；在它壮丽而倔强的高处，古堡注视着我们经过；当年真正的金雀花王为可怜的圣亨利的血步步紧逼时，人们就把他藏在那里。
这是一片处处栖居着非世间之物的风景；栖居着被我们的上帝逐出这些山丘的诸神；大概也栖居着逝去的伟人的记忆与他们的幽魂；栖居着沿内陆道路开进的帝国军团、诸位首领，以及一千五百年前对吕奥涅斯群岛的岛屿与礁石施下法咒的那些极圣洁的人。
我们就这样一程又一程地驶下去，朝威尔士的大西洋南角偏航。这时，远在海面中央，我们听见了钟声。……
那钟声不仅哀伤（同时又可畏），还有一种奇特的传信的意味；仿佛它不属于我们航行其间的这个世界。

===Original===

For a bell is a land thing, and even in salt water we think of it as sounding upon a buoy near shore, or as the companion and regulator of human things, like the bell on board ship. For Mr. Masefield is very just when he notes how the clang of the ship’s bell by accident in the swoop of a ship down the great waves of an Atlantic storm, has an unnatural call: being rung without the aid of men’s hands.
Now this bell, as I heard it plainting away deeply, unable as yet to see the thing on which it swung, mixed up its noise in my mind with the great story of Saint Patrick: a story I had designed to write many years ago in Arras, where I went to see the manuscript which most vividly recalls him. But of that task I found myself incapable. However, I did visit his staff in Rome.
For the bell thus swinging on its buoy far out to sea swings on the western edge of that strange, long undersea road which they call the “Sarn Badrig” (which means “The Causeway of Saint Patrick”) and cannot but awake the great story of the coming of Europe to Ireland.
How right they were to call that sunken ridge pointing straight to Ireland “The Roman Road of Patrick”! And how its sudden cessation symbolises the break, the wound, the rupture, by which this island was cut off from that: more than two hundred years ago! The ridge ends suddenly in deep water and continues no more. But the bell tolls on, appealing.
Saint Patrick is the second of those witnesses which remain to us of the more than natural quality of Ireland. The first is the old pagan story of the western harbours from which the dead were ferried over by night to their felicity. The second is that undying phrase in the “Confessio,” where Saint Patrick himself says that at night and in dreams “he heard the voices of the Irish calling.”
It is gloom upon the mind to remember that the magnitude of the task set to Britain (a Roman land) in the matter of Holy Ireland, has been so missed: as though a man, having to encounter a mountain a little before the day thought it no more than a slight hill against the dawn. Here was the difficult land﻿—but inspired﻿—which Agricola said (and he knew his business) he could have mastered for Rome and right order with one legion. That legion was not given him, any more than the divisions were given that might have made the break through before Cambrai.

===Chinese===

钟是陆地之物，即便在咸水里，我们也只当它响在岸边的浮标上，或是人事的伴侣与调节者，如船上的钟。梅斯菲尔德先生说得很对：大西洋风暴的巨浪间，船俯冲而下时船钟偶然的一声锵响，有一种非自然的召唤：因为那是不借人手而自鸣的。
此刻这口钟在远处深沉地哀鸣，我还看不见它悬着的那件东西，它的声响便在我心里与圣帕特里克的大故事搅在一起：那故事我多年前在阿拉斯就打算写，我去那里看过最能鲜活追想他的那份抄本。可那桩任务我发觉自己担当不起。不过，我倒真在罗马拜访过他的手杖。
因为这口钟在远海浮标上这样摇摆，正是悬在那条他们称为“萨恩·巴德里克”（Sarn Badrig，意为“圣帕特里克石堤”）的奇怪而漫长的水下长路的西端上摇摆，不可能不唤醒“欧洲来到爱尔兰”那个大故事。
把那条直指爱尔兰的水下石脊称作“帕特里克的罗马大道”，叫得多么对啊！而它突然终止之处，又多么象征那次断裂、那道伤口、那道裂口——这座岛由此与那座岛隔绝：两百多年前！石脊在深水中突然终结，不再延续。可钟声一直敲着，恳求着。
圣帕特里克是留存给我们的、见证爱尔兰超自然品质的第二件证物。第一件，是那些西部港口的古异教故事——死者夜里从那里被渡往他们的极乐。第二件，是《忏悔录》里那句不朽的话，圣帕特里克亲口说，夜里在梦中“他听见爱尔兰人的呼声”。
想到不列颠（一片罗马的土地）在神圣爱尔兰一事上所领任务的分量竟被如此错过，心里便觉阴沉：仿佛一个人天亮前要翻一座山，却当它不过是晨光里的一座小丘。这里便是那片艰难的——却受神启的——土地，阿格里科拉说（他是懂行的），用一个军团他就能替罗马、替正当的秩序把它拿下。那个军团没有拨给他，正如后来本可以在康布雷之前实现突破的那些师没有拨下去一样。

===Original===

Would that the officials at Rome had listened to him! Here was Ireland that most difficult of subjects, most intriguing of friends, most tenacious of enemies, set up against the western shores of Britain, of Roman land; and through all those centuries nothing was done to enrich the subject, to acquire the friend, to reconcile the enemy. But I am weak enough to believe that this failure also was in the Providence of God.
The mountains look upon the mountains: the mountains of Britain to the mountains of Ireland; from the east to the west, over a magic sea. But they look in wrath and remain sullen giants. Nor is the battle concluded.
As we sail past the bell very slowly (that Mark having come into sight) we discovered it to be enclosed in a sort of great cage which swung with slow dignity and bowed backwards and forwards upon the large swell, unaffected by the light breeze which gave us our two knots through sluggish water. My companion said to me, brutally enough, “How would you like to be shut up in that cage, and left there to the night and to the wind?” I answered, “Not at all,” and in this I told the truth. But the bell did not repine, for it was fulfilling its function, expiating its sins, pursuing its end, and consonant to the will that is behind this world. It tolls there beyond the seaward end of the Sarn Badrig to warn mariners of that long ledge submerged: barring the sea ways.
My mind returned to that great Causeway, that shelf of rock and shingle behind us, just hidden by the waves, pointing westward into the deep, mile after mile, like some drowned giant’s pier: the beginning of a bridge, as it were, between Britain and the Other Island; the marriage of Britain with the older island, whence the dead are ferried over to their repose.
But that Causeway was never completed: that bridge was never built; and the weight, the future consequence, of such a failure lies not upon Ireland, but (alas!) upon England.
For how many years did I not see the tragedy played out!
I knew its roots as well as any man. I knew that the gulf was a gulf of religion. I knew that the riddle was without solution for men whose eyes were cast downwards; who did not admit the unseen powers; who could see nothing but the mud and wealth in which they stood﻿—yet I marvelled that the dreadful affair should drag on so long.

===Chinese===

罗马的官员们当年听了他的话该多好！爱尔兰，这最难缠的臣属、最曲折的朋友、最执着的仇敌，就顶着不列颠——罗马的土地——的西岸；多少个世纪过去，没有人去充实这份臣属、赢取这位朋友、和解这位仇敌。可我软弱地相信，这一次失败也在上帝的眷顾之中。
群山望着群山：不列颠的群山望爱尔兰的群山；自东向西，隔着一片魔法之海。可它们含怒相望，做着郁郁的巨人。战事也未了结。
我们缓缓驶过那口钟（那座马克浮标已经进入视野），发现它罩在一只巨大的笼子里，庄严而缓慢地摇着，在长涌上前俯后仰，全不理会那给我们两节速度、推着我们驶过迟滞水面的微风。我的同伴毫不客气地对我说：“把你关进那笼子里，留给黑夜和风，你觉得如何？”我答：“一点也不愿意。”我说的是实话。可那口钟并无怨言，因为它在履行它的职能，赎它的罪，追求它的目的，与这个世界背后的意志相合。它在萨恩·巴德里克海端的尽头外敲着，警告水手那条沉没的长脊：封住海路。
我的心又回到那条大石堤，回到我们身后那条刚刚没入波涛的岩石砾石脊，一英里接一英里地直指西方深处，像某个溺死巨人的码头：仿佛是不列颠与另一座岛之间一座桥的开端；是不列颠与那座更古老的岛——死者正是从那里被渡往安息——的联姻。
可那道石堤从未筑成：那座桥从未架起；这一失败的分量与后患，不落在爱尔兰头上，而（唉！）落在英格兰头上。
这出悲剧我看了多少年啊！
我像任何人一样了解它的根子。我知道那道鸿沟是宗教的鸿沟。我知道，对那些眼睛只朝下看的人、不承认不可见之力的人、只看得见自己陷在其中的烂泥与财富的人，这个谜无解——可这可怕的事情竟能拖这么久，我仍觉惊异。

===Original===

To the one set, to those sham “Liberals” and “Unionists” of the wretched money game at Westminster, it was a lucrative jest. They cared not for Ireland at all, and not too much for England. They treated the martyrdom of Ireland as a counter in their play. To do them justice, they thought that England could never suffer. To the others, to those of Ireland, the struggle was (as they thought) a battle always lost.
In the beginning of their effort, the men who had organised a political caucus for Ireland and had attempted, by what they called the “Nationalist” Party, to get some settlement by way of the Parliamentary morass, were sincere; yes, at first, flamingly sincere. Parnell I never met; but Davitt I knew﻿—and a man more heroically sincere I have never had the fortune to meet and respect. To the very end the effort of the Irishmen at Westminster retained some dwindling element of truth. And this I say though I know that many Irishmen today may laugh at me for saying it. But it is true. Down to the very end, sunk as they had grown in the sodden Westminster habit, and conventional as had become the repetition of their demand, some conception of a goal to be reached at last still inhabited these men. I knew them and I can testify to it. Right up to 1914﻿—it sounds laughable, but it is true﻿—the group at Westminster which called itself “The Irish Party” did, in some fading way, still hold the idea of a “Parliamentary settlement.” It was true of John Redmond and his brother. I may fairly say that his brother died for that idea.
They really did think (to me it was astonishing; to the younger Irish, to many of them at least, incredible) that a solution was to be reached through the working of those then undermined, moth-eaten, now crumbled falsehoods of the professional politicians: that the farce of voting is “the voice of the people”; that the House of Commons is “a mirror of England”; that “the nation rules through its representatives”﻿—and all the rest of the absurdities bred by the grafting of French democratic attempts on to the decline of English aristocracy.
It sounds not possible that *any* Irishman, even after a lifetime at Westminster, could be so deceived. But, I say it again, I knew these men. I can testify to the truth of what I have said. They did right up to 1914 still faintly hope. I knew them as friends and as opponents.

===Chinese===

对一边的人，对威斯敏斯特那场卑劣金钱游戏里的假“自由派”和“统一派”，这是一桩有利可图的玩笑。他们根本不关心爱尔兰，对英格兰也不太关心。他们把爱尔兰的殉难当作他们赌局里的筹码。平心而论，他们以为英格兰永远不会吃亏。对另一边的人，对爱尔兰人，这场斗争（在他们看来）是一场永远打输的战斗。
在他们的努力之初，那些为爱尔兰组织政治核心会议、试图通过他们所谓的“民族党”、从议会泥沼里讨某种解决的人，是真诚的；是的，起初是烈火般的真诚。帕内尔我从未见过；但戴维特我认识——再没有比他更英勇真诚的人，我有幸遇见并敬重。直到最后，爱尔兰人在威斯敏斯特的努力都还存留着一点萎缩的真诚。我这样说，尽管我知道今天许多爱尔兰人会笑话我。可这是真的。直到最后，尽管他们已经深陷威斯敏斯特那种烂醉的习惯，尽管他们的要求已经沦为照本宣科，某种终将达到目标的构想仍栖居在这些人心里。我认识他们，我可以作证。直到1914年——听着可笑，但是真的——威斯敏斯特那个自称“爱尔兰党”的集团，仍以某种日薄西山的方式，抱着“议会解决”的想法。约翰·雷德蒙和他的兄弟便是如此。我可以说，他的兄弟是为那个想法死的。
他们确实以为（我觉得惊人；对年轻的爱尔兰人，至少对其中许多人，难以置信）——解决可以通过那些职业政客的、当时已被蛀空、虫蚀、如今已然崩塌的谎言的运转来达成：投票的闹剧是“人民的声音”；下议院是“英格兰的镜子”；“国家通过它的代表们统治”——以及法兰西民主的尝试嫁接在英格兰贵族制衰落之上所孳生的全部荒唐。
听上去不可能有哪个爱尔兰人，哪怕在威斯敏斯特待上一辈子，会被骗成这样。可我再说一遍，我认识这些人。我可以为我所说的作证。直到1914年他们还存着微弱的希望。我作为朋友、也作为对手认识他们。

===Original===

They were my opponents when I took my share in defending the Catholic schools in 1906, and they were angry at my going to see the Pope in that year, to lay before him what I thought to be sufficient amendments to Mr. Birrell’s Bill (for they regarded the Catholic schools in England as their private domain). What is enormous, these men, Redmond and his party, thought that their Westminster method could still achieve something after the manifest betrayal of Ireland by the politicians labelled “Liberal” upon the outbreak of the Great War.
It is a process I have watched over and over again in the course of my short but too long life; men not seeing what (one would imagine) should be apparent to a child.
These men did not see that you cannot get strength out of rottenness; nor justice, nor truth, nor generosity out of moribund institutions (such as is now the House of Commons), which, after a certain stage in decline, no longer live save for personal intrigue.
I say nothing, therefore, against them, in spite of their failure, and I revere their memory. The Irish in good time will give to the last protagonists in that futile attempt a due position in their annals; and specially to William Redmond and his brother, John Redmond.
For writing this I may be assailed from either side; but it is true.
Anyhow, I saw the tragedy going on before my eyes, as a man on a shore height may see a boat making straight for rocks under water, apparent to him, but of which her steersman knows nothing. I saw it in the years when I watched it as a young man from the gallery of the House of Commons; I saw it during the two Parliaments in which I sat; I saw it when I had left the place in disgust. I saw it in the last four years before the Great War; England, still drifting hopelessly on to the Irish reef: till she struck.
I saw, year after year, from my boyhood to my middle manhood and later, the Parliamentary poison eating up the Irish effort and the English face turned away from Ireland, failing to understand its own doom. For England, a seat of vision, might have understood the opportunity and the peril had the Irish themselves used pen and word directly. But the wrong instrument was used: the broken and now useless instrument of the Commons.

===Chinese===

1906年我参与保卫公教学校时，他们是我的对手；那年我去觐见教皇，向他呈交我认为对比勒尔法案足够的修正意见，他们对此很恼火（因为他们把英格兰的公教学校视为他们的私产）。了不得的是，雷德蒙和他的党，在大战爆发、“自由派”标签的政客们对爱尔兰的背叛昭然若揭之后，竟然还以为他们的威斯敏斯特方法能有所成就。
这过程我在我短暂又漫长的生涯里一遍又一遍看着：人们看不见（你会以为）连孩子都该明了的事。
这些人看不见：腐烂里榨不出力量；垂死的机构（如今的下议院即是）里榨不出正义、真理与慷慨——那类机构过了衰落的某个阶段，就只为私人倾轧而活了。
因此，尽管他们失败了，我不说他们一句坏话，而且我敬重他们的记忆。爱尔兰人会在适当的时候，在那场徒劳尝试的最后几位主角的历史里给他们一个应有的位置；尤其是威廉·雷德蒙和他的兄弟约翰·雷德蒙。
为写这些话，我可能两面挨骂；可它是真的。
总之，我眼看着悲剧在眼前上演，像岸上高处的一个人，看着一条船直朝水下暗礁驶去——他看得见，舵手却一无所知。我青年时代从下议院的旁听廊里看着它；我在坐过两届议会的那些年里看着它；我厌恶地离开那个地方之后还看着它。大战前的最后四年我看着它：英格兰无可救药地漂向爱尔兰的礁石：直到撞上。
从少年到盛年再到以后，我年复一年看着议会的毒药吞噬爱尔兰的努力，看着英格兰的脸从爱尔兰转开，不懂得自己的劫数。因为英格兰，这个有异象禀赋的国度，倘若爱尔兰人直接用笔和言语，本可以懂得那机会与那危险。可他们用错了工具：用了下议院这件破碎而如今全然无用的工具。

===Original===

I saw the politicians whom the Irish sent over, or rather, who had the machinery of the Irish protest in their hands, grow fonder and fonder of the Westminster drug and degradation. I saw them half in doubt, at last, whether it were not better to remain in London half enjoying what had become second nature to them, the wretched pomposities of the House of Commons. They should rather have nourished the flame of their country. Yet they were honest men, and at heart and at root were Irish to the end. But they thought that England was to be made to understand by the use of a machine, the Parliamentary machine, which was already worn out. It was their fundamental error. What man desiring to move men would waste his time in Parliament?
If a man may boast of a small personal experience, I myself told them what would come. But they would not believe me. They thought me fanatical in this﻿—and to the end. They put their trust in the slime of the lobbies. It swallowed them up.
I saw the end; or rather the beginning of the end﻿—for the true end of the Irish quarrel is still hidden, and will be perhaps for us, here in England, very terrible. I saw the professional politicians carrying their silly labels of “Liberal” and “Unionist,” footling, on the very edge of the war precipice in 1914, at a game for avoiding the Irish issue, and (as they dined together) arranging tricks to put it off on one side. I saw their vast relief when they were given their chance to the threat of a mutiny against the salvation of England and Ireland. And I saw﻿—I lived to see﻿—the contemptible capitulation: the Parliamentary puppets (Lord Chancellors and the rest) shouting on a Wednesday that “they would not rest until they had taken the last arm from the hands of the last rebel,” and on the following Friday, at the bidding of their financial masters, surrendering to Ireland forever: Ireland that might have been the bulwark of England.﻿ ﻿… It is done.

---

===Chinese===

我看着爱尔兰派来的政客——或者说，把爱尔兰抗议的机器握在手里的人——对威斯敏斯特这剂毒品和这场堕落爱得一天深过一天。我看着他们临了竟半信半疑：赖在伦敦、半享受着已成为他们第二天性的下议院的可怜排场，岂不更好。他们本该去养育他们国家的火焰。他们是诚实的人，心底和根子上到死都是爱尔兰人。可他们以为，靠一台机器——议会机器，一台早已磨损的机器——就能让英格兰明白。那是他们根本的错误。想打动人的人，谁会把时间浪费在议会里？
若容我夸一句小小的亲历：我自己就对他们说过将来会怎样。可他们不信。他们觉得我在此事上狂热——直到最后。他们把信心放在走廊的泥浆里。泥浆把他们吞了。
我看到了结局；或者说结局的开端——因为爱尔兰这场争端的真正结局还藏着，而且对我们、对英格兰这里的我们，或许会非常可怕。我看着职业政客们顶着“自由派”“统一派”那些愚蠢的标签，在1914年战争悬崖的边上瞎折腾，玩着回避爱尔兰问题的游戏，还（一边同桌吃饭）安排着把它甩到一边的把戏。我看着他们得到机会时如释重负——那机会就是对拯救英格兰与爱尔兰之举施以兵变的威胁。我还看到——我活到了看到——那可鄙的投降：议会的傀儡们（大法官之流）星期三还喊着“不缴获最后一个叛乱者手里的最后一件武器决不罢休”，到了星期五，一听他们金融主子的号令，就永远地向爱尔兰投降了：那本可以是不列颠屏障的爱尔兰。……木已成舟。

---

===Original===

So we sailed slowly on, past the end of Saint Patrick’s Causeway, past that mournful great tolling bell which hangs there in the middle sea; imprisoned in its cage, expiating the too careless sins of its youth. The lamentable sound of it, the unsatisfied appeal, followed us over the deep. So we sailed on, with our nose for the Atlantic headlands of the south, and for the corners of Wales, where they face the Cornish and the Devon seas and Ireland also, beyond, with the great ocean rolling in between.

---

===Chinese===

我们缓缓驶过圣帕特里克石堤的尽头，驶过那口悬在大海中央的哀伤大钟；它囚在笼中，为它年少时太不知谨慎的罪赎着。它那可悲的声音，那未获应答的恳求，隔着深海一路随我们。我们就这样驶下去，船头对着南方的大西洋海岬，对着威尔士的那些拐角——在那里，它们面对康沃尔海、德文海，还有更远处的爱尔兰，大洋在中间滚滚而过。

---

===Original===

High as are the mountains on this shore, yet by a trick of the air they were soon out of sight.
That is a thing I have never understood. High land which ought to be plainly visible, and even overhanging one, disappears like this at sea magically on a clear day. When the weather is thick, one expects that; but the curious thing is that one may see clear air all around and a sharp horizon, nothing but sky and sea, and yet know perfectly well that the empty heaven at which one is gazing is really full of great hills close at hand. I do not know whether this happens in the clearer parts of the world. I have never seen it off North Africa or California. But upon the coasts of these islands it happens continually, and it is one of the hundred things which, I think, has made their inhabitants familiar with mystery and amenable to the unseen. For those of these islands, British and Irish, have always been remarkable for the sense of mystery; and among them none feel the effect of mystery more strongly than such as sail upon the sea.
It was an astonishing thing to sit there, at the end of the afternoon, just past the end of Saint Patrick’s Causeway, heading for Strumbles light below the horizon, with a slight air, now from the northwest, giving the boat steering-way and no more, with the tiller lazily pushed a little up to keep her course, and looking over the rail to the left, eastward, and seeing nothing there but an horizon of empty sky; yet knowing very well that the sky was not empty, but that the enormous mass of Cader Idris and the enormous mass of Plinlimmon stood guarding the sea. It was like the continual experience of this life wherein the wise firmly admit vast Presences to stand in what is an apparent emptiness, unperceived by any sense.
As I thus looked over the rail musing upon the strangeness of such things, I remembered how, many years ago, in this same boat and holding this same tiller, I had wondered, after a too rapid passage across the Channel from France, under too much canvas and with too much wind, how near I might be to England. For one could not see the coast at all. The wind had dropped suddenly, it had grown dead calm, and the *Nona* lolloped on the big lump which the breeze had left. I had no idea, within ten miles, where I might be; it is a ridiculous confession to make, but true.

===Chinese===

这岸上的山尽管高，却被空气的一个把戏，很快弄出了视野。
这事我从来不能明白。本该清楚可见、甚至悬在头顶的高地，会在晴朗的日子里这样在海上魔术般地消失。天气恶劣时倒也罢了；奇怪的是，你可以看到四周空气澄澈，地平线锋利，只有天与海，却清楚地知道，自己凝望的那片空空的天上，其实满是近在咫尺的大山。我不知道这在世界上空气更清澈的地方会不会发生。北非外海、加利福尼亚外海我从未见过。可在这些岛屿的海岸上，它时时发生；我想，这正是使岛民们熟悉神秘、驯服于不可见之物的百样事情之一。因为这些岛——不列颠与爱尔兰——的居民，向来以神秘感著称；而其中，没有谁比航行海上者更强烈地感到神秘的效力。
傍晚将尽时坐在那里，刚过圣帕特里克石堤的尽头，朝地平线下的斯特朗布尔斯灯塔驶去，一缕来自西北的微风刚好给船以舵效、不多一分，懒懒地把舵柄向上推一点点以稳住航向，越过左舷栏杆向东望去——那里除了空荡天空的地平线什么也没有；却清楚地知道那天空并不空，而是卡德·伊德里斯的庞大山体与普林利蒙的庞大山体站在那里守卫着海。这实在是一桩奇事。这就像此生不断的经验：智者坚定地承认，巨大的临在就立在显然的空无之中，任何感官都察觉不到。
我这样倚着栏杆、思量着这类事情的奇异时，记起许多年前，就在这条船上、握着这同一副舵柄，在从法国过海峡的一次太快的风横渡之后——帆太多，风太大——我曾纳闷自己离英格兰可能有多近。因为海岸根本看不见。风陡然落了，死一般平静，诺娜号在微风吹剩的大涌上颠晃。十英里之内我身处何处，毫无概念；这坦白说来可笑，但是真的。

===Original===

It was the height of the spring tides, and I only roughly knew the set of them in mid-Channel. While I was so wondering, I thought I saw some difference between the lower and the upper sky, as though the lower grey were faintly darker than the upper. In a moment the line was defined, and I found myself looking at the turn of the high land east of Folkestone, where it runs away inland towards the old Roman harbour of Lympne. I was not more than three miles from shore.
And as there is a concealment of reality at sea, corresponding to the concealment of reality from our experience in human life, so also at sea there is occasionally vision, corresponding to that occasional vision which you also have in human life: but vision is much rarer than the concealment of reality.
Once out near the Looe with an ugly thunderstorm glowering and lumbering down from the north, full of zigzags of lightning, I, in this same boat, was shortening sail rapidly to meet the gust when it should come. Even as I did so, I saw a fierce red light beating upon the Seven Sisters and Beachy Head, more than forty miles away. You could see every detail of those chalk cliffs. You could have touched them with your hand; yet the sea in between was vague: and that was a vision of the unseen. I say that the sea is in all things the teacher of men.

===Chinese===

那正是大潮的顶点，我对海峡中央的流向也只大致知道。正纳闷间，我觉得看见下层天与上层天之间有些差别，仿佛下层的灰色比上层略暗。片刻之间那条线清晰了，我发现自己望见的，是福克斯通以东高地的转折处，高地从那里折向内陆，朝利姆尼的古罗马港跑去。我离岸不过三英里。
海上既有对真实的遮蔽，对应着人生经验中对真实的遮蔽，海上也就间或有异象，对应着人生中你也偶得的异象：但异象远比遮蔽稀有。
有一次在卢附近的海面，一场狰狞的雷雨从北方滚滚压下，满是锯齿形的闪电，我就在这条船上，赶在阵风来临前急速收帆。正收着，我看见一道凶猛的红光打在四十多英里外的七姐妹崖和比奇角上。那些白垩悬崖的每一处细节都看得见，仿佛伸手可触；其间的海反而模糊：那便是一次对未见之物的看见。我说，海在一切事务上是人的教师。

===Original===

With the afternoon the wind freshened, and, as it freshened, went right round by north to a little east of north, whence it blew steadily enough, and gave us about four knots at the fall of darkness.
My companion had never held a tiller, but he was very expert at all sports, and I thought to myself, “I will see whether so simple a thing as steering a boat cannot be easily accomplished by a man at the first trial. Then shall I be able to get what I badly need, which is a little sleep.” So I lighted the binnacle lamp, I explained to him the function of the lubber’s mark, and gave him the point on the card which he was to keep on the lubber’s mark. I said to him: “If it comes on to blow a little harder and the card swings, and the boat tends to yaw a little, don’t mind that, but keep the lubber’s mark on the average at the point I have given and that will be enough.” He said that he understood all these things, and for the first time in his life set himself to steer a ship. But I, for my part, went down to sleep, confident that if it should come on to blow at all hard it would awaken me there and then, so no great harm could come. I slept for many hours, when suddenly I was awakened by my companion giving a loud cry of astonishment. I tumbled up on deck quickly, and I found him pointing at a light which shone brilliantly upon the horizon, dead on our bow. He said to me: “Look, look, there is a light dead ahead!” I said to him: “Of course!” and that it was the light of Strumble Head, outside Fishguard; and I asked him what he would have expected. I had given him his course, and, naturally, he had lifted the light in good time. But he, for his part, could not get over it; he thought it a sort of miracle. He kept on repeating his amazement that so clumsy a thing as a tiller and a rudder, and so coarse an instrument as an old battered binnacle compass, should thread the eye of a needle like that; it was out of all his experience. It is true that he had not been disturbed by any current or strong tide, but even had he been so, he was bound on a clear night to make that light not much off either bow.
That things should turn out so gave him quite a new conception of the sea and the sailing of it, and he talked henceforward as though it were his home.

===Chinese===

入了下午，风渐渐增强，而风一起，便径直转北，转到略偏东的北向来，稳稳地吹着，暮色四合时给了我们大约四节的船速。

我的同伴从未掌过舵柄，但他各项运动都很在行，我心里盘算：“我倒要看看，掌舵这么简单的一件事，一个人头一回上手是不是就能轻松学会。那样我就能得到我急需的东西——睡上一觉。”于是我点亮罗盘柜的灯，向他讲明罗经基线是做什么用的，又告诉他在罗经卡上该把哪个点对住基线。我对他说：“要是风再紧一些，罗经卡晃动，船有点偏荡，不必理会，只要让基线平均停在我给你的那个点上，那就够了。”他说这些都明白了，于是平生第一次执舵行船。而我呢，下楼睡觉去了，满有把握：风若真的大起来，当场就会把我震醒，出不了大乱子。我一睡许多个钟头，忽然被同伴一声惊呼唤醒。我翻身冲上甲板，见他正指着一盏灯，那灯在地平线上灿然亮着，正在我们船头正前方。他对我说：“看哪，看哪，正前方有灯！”我对他说：“可不是嘛！”那是菲什加德外斯特朗布尔角的灯；我问他，不然他还指望什么。航向我早给了他，他自然及时把那盏灯迎了上来。可他这边却怎么也缓不过这个劲；他当这是一桩奇迹。他反反复复地惊叹：舵柄和舵这样笨拙的东西，加上一只饱经风霜的旧罗盘这样粗陋的器械，竟能这样穿针眼；这完全出乎他全部的经验。不错，他没受水流或强潮的搅扰；但即便受了，在晴夜里他也必定仍会把那盏灯迎在船头左右不远的地方。

事情竟有这样的结局，使他对海、对航海有了一个全新的概念，从此谈起来，仿佛海上就是他的家。

===Original===

This corroboration by experience of a truth emphatically told, but at first not believed, has a powerful effect upon the mind.
I suppose that of all the instruments of conviction it is the most powerful. It is an example of the fundamental doctrine that truth confirms truth. If you say to a man a thing which *he* thinks nonsensical, impossible, a mere jingle of words, although you yourself know it very well by experience to be true; when later he finds this thing by his own experience to be actual and living, then is truth confirmed in his mind: it stands out much more strongly than it would had he never doubted. On this account, it is always worth while, I think, to hammer at truths which one knows to be important, even those which seem, to others, at their first statement mere nonsense. For though you may die under the imputation of being a man without a sense of proportion, or even a madman, yet reality will in time confirm your effort. And even though that confirmation of your effort, the triumph of the truth, should never be associated with your own name, yet it is worth making, for the sake of the truth, to which I am sure we owe a sort of allegiance: not because it is the truth﻿—one can have no allegiance to an abstraction﻿—but because whenever we insist upon a truth we are witnessing to Almighty God.
A man who knows that the earth is round but lives among men who believe it to be flat ought to hammer in his doctrine of the earth’s roundness up to the point of arrest, imprisonment, or even death. Reality will confirm him, and he is not so much testifying to the world as it is﻿—which is worth nothing﻿—as to Him who made the world, and Who is worth more than all things. And, as it seems to me, a man ought to do this even about the truths not so very important, but he should observe some proportion between them and truths of vital importance. For instance, it is a truth of no very apparent immediate importance today that any great poem of the past must have been written by a poet, and that those who think a great poem can be written by a committee or pieced together out of traditional ballads, think nonsense. It is also a truth that Great Britain is no longer secured by sea power. It is also a truth that the interests of this country were till lately identical with the interests of international banking and finance﻿—and are so no longer.

===Chinese===

一条真理被人斩钉截铁地说出而不被相信，日后竟由亲历加以印证——这对心灵有一种强大的效力。

我想，在一切说服人的利器之中，这要算最有力的一件。它是“真理印证真理”这条根本原理的一个例证。你若对一个人说一件事，他认为纯属胡话、绝无可能、不过是字面的聒噪，而你明明凭经验知道它是真的；等到后来他凭自己的经验发现这件事当真实在、当真活生生，真理便在他心里牢牢确立：比他从未怀疑过时突出得多。为此，我认为，凡是自己知道要紧的真理，哪怕在别人初听来纯属梦呓，也总值得反复敲打。因为哪怕你顶着“不知轻重”乃至“疯子”的骂名死去，现实终有一天会证实你的努力。纵然那证实、那真理的凯旋，永远不会同你的名字连在一起，这努力也值得做——为了真理的缘故；我确信我们对真理负有一种效忠的义务：不是因为它是真理——对抽象物谈不上效忠——而是因为，每当我们坚持一条真理，我们就是在为全能的天主作证。

一个人明知大地是圆的，却生活在相信大地是平的人们中间，他就该把大地是圆的这一条敲进人心，敲到被拘捕、被下狱、乃至丢了性命的地步。现实会为他作证；而他见证的对象与其说是这个世界——那不值什么——不如说是造出这个世界的那一位，祂比万事万物都贵重。而且在我看来，人连那些不怎么要紧的真理也该这样坚持，只是须在它们与性命攸关的真理之间保持一个分寸。譬如，“往昔的伟大诗篇必定出自诗人之手，以为大诗可以由一个委员会写出来、或由传统歌谣拼缀而成的人，都是在想入非非”——这在今天是一条看不出有什么直接要紧的真理。又如，“大不列颠的安全已不再由海权保障”，这也是一条真理。再如，“这个国家的利益不久前还与国际银行和金融的利益相一致——如今不再如此了”，这同样是一条真理。

===Original===

All these three truths are true, but the first truth touches no vital matter, while the last two truths are of immediate importance; and a man aware of them should hammer away at them, and not neglect them in order to pursue too constantly that other much less important truth about old poems.
It is not often that one comes across men in a modern lifetime intent upon the proclamation of unaccepted, or of ill-accepted or unknown truths; for such men must suffer. And few will suffer today save for some reward. Very few modern men value any reward that is not immediate and material. Huxley, who sacrificed himself entirely, both to false things which he believed to be true and to real things that were true, I never knew. But I knew Déroulède well, and Déroulède hammered away all his life at the expense of ceaseless insult and contempt, paying for the preservation of his honour the heavy price of an unbroken isolation, and dying without seeing any apparent fruit of his effort. It was his mission to proclaim to his French compatriots the elementary truth that, until they had secured the defeat of Prussia in a war, they themselves were doomed to increasing decay, and Europe to increasing ills. I visited his grave just after the Battle of the Marne. It is in a little, somewhat neglected churchyard, some three miles from the house he had inherited: a place windswept and overlooking from beyond its walls a great horizon to the north. The season being late summer, and the war having left the little place untended, the grass was rank and high around the mound and the slab which marked the place where he lay. There, leaning against the wall, someone had put casually, as though by chance, in high dedication to his memory, the first of the frontier posts which the army had pulled up from the summits of the Vosges during the first ill-fated charge into Alsace.
I knew also one other man (whom, for the honour I bear him and because he is still alive, I shall not name), an Englishman; at the outbreak of war in South Africa he said to all who would hear him, and printed in a journal to which he had admission, the simple truth that this adventure would be the beginning of a decline in the financial credit of England. To discover whether he was right or wrong, look at any curve of that credit and mark the dates.

===Chinese===

这三条真理都是真的；但第一条不关痛痒，后两条却刻不容缓；知道这两条的人就该不住地敲打它们，而不该为了那条关于古诗的、无关宏旨得多的真理，把它们荒疏了。

在现代人的一生里，立志宣说那些不被接受、不被善纳或不为人知的真理的人，并不常见；因为这样的人必定受苦。而今天肯受苦的人，若没有报酬，寥寥无几。现代人对不属于即时的、物质的报酬，看重的没有几个。赫胥黎把自己整个儿牺牲给了他信以为真的谬误、也牺牲给了真正为真的东西——我没有赶上认识他。但德鲁莱德我是深知的人。德鲁莱德敲打了一辈子，受尽不停的羞辱与轻蔑，为保全自己的荣誉，付出了一世孤立无援的重价，到死也没看见自己努力结出什么明显的果实。他的使命，是向他的法国同胞宣说一条初等的真理：不先在一场战争中击败普鲁士，他们自己就注定日益衰败，欧洲就注定祸乱日深。马恩河战役刚打完，我去扫过他的墓。那是在一座小小的、有些荒疏的教堂墓园里，离他继承的宅子约三英里；一个风声猎猎的地方，墙外俯瞰着朝北的一大片天际。时值夏末，战事又使这小地方无人照管，坟头的草长得又深又密，盖着他长眠处的石板。就在那里，靠着墙根，有人随手放着——仿佛是碰巧放在那里，实则是献给他英灵的崇高祭奠——军队在第一场不幸的阿尔萨斯冲锋中、从孚日山巅拔下来的第一根界碑。

我还认识另一个人（出于我对他的敬意，也因为他尚在世，我不说出他的名字），一个英国人；南非战事爆发时，他对所有愿意听的人说、并在他有门路投稿的一家刊物上印出了一条简单的真理：这场冒险，将是英格兰金融信用由盛转衰的开端。要验证他的对错，只消找一条那信用的曲线，标上那几个日期。

===Original===

He said to all who would hear him that from this adventure onwards we should find ourselves increasingly embarrassed, with increasing taxation, with an increasing uncertainty in our foreign policy, and in our hold upon the markets we desired to control abroad. His action was not that of the prophet, but of the reasoner. His unpopularity was extreme. He was the only public man who talked conscientiously and rationally in this affair of the Boer War, and who did not confuse his conclusions with that disgusting antipathy to their own country which marked the most of those who protested against the war. As for the enemy, the South African Dutch, he knew them and heartily despised them. The Outlanders he also knew, and thought them the scum of the earth. But he acted on no mood of like or dislike. He was concerned with the future of England and he reasoned. He talked a sort of mathematical sense, and all his words were true; but he shall know no reward. Only, this remains true also; that all who heard him or read his writing have secretly returned to those words in their own minds, and have said: “He was right and we were wrong.”
There is another form of impressing the truth, and testifying to it, and doing good by it, which is the dogmatic assertion of truth by the old and the experienced and the revered, to the young. It is out of fashion; it is invaluable. I can myself testify to two such experiences which stand out supreme among many hundreds in my own early life. I am afraid they may seem trivial to my readers; I can only say that for myself they were as strong experiences as any great joy or pain could be. One was a sentence which Cardinal Manning said to me when I was but twenty years old. The other was one which the Master of my College, Dr. Jowett, of Balliol, said to me when I was twenty-two years old.
The profound thing which Cardinal Manning said to me was this: *All human conflict is ultimately theological.*
It was my custom during my first days in London, as a very young man, before I went to Oxford, to call upon the Cardinal as regularly as he would receive me; and during those brief interviews I heard from him many things which I have had later occasion to test by the experience of human life.

===Chinese===

他对所有愿意听的人说：从这场冒险往后，我们将发现自己日益窘迫，赋税日增，外交政策日益摇摆不定，对想控制的海外市场的把持日益松动。他的行径不是先知的行径，而是说理者的行径。他遭到的厌弃无以复加。在那桩布尔战争的事务里，他是唯一一个凭良心、按理性讲话的公众人物，唯一一个没有把自己的结论同那种对自己祖国的可憎厌恶搅在一起的人——当时抗议战争的人，大多正带着这种厌恶。至于敌人，那些南非的荷兰人，他认识他们，并由衷地鄙视他们。“外邦人”他也认识，视之为人类的渣滓。但他行事不凭好恶。他挂心的是英格兰的前途，他只管推理。他说的是一种数学式的道理，他的话句句属实；但他休想得到任何报偿。只有一点也是真的：凡听过他、读过他文章的人，后来都在心里悄悄回到那些话上，暗自说：“他对了，我们错了。”

还有一种宣示真理、为真理作证、以真理行善的方式，那便是年高、阅历深而受人尊敬的人，向年轻人作出武断的宣示。它已经不时兴了；它却贵重无匹。我自己可以作证，在早年成百上千的经历中，有两桩卓然特立。我怕读者会觉得它们琐屑；我只能说，对我自己而言，它们是同任何大欢乐、大痛苦一样强烈的经历。一桩是曼宁枢机在我年仅二十岁时对我说的一句话。另一桩是我所在学院的院长、巴利奥尔的乔维特博士在我二十二岁时对我说的一句话。

曼宁枢机对我说的那句深奥的话是这样的：人间的一切冲突，归根到底是神学的冲突。

我初到伦敦的头些日子，还是个十分年轻的人，未去牛津之前，习惯按枢机肯接见的频度定期去拜望他；在那些短短的晤谈中，我从他那里听到许多话，日后都得了人间阅历的印证。

===Original===

I was, it may be said, too young to judge things so deep as sanctity and wisdom; but, on the other hand, youth has vision, especially upon elemental things; and Manning did seem to me (and still seems to me) much the greatest Englishman of his time. He was certainly the greatest of all that band, small but immensely significant, who, in the Victorian period, so rose above their fellows, preeminent in will and in intelligence, as not only to perceive, but even to accept the Faith. Not only did his powerful mind discover, but his powerful will also insisted upon all the difficult consequences of such an acceptation. He never admitted the possibility of compromise between Catholic and non-Catholic society. He perceived the necessary conflict, and gloried in it.
This saying of his (which I carried away with me somewhat bewildered) *that all human conflict was ultimately theological*, that is that all wars and revolutions and all decisive struggles between parties of men arise from a difference in moral and transcendental doctrine, was utterly novel to me. To a young man the saying was without meaning: I would have almost said nonsensical, save that I could not attach the idea of folly to Manning. But as I grew older it became a searchlight: with the observation of the world, and with continuous reading of history, it came to possess for me a universal meaning so profound that it reached to the very roots of political action, so extended that it covered the whole.
It is, indeed, a truth which explains and coordinates all one reads of human action in the past, and all one sees of it in the present. Men talk of universal peace: it is only obtainable by one common religion. Men say that all tragedy is the conflict of equal rights. They lie. All tragedy is the conflict of a true right and a false right, or of a greater right and a lesser right, or, at the worst, of two false rights. Still more do men pretend in this time of ours, wherein the habitual use of the human intelligence has sunk to its lowest, that doctrine is but a private, individual affair, creating a mere opinion. Upon the contrary, it is doctrine that drives the state; and every state is stronger in the degree in which the doctrine of its citizens is united.

===Chinese===

或许可以说，我太年轻，评判不了圣德与智慧这样深的东西；可是另一面，青年人自有眼力，尤其在根本的大事上；而曼宁在当时的我看来（如今仍然如此）是他那个时代最伟大的英国人，远迈群伦。维多利亚时代有那么一小群人，人数寥寥而意义至巨，凭意志与才智卓然超拔于侪辈之上，不仅识得信仰，而且接受了信仰；曼宁无疑是这群人中的魁首。他那副强有力的头脑不但发现了真理，他那副强有力的意志还坚持着这接受所带来的一切艰难后果。他从不承认天主教社会与非天主教社会之间有妥协的可能。他看出这场冲突势所必然，且引以为荣。

他这句话——一切人间冲突归根到底是神学的冲突，即一切战争、革命、党派间一切决定性的角力，皆起于道德学说与超性学说之异——我是有些迷惘地带走的，在当时全然新颖。对一个小伙子，这话无从索解：我几乎要说它是胡话，若不是我把“愚蠢”这个念头安不到曼宁头上。但随着年岁增长，它成了一道探照灯：随着对世事的观察、随着对历史不间断的阅读，它对我渐渐具有了一种普遍的意义，深达政治行动的根柢，广覆政治行动的全局。

这确乎是一条真理，它解释并贯通了人们在往昔读到、在当今看到的人类一切行动。人们谈普遍的和平：唯有靠一种共同的宗教才能得到。人们说一切悲剧都是平等权利的冲突。那是撒谎。一切悲剧都是真权利与假权利的冲突，或大权利与小权利的冲突，至多是两个假权利的冲突。人们更装作——在我们这个人类智力的惯常用度跌到最低的时代——信条只是私人的、个人的事务，只造成一种意见。恰恰相反，驱动国家的是信条；国民的信条愈统一，国家愈强。

===Original===

Nor have I met any man in my life, arguing for what should be among men, but took for granted as he argued that the doctrine he consciously or unconsciously accepted was or should be a similar foundation for all mankind. Hence battle.
The truth Dr. Jowett gave me came thus. He asked me the political question which was always uppermost in his mind, and which he believed all young men should consider. It was, “Under what form of government is the state of man at its best?” I answered as all young men should answer, “A republic,” to which he answered gently in his turn, “You cannot have a republic without republicans.” Now that, for terseness and truth and a certain quality of *revelation*, was worthy of Aristotle. It is the full answer, historical and moral, to every honest man who desires, as most honest men do, democracy, and who wonders why it is so hard to attain. But I had never considered that answer; and I think if I had not heard these half-dozen words I might never have considered it.
Democracy﻿—that is, the government of the community by the community: a state wherein a man stands equal with his fellows, and has to suffer neither subservience nor the corruption of flattery and power: a state in which office alone commands, and not the being clothed with office﻿—that is the ideal at the back of every man’s mind who cares for right in public affairs, and who has within himself anything left of private honour. It is simplest put by saying that democracy is the noblest form of government. But the moment you begin to deal with men, you find in varying degree, according to the human material handled, a difficulty in the direction of such an affair. You have experience of the wickedness and folly of men, and if you add to such growing experience the vast experience of history, you will find that, save in some few, and those small, communities, the ideal of democracy must break down in practice; and that so far from enjoying the noblest of social conditions, men attempting democracy in great states are soon suffering the basest forms of control by the rich. That is because most men, though intimately desiring a republic, are not republicans: when you have great numbers, those worthy of democracy are few. In the same way most men, though individually desiring peace within, have not the control of themselves which makes such peace possible.

===Chinese===

我平生也未曾遇见一个人，在为人间应当如何而争论时，不把他自觉或不自觉接受的信条当作、或认为应当当作全人类共同的基石。于是有战争。

乔维特博士给我的真理是这样来的。他问了我那个在他心头永远占第一位、又相信每个青年都该思考的政治问题：“在什么形式的政府之下，人的境况最好？”我照一切青年该答的那样答道：“共和政体。”他于是温和地回了我一句：“没有共和党人，就没有共和政体。”这一句，就其简峭、真实和某种启示的品格而言，配得上亚里士多德。它是对每一个诚实人的完满回答——历史的、道德的回答；这样的人渴望民主（大多数诚实人都渴望），又奇怪它何以如此难求。可我从未想过这个回答；我想，若不曾听见这寥寥几个字，我可能永远不会去想它。

民主——即共同体受共同体的治理：一种人人与其同侪平等而立、既不必忍受屈从、也不必忍受谄媚与权力的腐蚀的状态；一种唯职务本身号令、而非职务之衣冠号令的状态——这是每一个关心公共事务之是非、胸中尚存一分私人荣誉的人心底的理想。说得最简单些：民主是最高贵的政体。可你一旦同人打交道，便发现——依你所打的人的材料高下不等——办这样一桩事业总有难处。你领教过人的邪恶与愚妄；若再往这日渐增长的经验上，添上历史的浩瀚经验，你便会发现：除寥寥几个、而且都是小的共同体外，民主的理想在实践中必至崩坏；在大国里试行民主的人们，非但享受不到最高贵的社会境况，反而很快尝到富人最卑鄙形式的宰制。那是因为大多数人虽然内心渴望共和，却不是共和党人：人数一多，配得上民主的人寥寥。同样，大多数人虽然渴望内心的平安，却没有那份自我节制，而唯有它才使平安可能。

===Original===

So much for the Master’s excellent platitude.
It is strange that things worth saying and hearing, guiding things, should always have that quality of turning into platitudes, once they are familiar; for they were sudden revelations when first they came. To me now the impracticability of democracy among men indifferent to honour and justice is so clear that I never pause to consider it, well knowing that you cannot have the thing in any modern plutocratic state; that even in small states it needs a peculiarly admirable and rare temper in the human material of them. But this conviction came slowly, and all started from those few words.
And what has all this to do with the sailing of the sea? Nothing, save that it is during the sailing of the lonely sea that men most consider the nature of things.

===Chinese===

院长先生这句绝妙的老生常谈，就说到这里。

奇怪的是，凡是值得说、值得听、能指点人的话，一旦家喻户晓，总免不了变成老生常谈；可它们初来的时候，原是豁然的启示。如今，民主之于漠视荣誉与正义的人们之不可行，在我已洞若观火，从不屑停下来想一想——我深知，在任何现代富豪当国的国家里，你得不到民主；即使在小国，也须其人材具有一种格外可钦而罕见的气质。可这一信念来得极慢，而全部的起点，就是那寥寥几个字。

这同航海又有什么相干？毫无相干——只是，唯有在孤独的海上航行时，人才最肯思索万物的本性。

===Original===

The day broke long before we were anywhere near Fishguard breakwater. We must have done, I suppose, ten or twelve miles by the time it was broad light. Strumble Head kept on flashing, white and inept, under the daylight, and then went out. When it ceased there was no sign of land where it had been. Also the wind had fallen sluggish and we were not making three knots. But such as it was, it was steady; and by midmorning we saw all the coast quite plain, and, shortly after, the low black line of the breakwater which they have built out here to make their great artificial harbour of Fishguard.
With the wind still falling we drifted rather than ran for the point of it and crawled in out of the wide sea. Even in that last hour, as we came in, we began to note a long heave gradually sweeping up out of the Atlantic through Saint George’s Channel, as though it had wind behind it; but since it set northeastward it did not affect the calm of the broad roads within the breakwater. We very slowly crawled to that inner southern part where boats of our sort could lie without interfering with the steam monsters, and there, after just twenty-four hours of the passage across Cardigan Bay, a run of seventy-odd miles, we let go the anchor, and, tying up our canvas in a very slovenly fashion, we hailed the shore and got a boat to come out, seeing that I had lost my own dinghy during the tempest in Bardsey Sound.
The man who came out to us in the boat hailed us as he approached in the most beautiful English, either because we were now in that so-called “Little England beyond Wales,” or else because he was indeed an Englishman, exiled to these parts; or perhaps again he was bilingual and had acquired this perfect English as a classical and foreign tongue. It was a privilege and an honour to be rowed ashore by such a man, for he was free of his conversation and all that he said was interesting, true, and well put. He warned us against the people of the place﻿—why I do not know. He told us that where we lay was good holding-ground, and he gave us in that little trip quite a lot of useful information about the difficult passages between the islands and the mainland on the Points of Pembroke which we had to pass on our way southward. He asked us as we landed an astonishingly small payment for his services and then he promised to meet us again at a fixed hour to take us aboard.

===Chinese===

天早亮了很久，我们离菲什加德的防波堤（breakwater）还远得很。我估计，到大天亮时我们大约走了十到十二英里。斯特朗布尔角还在白昼下白白地、无用地闪着，随后熄灭了。它一停，它所在之处便没有一点陆地的影子。风也变得迟滞，我们跑不到三节。但尽管如此，风是稳的；到上午十点来钟，整条海岸已看得清清楚楚，不久又望见他们在这里筑出去、用以造出菲什加德大型人工港的那道低矮的黑线。
风还在减，我们与其说驶向它的尽头，不如说漂过去，从宽阔的外海缓缓爬进去。就连进港的最后那个钟头里，我们也开始注意到一道长长的涌渐渐从大西洋扫过圣乔治海峡，仿佛背后有风推着；但既然它朝东北去，便没有搅扰防波堤内广阔锚地的平静。我们非常缓慢地爬到内侧南边那一带——我们这类船可以停在那里、不妨碍那些蒸汽巨兽——在横渡卡迪根湾整整二十四小时、跑了七十多英里之后，我们抛下了锚，把帆收得潦潦草草，朝岸上喊话，叫来一条船，因为在巴德西海峡那场风暴里我已丢了自己的小艇。
乘船出来的人一边靠近一边用最漂亮的英语向我们喊话，或是因为我们如今到了那所谓的“威尔士之外的小英格兰”，或是因为他确实是个流放到这一带来的英格兰人；也可能他是个双语者，把这口完美的英语当作一门古典外语学来了。由这样一个人划船送你上岸，是一种体面与荣幸，因为他谈吐随意，所说的一切都有趣、真实而言辞得当。他告诫我们提防此地的人——为什么，我不知道。他告诉我们锚泊处抓底很好；在那短短一趟里，他还给了我们不少有用的信息，讲彭布罗克诸岬岛屿与大陆之间那些难走的水道——我们南行的路上必须经过。上岸时，他为他的服务要了一笔小得惊人的报酬，又约定一个固定的钟点再来接我们回船。

===Original===

In all things this man was worthy and a friend, for I could see in his eyes that he suffered exile.
We came back to him after we had provisioned, clambered aboard, and made ready for one of those empty days which pass so quickly on board a boat: hours and hours during which nothing is done, hours which on shore would exasperate every nerve, but which here, on the soft movement of the sea, go past as easily as sleep. For time on the water is quite different from time on land. It is more continuous; it is more part of the breathing of the world; less mechanical and divided. There was not a breath of air, so there was no question of starting again during all those hours. The great rollers outside were now at last beginning to affect the harbour a little, and the *Nona*, with her too high topmast, swung rather heavily to the slight movement; but of wind not a stir. The falling of the gradual darkness brought on a night brilliant with stars and no moon. There we lay, sleeping and waking, smoking, talking a little, vacuous, ready to put up sail with the first hint of a wind and to get outside. But all that night until morning again, the calm continued.
How vast was that haven in the stillness: a mile of ebony water holding the stars and cut off like a lake from the deep by man! How admirable are the great works of man! But greater is God.
In the profound night and the silence I considered that wide space of shelter and the dyke man had made against the Atlantic Power.
For two hours I so pondered upon our strength and our nothingness, worshipping also the night until the stars had changed. Then I went down below and slept. But even with the dawn there came no breeze.
It was about half-past nine in the morning when there did come a draught from the northwards. There was very little of it; it hardly crisped the oily, lumping water of the great haven, but such as it was, we, weary of all this indolence, determined to take advantage of it and to set sail. So we took up the anchor and very slowly lay close to that breath of wind until we came to the breakwater ahead, and there we found the rollers outside large as though they were in the main ocean: so large that when we sank into their troughs there was no headway on the boat and the light air only caught her insufficiently upon the crests.

===Chinese===

样样事情上，这个人都是正派人、是朋友，因为我能从他眼里看出他承受着流放。
我们补给完毕回到他那里，攀上船，准备度过船上人那种一晃而过的空日子：一连几个钟头无所事事，这样的钟头若在岸上会磨得人神经冒火，可在这里，在海的轻柔起伏上，却像睡眠一样轻易地过去了。因为水上的时间与陆地上的时间全然不同。它更连贯；它更是这世界呼吸的一部分；不那么机械，不那么割裂。一丝风也没有，所以这些钟头里根本谈不上再启航。外头那些大涌此刻终于开始稍稍搅动港内，诺娜号带着她那根过高的中桅，在这样的轻晃里相当沉重地摆动着；可风，一丝也不动。渐浓的黑暗带来一个星光灿烂而无月的夜。我们躺在那里，睡了又醒，抽着烟，偶尔说说话，空空的，随时准备一有风的迹象就升帆出港。可整整一夜直到早晨，平静依旧。
静止中的那片港湾何其浩大：一英里乌木色的水盛着星星，被人为地从深海那儿截成一个湖！人类的伟大工程何其可赞！但上帝更大。
在深沉的夜里与寂静中，我思量着那片宽阔的庇护之所，思量着人类为对抗大西洋之力筑起的这道堤。
两个钟头我就这样思量着我们的力量与我们的虚无，也崇拜着夜，直到星辰换了位。然后我下舱睡了。可即便天亮，也没有风来。
上午九点半左右，北边果真来了一缕风。风很小；几乎吹不起这座大港油腻发闷的水面，但尽管如此，我们已被这闲散磨够了，决计利用它启航。于是起锚，紧紧贴着那缕风极慢地走，直到防波堤跟前；在那里我们发现外面的涌大得像在大洋中央：大得当我们滑进浪谷时船便失了前进力，轻风只在浪尖上不痛不痒地擦她一下。

===Original===

Even as we thus attempted to make some way﻿—lifted hugely up and down in a solemn sport of the sea, now seeing nothing but water astern and ahead and then, immediately after, the whole coast and its heights, and then down into the trough again﻿—the little wind there was entirely failed us. We drifted aimlessly and in all the futility of such motion, up and down over the great swells and there even seemed to be a sort of slight drift. It was, perhaps, only the set of the swell taking us against our course back eastward and northward.
We took council together and determined that with the very first breath of air we could find we would run back to anchor and wait for a wind worth having. So we did, and a second day passed in Fishguard harbour, as empty as the first, and a second night; and then it was I completed the tune and first verse of a song called the “Chanty of the *Nona*.” The whole of that time the calm was broken by but few hints of very uncertain airs.
But just as dawn was beginning upon the third day, when it was already so light that the sea looked white against the black land, and before the first touch of colour had made living the edges of the inland hills, a heartening wind from those hills, cool with morning, fresh and heavy, not too strong, a friendly wind, a wind just suited to our course﻿—for it was a point or two north of east﻿—blew down from old Wales upon the sea.
Of this we took an immediate advantage and were out at once, my companion steering for the breakwater head at a good pace, while I made the anchor secure forward and then came back and joined him. We rounded the breakwater, took her well out and then gybed over with something of a clatter and a shock and ran down for Strumble, under the growing day. The great light was still flashing from its tower, standing on an island off the head, and the name of that island is Michael. Once we were round that headland, the wind was fairly on our port quarter and it was a merry race with a happy offshore wind all the way down to Saint David’s, so that not only the day but also the old land and the much older sea and the old boat, that was launched so long ago, and we ourselves, were for the moment young.

===Chinese===

正当我们这样试着赶一点路——在海的一场庄严游戏里被高高抬起又放下，一时只看得见船头船尾的水，随即整条海岸及其高处又映入眼帘，接着又落回浪谷——那一点小风也彻底没了。我们漫无目的地漂着，在这种徒劳的运动里，在巨大的涌上上下下，甚至似乎还有一点缓缓的漂移。那也许只是涌的推移，把我们逆着航程朝东北方向带回去。
我们合计了一番，决定一找到哪怕一丝风，就回锚地抛锚，等一股值得要的风。我们照办了；在菲什加德港又过了和头一天一样空的第二天、第二个夜；就是在那时，我写完了一首歌的曲调和第一段歌词，歌名叫“诺娜号船歌”。那整段时间里，平静只被几阵游移不定的风息打断过。
可就在第三天将破晓时，天已亮得海在黑色的陆地映衬下显出白色，内陆群山的边缘尚未染上第一笔颜色，一阵振奋的风从那些山上吹下来，落在海上——带着清晨的凉意，新鲜而饱满，不太强，是一阵友好的风，一阵恰好合我们航路的风——因为它是东偏北一两点——从古老的威尔士吹下来。
我们立刻抓住它，马上出了港，同伴掌舵，以不慢的速度驶向防波堤头，我则在船首把锚收拾牢，再回来与他作伴。我们绕过防波堤，把她好好带出去，然后哗啦一声、震了一下，转过帆向，在渐亮的天色里向斯特朗布尔驶去。大灯还从它的塔上闪光，塔立在海岬外的一座岛上，那岛名叫迈克尔。一绕过那个海岬，风便稳稳落在左舷尾，一路到圣戴维都是一场乘着可心离岸风的欢快赛跑，于是不光这一天，还有那古老的陆地、更古老得多的海、那条很久以前下水的老船，以及我们自己，一时都年轻了。

===Original===

So ran we down for Saint David’s and there, putting her nose due south, I, holding the tiller, headed her for that exceedingly dangerous millrace of a strait between Ramsey Island and the mainland, called Ramsey Sound.
It was full ebb. The water roared and thundered over the rocks inshore for all the world as though we were not upon the sea at all but upon the lower reaches of a tidal river. Though we were running under the lee of the land we were not too much blanketed, and it was a comfort to feel the strength of the wind on the boat and to know that one had full way in such a pass, for these narrow sounds of the Pembrokeshire coast are perilous to ships and men. Here, in this very passage, lay two sets of rocks to the right and to the left, called by the name of the two perils attendant upon the wealthy young. For the first set of rocks is called “The Horses” and the other “The Bitches.” These are rocks over which, I say, the rush of the ebb made a noise like a weir. We were through it in no time, at a pace which, had she touched anything, would have been the end of herself and of all aboard; but there was no danger of that because, as I have said, she had full way upon her and was lively to the helm. Nevertheless was it a relief to come tearing out of this perilous corridor into the wholesome width of Saint Bride’s Bay beyond.
Now, as I was coming through this ticklish management of Ramsey Sound, I was too much taken up with creening below the boom to make out the water at the rush of the bows and to steer with precision, I was too much taken up with the business of exactitude under the strain, to turn to a thing which, from the land long ago, had puzzled me. That is the way in which one of the most ancient roads in Britain comes down here to an open beach just above the rush and the roar of Ramsey Sound and stops dead upon the sands. That open beach is to this day called *Porth Mawr*﻿—“The Great Port.” The ancient road here comes along the southern coast of Wales as far as Carmarthen and then branches, one branch coming up Cardigan Bay, which is called, I think, after Saint Helena, the mother of Constantine, and the other coming straight along by Saint David’s.

===Chinese===

我们就这么朝圣戴维跑去，到了那里，把船头正对南方，我握着舵，让她对准拉姆齐岛与大陆之间那条极其凶险的、磨坊水道似的海峡，人称拉姆齐海峡（Ramsey Sound）。
正是平潮急落的时分。水在岸边礁石上咆哮如雷，仿佛我们根本不在海上，而在一条潮汐河的下游。虽然我们行在陆地的背风面，却也没有被遮蔽得太狠，能感觉到风压在船上的力量、知道在这样的关口船有十足的速度，是件让人安心的事，因为彭布罗克海岸这些狭窄水道对船和对人都是凶险的。就在这条水道里，左右各卧着一组礁石，名字取自富裕青年常遭的两种祸事：一组叫“群马”，另一组叫“群牝”。我说，落潮从这些礁石上冲过，声如堰坝。我们转眼就穿了过去，那速度，只要擦上任何东西，船和船上所有人就都完了；但绝无此虞，因为如前所说，她速度十足，舵下灵便。话虽如此，从这条凶险的走廊里冲腾而出、进入前方圣布莱德湾爽朗的宽阔水面，仍叫人松一口气。
我在拉姆齐海峡这段需要小心对付的航程里，一直忙着低身缩在帆桁下张望船首冲开的水、把舵掌准，在紧张里专注于精确，无暇去想一件自岸上远远望来便令我困惑的事：不列颠最古老的道路之一，怎么会在这里下到一片开阔的沙滩上——就在拉姆齐海峡的奔流与咆哮上方——便在沙上戛然而止。那片开阔的沙滩至今叫“波特·毛尔”——“大港”。这条古道沿威尔士南海岸走到卡马森便分岔，一支沿卡迪根湾上行——我想它是以君士坦丁之母圣海伦娜命名的——另一支径直沿圣戴维过来。

===Original===

They call these old roads “Roman roads,” and so I suppose they were, but still many of them were there in some form before this island entered the comity of Europe, before the Roman unity and order were finally imposed so that England became England.
Anyhow, there was a very old road of the first importance running down that way, and near it stands the see of Saint David. Now the interest is this: it comes down to that beach above the narrow roaring river of Ramsey Sound. Why on earth does it strike the sea there? It is true that antiquity pulled up boats on to the shore and might use the beach as we today use a harbour﻿—though in our tidal seas a harbour was always better, I should have thought. But even if they thought the beach better than a harbour (which it manifestly could not be for commerce), why should they call it “The Great Port”? There it lies on a most impossible bit of coast, leading nowhere and as valueless a landing-place as you could get﻿—granted a beach﻿—anywhere in the island. Did the ancients throw out great breakwaters and thus form a harbour? That could not be, for surely, had that been done, there would be remains of such mighty work.
I leave it unsolved, as I and all other men must do this and all other problems, with the possible exception of the trisection of the plane angle, which I once all but caught hold of by the tips of my fingers during a long crossing of the Atlantic in a miserable lame steamer, too old for her job, in the year 1897, before the gods had left this broken world.
The *Nona* came therefore grandly out of Ramsey Sound with a sweep and dignified nod into the calm of Saint Bride’s Bay, but I knew very well that there was before me another trial, more difficult still, called Jack Sound, between Skomer and the mainland, seven miles to the south.
Lord, what a tangle of dangers are here for the wretched mariner!
