Rocks and eddies and overfalls and shooting tides; currents and (as you shall shortly hear) horrible great mists, fogs, vapours, malignant humours of the deep, mirages, false ground, where the anchor will not hold, and foul ground, where the anchor holds forever, spills of wind off the irregular coast and monstrous gales coming out of the main west sea; and, most terrible of all, Wild Goose Race.

===Chinese===

人们把这些古道叫作“罗马大道”，我想也是；但其中许多条，在这座岛进入欧洲大家庭之前、在罗马的统一与秩序最终确立、英格兰由此成为英格兰之前，就以某种形式存在了。
无论如何，有一条头等重要的大路正是通到那一带，圣戴维的主教座堂就立在近旁。有趣之处在这里：它下到那片沙滩，就在狭窄咆哮的拉姆齐水道之上。它到底为什么偏偏在那里撞上海？古人确会把船拖上岸，把沙滩当作我们今天的港口用——尽管在我们这潮汐海里，港口总归更好，我本该这么想。但即便他们认为沙滩比港口好（对商业而言它显然不可能更好），为什么叫它“大港”？它躺在一段最不可能的海岸上，哪儿也不通，只要你容许是一片沙滩，它就是全岛找得到的最没价值的登陆地。古人莫非筑起巨大的防波堤，从而形成了一座港？不可能，因为倘若筑过，那样浩大的工程必会留下遗迹。
这个问题我搁下不解答，正如我和所有人都必须搁下这个问题和所有别的问题一样——可能的例外是平面角的三等分；1897年，在诸神尚未离开这残破世界的年代，我乘一条可怜的瘸腿轮船横渡大西洋的漫长航程中，一度差一指尖就抓住了它。
诺娜号就这样气派地冲出拉姆齐海峡，一转一颔首，进入圣布莱德湾的平静；但我清楚知道，前面还有一道更难的关口等着我，叫杰克水道，在斯科默岛与大陆之间，往南七英里。
主啊，这片水域给可怜的水手们预备了怎样一团乱麻般的凶险！礁石、涡流、乱流涌、疾潮；海流，还有（你很快就会听到的）可怕的大雾、浓霭、深处的恶性体液、蜃景、锚抓不住的烂底，和锚一咬就永不再放的污底；不规则海岸上洒下的阵风，和从西面大洋里冲出的妖风巨飙；而最可怕的，是野雁急流（Wild Goose Race）。

===Original===

I will maintain with the ancients that there are some parts of the sea upon which a god has determined that there shall be peril: that these parts are of their inward nature perilous and that their various particular perils are but portions of one general evil character imposed by the Powers. For you will notice that wherever there is one danger of the seas there are many. If it is an overfall or a race, then in that neighbourhood you will also have reefs, unaccountable thick weather, shifting soundings, bad holding, and all the rest of it. Witness the western approach to the Isle of Portland, or the Bight of Saint Malo, with the Channel Islands and their innumerable teeth; the entry to the Straits of Messina and other places recorded in histories and in pilot books. Our moderns will have it that such things are chance and an accumulation of them a blind accident, but I hold with those greater men, our fathers. Someone here in these places, some early captain, first sailing offended the gods of the sea. Hence all the tangle of the southern corner of Wales, Jack Sound and Ramsey Sound and the Bishop and his clerks, and, worst of all, Wild Goose Race.
I write without knowledge of the Wild Goose Race. I have never been through it; neither have you been through death, you who read this. Yet, as we all know that death is a perilous passage and in accordance dread it, so I with the Wild Goose Race, well so named. My book told me that little ships like mine getting into that water were often dismasted and “even foundered.” I like that word “even.”
It seems that in Wild Goose Race a boat is taken up and pitched to heaven and let drop again, twirled round like a teetotum, thrown over on her side, banged off sideways with great stunning blows upon the cheekbone, and blinded all the time with cataracts of spray, the while the air is filled with a huge mocking laughter. Many races do I know, Portland Race and Alderney Race and Little Saint Alban’s Race, of which you shall later hear, and the Race round the Skerries of Anglesey, if you may call that a race, and the Race in Bardsey Sound.

===Chinese===

我要与古人一道坚持：海上有一些地段，是某位神明定了要它们出险情的；这些地段其内在本性就是凶险的，它们各自的种种特别的险，不过是诸神加予的一个总体恶性的各个部分。因为你会注意到，凡海上有一种危险的地方，必有许多种。若是乱流或急流，那么左近你还会碰上暗礁、莫名的大雾天、变迁的水深、抓不住的锚地，以及其余的一切。波特兰岛的西入口便是明证，圣马洛湾与海峡群岛及其无数獠牙也是，墨西拿海峡的入口也是，还有史书与航路指南里记载的别处。我们的现代人硬说这些是偶然，凑在一处是盲目的意外，可我站在我们父辈那些更伟大的人一边。这些地方的什么地方，某位早期的船长，首航便得罪了海上的诸神。于是有了威尔士南角这一整团乱麻：杰克水道、拉姆齐水道、“主教与他的教士们”礁群，还有最糟的，野雁急流。
我写野雁急流，并无亲历。我从没走过它；正如读这本书的你也从没走过死亡。然而，正如我们都知道死亡是一段凶险的路程并因此畏惧它，我对野雁急流——名字起得多好——也是如此。我的书告诉我，像我这样的小船进了那片水，常常被刮断桅杆，“甚至沉没”。我喜欢“甚至”这个词。
据说在野雁急流里，船会被举起抛上天，再撒手落下，像陀螺一样拧转，被甩到一侧，颧骨上挨着一记记震得人发懵的大耳光，自始至终被瀑布般的浪花打得睁不开眼，而空中回荡着一片巨大的哄笑。急流我识得许多：波特兰激浪、奥尔德尼激浪，还有小圣奥尔本斯急流（这个你稍后会听到），安格尔西岛礁外的急流——如果那也算急流的话——还有巴德西海峡里的急流。

===Original===

But there are many races, which, thank heaven, I do not know and which I do not propose to know, such as the Race of Ushant, and the very damnable races of the northern headlands, including that Great Maelstrom of which we hear so much and see so little, and which puts on great airs: but none of these, I am told, is comparable to Wild Goose Race, therefore did I let it alone upon this passage. Do not believe it when you read it in books that a race is made by anything so simple as a tide against a wind or a sudden precipice in the depths of the sea. It is made by the anger of those that rule the sea. And yet of races there is one that has always treated me kindly, and that is the race off the Start. I have been through it I know not how many times and always was the sea there either quite smooth, gentle, purring, and domestic; or an honest running sea, parallel and ordered. But, perhaps, whatever governs the Start Race had received from me without my knowing it, some courtesy. I take this opportunity to thank whatever Powers govern that little corner of the watery world for their kindness.
I have often seen pictures of these Powers, horses and men and women with fishes’ tails and scales, and gods without scales that ride in chariots over the sea. I suppose in that world, or in those circles, the scales are a badge of service, making the link between the immortals and the dumb fishes of the sea; just as our highbrows make a link between the rich masters who rob the modern world and the millions who work. Only there is this difference, that our highbrows have no scales; they have nothing in common with the dumb millions who work. But I will not pursue this metaphor lest I should offend Mr. and Mrs. Able, who themselves indeed live in western suburbs but, upon the other hand, are perpetually in the houses of the great.
Where then was I? Why, coming into the quiet and amplitude of Saint Bride’s Bay, but having before me the terrors of Jack Sound. For it is true of the sea here as everywhere, that it is the symbol of life, and of our ceaseless duties, and of death. We must never expect long quiet in the business of our living, nor any long security in any passage of the sea.

===Chinese===

但也有许多急流，感谢上天，我不认识也不打算认识：比如阿善特急流，北方诸海岬那些极其可咒的急流，包括我们听得那么多、见得那么少、架子摆得那么大的大漩涡；但据说这些没有一个比得上野雁急流，所以我这一回绕开了它。书上说急流不过是潮顶了风、或海底突然一道悬崖之类简单的东西，你别信。急流是那些统治大海者的愤怒造出来的。不过急流之中，倒有一处始终善待我，那就是斯塔特角外的急流。我不知从它中间过了多少回，那里的海要么十分平顺、温柔、呜呜作响、驯服如家犬，要么是一片诚实的奔涌之海，平行而有序。不过，也许是治理斯塔特急流的那些存在，曾在不知不觉间受过我某种礼遇吧。我愿借此机会感谢掌管这方水界那一小角的诸势力，谢他们的好意。
我常常见到这些势力的画像：马，男女，长着鱼尾和鳞，还有不带鳞、驾着战车驰过海面的神。我想在那个世界、在那些圈子里，鳞是仆役的徽记，连结着不朽者与海中哑口的鱼类；正如我们的高知们连结着掠夺现代世界的富有主子们与劳作的千百万大众。只有这一点不同：我们的高知们不生鳞；他们与劳作的哑口大众毫无相通之处。但这个比喻我不往下追了，免得开罪埃布尔先生与太太，他们自己确实住在西郊，可另一方面又永远出入豪门。
那么我方才说到哪儿了？哦，正驶进圣布莱德湾的安宁与开阔，可前面还有杰克水道的种种恐怖。因为这里的海和各处一样，是生命的象征，是我们无尽职责的象征，是死亡的象征。在活着这门营生里，我们永远别指望长久的安静；在海的任何一段航程里，也永远别指望长久的安全。

===Original===

But I must say that this run across Saint Bride’s Bay was very genial upon that summer noon, only there came with the early afternoon those ominous patches of an oily look to windward, the falling of the breeze, the sea still running, and yet the canvas not always over well filled: briefly, the renewed menace of a deadly calm. That calm fell upon us suddenly enough, just at the moment when we might, with the last of the ebb, have run through Jack Sound. It came upon us at the mouth of that forbidden passage, and left us idle and foolish, pointing anyhow and seeing, to our despair, the marks upon the distant shore slowly moving backward as (alas!) we drifted north.
Truly, indeed, did the immortal trio﻿—Swift, Arbuthnot, and Pope﻿—exclaim in chorus: “What is mortal man but a broomstick!” Never are we in half a gale of wind but we pray crapulously for calm. Never are we in a calm but we whine peevishly for wind. What, Dog, would you have the weather cut out for you like a suit of clothes? Is all the universe to arrange itself simply to your convenience, as it does for the very rich﻿—so long as they keep off the sea? Will you not be content with sailing unless just that wind plays which is exactly trimmed for your miserable barque, neither too strong nor too light nor too far forward so that you have to beat, nor so far aft that you fear a gybe, or pooping from a running sea? Will you never repose in the will of your Maker and take things as they come? Why, then, drift round Skomer like a fool!
Which, indeed, was what the *Nona* did, for as it fell dead calm, the set of the sea took her in the most amusing unaccountable way on a jolly little voyage of exploration. The sea took charge. The tiller was not worth holding, the helm swung idle, and the canvas hung like the flabby muscles on the face of an Opposition leader towards the end of the third hour of a speech from the Front Bench to which he has had to listen, sorely against his will. It hung loose and inept, like the hands of a poor man waiting orders in the presence of a rich man, or like the mind of a sceptic when he considers the ultimate nature of good and evil﻿—let alone beauty. The sea took charge and trolled the little boat along, keeping her just so far from the shore, as who should say, “See, my child, how remarkable is the Island of Skomer!

===Chinese===

但我得说，在那个夏日正午横渡圣布莱德湾是一桩很惬意的事，只是午后刚到，上风处便现出那些油亮的不祥斑块，风软了下去，海还在涌，帆却不再总是吃得饱满：简言之，死一般无风的威胁又来了。那平静来得够突然，恰好在我们可以搭落潮的尾巴穿过杰克水道的时刻落到我们头上。它在那条禁航水道的口子上拿住了我们，把我们撂在那里无所事事、傻里傻气，船头指着随便什么方向，绝望地望着远处岸上的标志慢慢向后移——因为（唉！）我们在朝北漂。
那不朽的三人组——斯威夫特、阿巴思诺特和蒲柏——齐声感叹得真对：“人不过是一根扫帚柄！”每遇半场大风，我们就醉醺醺地求平静；每遇无风，我们又哼哼唧唧地讨风。怎么，你这狗东西，还想让天气像西装一样照你的尺寸裁出来？整个宇宙都要单单图你方便，像它伺候大富翁那样伺候你——只要他们不下海？除非风恰好为你那条可怜的小船调校得恰到好处，不太强、不太弱、不太靠前逼你抢风，也不太靠后叫你怕突然转帆或被追浪打尾，你就不能心满意足地航行？你就从不能安息于造你者的旨意、来什么受什么？那么好吧，像个傻子一样绕着斯科默漂吧！
这也确乎正是诺娜号的遭遇：风一死，海的推移便以最有趣、最没来由的方式接管了她，带她做了一次小小的欢乐探险。海做了主。舵柄不值得握，舵空荡着，帆皮塌塌地垂着，像反对党领袖的脸颊肌肉——在头排议席的长篇演讲进行到第三个钟头时，他不得不听，满心不情愿。它松垮而无用，像穷人在富人面前候命的手，又像怀疑论者思量善与恶——更别提美——的终极本质时的头脑。海做了主，牵着小船一路拖钓，让她离岸始终那么远，仿佛在说：“看哪，孩子，斯科默岛何其可观！跟我来，我把它外岸的每一道缝都指给你看。”就这样，在那片平静里，潮水带我们绕开斯科默，离杰克水道越来越远。

===Original===

Come with me, I will show you every cranny of its outer shore.” So were we taken off round Skomer by the tide in that calm, further and further from Jack Sound.
When they had played this farce with us long enough, the Powers halted the sea, as is their fashion every six hours, and we found ourselves close aboard a large ketch which lay at anchor off that Skomer shore. There was one man upon the deck, smoking a pipe and keeping his mind empty, as is the duty of all mortals in such few intervals of leisure as heaven affords us. He bade us not knock into him, and we called out to him (but not very loud, for we were at a familiar cast of but few yards) that if we came too close aboard we would shove off and do him no harm. We asked him who he was and where he was going. He did not tell us who he was, but he told us where he was going, which was to Cork, and what he had on board, which was a cargo of coal.
The lazy sun, halfway down the sky, looked indifferently upon us; the quite smooth sea turned again in its perpetual come and go, and took us slowly back in so many hours, till we found ourselves again by evening where we had started, at the mouth of Jack Sound. What this current or tide movement was which had thus taken us nearly round Skomer and back again, I know not, but it was a journey that cost nothing, and, tedious as it was, we had at least learned another few miles of shore. It seemed, however, all of a set purpose, for the wind now blew again lightly but steadily from the northeast. The ebb was well on its way, and we could point through Jack Sound, and hope to be in Milford Haven, round Saint Anne’s Head, before the late darkness of this day in early June.
So did we enter that passage.
Jack Sound in that evening light was a repetition of Ramsey, only more violent, for we were more in the full roar of the ebb. There was the same rush as of a millrace, the same cataracts of falling water over the landward rocks. We came out of it with the setting sun glorious upon our starboard quarter, and ran quickly for the end of Skokholm﻿—another Scandinavian name﻿—sure (in that steady breeze) of rounding Saint Anne’s Head easily before darkness, and dropping anchor in some cove of Milford Haven.
But nothing is ever certain at sea.

===Chinese===

他们跟我们把这场闹剧演够了，诸势力便叫海停下——一如他们每六个钟头的惯例——我们发现自己紧挨着一条大双桅帆船，它就锚泊在斯科默那面岸边外。甲板上有一个人，抽着烟斗，让脑子空着——上天赐给我们凡人的闲暇间隙既然这样少，让脑子空着便是所有人的本分。他叫我们别撞上他；我们朝他喊话（声音不大，因为我们离得已近，不过几码），说倘若靠得太近，我们会撑开，不会碰坏他。我们问他是谁、去哪儿。他没有说他是谁，但说了去哪儿——科克——以及船上装的是什么——一船煤。
懒洋洋的太阳挂在半空，漠然地看着我们；十分平滑的海又开始了它永远的来去，用几个钟头把我们慢慢送了回去，到傍晚我们发现自己又回到了出发的地方：杰克水道的口子。这股把我们几乎绕斯科默一圈又送回来的水流或潮汐运动是什么，我不知道；但这是一趟不花钱的旅程，尽管乏味，我们至少又多认得了几英里海岸。不过，这一切似乎都出于一个既定的安排，因为此刻风又轻轻而稳定地从东北吹来。落潮正在半途，我们可以指向杰克水道穿过去，指望在这六月初的这一天夜色迟迟降临之前，绕过圣安妮角，进到米尔福德港。
于是我们驶进了那条水道。
在那傍晚的光线里，杰克水道是拉姆齐的重演，只是更猛烈，因为我们更贴近落潮咆哮的主流。同样是磨坊水道般的奔流，同样是向岸礁石上泻下的瀑布。我们冲出来时，落日的余辉正辉煌地照在右舷尾上，随即快速驶向斯科克霍姆岛末端——又一个斯堪的纳维亚名字——（乘着那阵稳风）笃定能在天黑前轻松绕过圣安妮角，在米尔福德港的某处小湾下锚。
可海上从没有什么是确定的。

===Original===

We were, perhaps, a mile from the Head when there rolled up with astonishing rapidity from out the main ocean a solid bank of cloud, sweeping the level of the sea. I say cloud rather than fog, because it was so solid and so dark. It caught us like a cloak thrown over a man’s head; and, before we knew where we were, we found ourselves steering hopelessly by the card, not daring to keep too much out, lest we should lose the mouth of the haven; not daring to keep too much in, lest we should strike: and nothing to prevent that but listening anxiously to the great fog-blast, which was now hooting regularly in its panic from the height of the headland.
What followed for nearly two hours was such an adventure as only wretched amateurs would indulge in, and amateurs in a craft which did not draw more than six feet of water. The wind, such as it was, had dropped, of course, with the fog. It gave us only enough way to creep eastward before it; and what we proposed to do was so to feel our way in the fading light through that dense mist until we should hear the slight surge of water upon the rocks of the haven mouth to port, that is, on the north side of the haven entry. I had a large plan aboard, and I knew that there was water close up to the shore, and that it was plain enough going, with nothing hidden beneath the surface. So we gingerly put her head slightly round, when we made out the mournful menace of the foghorn to have come just aft of the beam; my companion took the helm, and I went forward with the lead, and also to call out to him to put the helm up whenever that might be made necessary by our getting too close. We faintly heard already the slight wash of the gently heaving water, and I saw, some few yards off the bow, little gleams of white through the mist, which were the foam sliding from the rock surfaces, as we slipped by. The *Nona*’s movement was so very slight that even if she had struck a glancing blow, nothing that was not jagged would have harmed her greatly; but we were preserved and did not touch.
We had this further good fortune, that, just as it grew quite dark, the sound of the slight surge to leeward receded and disappeared so that we knew we had turned the headland and opened the first bay of that long inland arm of the sea.

===Chinese===

我们离那海角大约还有一英里时，一片结结实实的云从大洋深处以惊人的速度滚了上来，扫着海平面。我说云而不是雾，因为它那样密实、那样黑。它罩住我们，像一件大衣兜头抛到人头上；还没弄清身在何处，我们已绝望地按罗盘航行，不敢太靠外，怕错过港的入口；又不敢太靠里，怕触礁：而能依靠的，只有焦灼地去听那巨大的雾号——此刻它正惶惶地、有规律地从海角高处鸣叫。
接下来的近两个钟头，是一场只有可怜的业余爱好者才会去招惹的冒险，而且是吃水不过六英尺的小船上的业余爱好者。风呢，本来就那么一点，当然又随着雾一起落了。它只给我们够爬行着朝东移动的一点速度；我们打算做的，是在渐褪的光线里摸索着穿过那片浓雾，直到听见左舷——也就是港口入口北侧——港口礁石上轻微的拍岸浪声。我手边有一张大图，我知道水一直深到岸边，一路干净利落，水面下没藏任何东西。于是，当我们辨出雾号的哀恫威胁已恰好落到船尾之梁后，我们小心翼翼地把船头略略转过去；同伴掌舵，我带着测深锤到船头去，兼着随时喊他向上风压舵，一旦我们靠得太近就有此必要。我们已经隐约听见轻涌拍水的微响，又看见船首几码之外的雾里闪过一星星白——那是从礁石面上滑下来的泡沫，正被我们一一流过。诺娜号的动作那样轻，即便擦上一记斜碰，只要不是锯齿，也伤不了她多少；但我们蒙受了保佑，一处也没碰。
我们还有一桩运气：恰恰在天全黑时，下风处那阵轻微拍岸浪的声音退去、消失了，于是我们知道已绕过海角，敞开了这道狭长内海湾的第一湾。

===Original===

Our chart showed us good depth and good holding-ground, and a good berth for the night, granted that there should be no other craft about. We heard none such through the fog; no voices, no swinging spars, no movement at all. So when we had got well into the bight, we dropped anchor; trusting to luck that, as she swung to her chain, she would not strike shoal ground nor anything near. Had we still had the dinghy, I would have gone out and explored and made certain, and risked my chance of getting back aboard through the mist, but, having no dinghy, we had to take things as they were, and these seemed secure enough, for we were well out of the fairway. The first of the night was interrupted by that perpetual call of the great horn upon the headland, but before midnight the fog lifted, the sound ceased, and a profound silence fell upon the sea and the land. We had also the comfort of seeing that we had plenty of room to swing, with the shore perhaps a quarter of a mile away.
That night as I fell asleep I designed the second and third verses of the “Chanty of the *Nona*.”
The next morning we found, following the flood up the Haven, a very slight breeze from the west and north, not enough to allow us to beat up against the current; so we passed the time till the beginning of the ebb, or rather till the flood should slacken, sailing slowly up that long fjord, Milford Haven, of which one reads so much in history, but which today, having no industrial hinterland, has half dropped out of men’s memories. Two evils of the past are rooted in its landscape, for it was here that the first Tudor claimant, “the foul usurper, Henry Tydder,” landed with his French mercenaries to try for the crown, which he grasped in two weeks on Bosworth Field. And it was hence that the transports sailed for the Puritan orgy of lust, loot, and massacre in Ireland under Cromwell’s leadership. I wondered as I cruised about, watching the shores and the little town near by, what further fate Milford Haven might hold for England. Things go by threes﻿—especially evils﻿—what will the third curse arising from this inlet be?
The wind freshened just as the flood was slackening, so we turned to beat out. It was well into the afternoon when we took the open sea, with the wind a trifle north of west, so that our last board out of harbour was a free run.

===Chinese===

海图显示水深良好、抓底牢固，倘若周围没有别的船，便是一个过夜的好泊位。雾里我们没听见有这样的船：没有人声，没有晃动的桅木，没有任何动静。于是进了这处小湾深处，我们便抛了锚；只能听凭运气，但愿她随锚链回旋时不致搁上浅滩或碰到任何东西。倘若小艇还在，我就荡出去探一探、弄个明白，冒一冒险再穿过雾回船上来；可小艇没了，我们只得听天由命，而眼下的情形似乎够安全，因为我们已远离航道。上半夜被海角上那只大号的连续呼号搅扰着，但午夜前雾散了，声音停了，海与陆落入一片深沉的寂静。还有一桩安慰：我们看得见回旋的余地很足，离岸大约四分之一英里。
那夜入睡时，我构思好了“诺娜号船歌”的第二段和第三段。
第二天早晨，我们乘着涨潮沿港湾上行，找到一股西偏北的微风，风力不够顶流抢行；于是我们慢慢航行，消磨到落潮开始、或者不如说涨潮转弱的时辰，缓缓上行在那条长峡湾里——米尔福德港，史书上读到过那么多，今天却没有工业腹地，已从人们的记忆里脱落了一半。过去的两桩祸事在这片景色里扎了根：第一位都铎王位觊觎者，“污秽的篡位者亨利·都铎”，就是在这里带着他的法国雇佣兵登陆、谋取王冠，两星期后他在博斯沃思战场把它攥到了手。也是从这里，克伦威尔麾下驶出了开赴爱尔兰去进行清教徒式的淫欲、劫掠与屠杀狂欢的运输船队。我一面巡行、望着两岸和近旁的小镇，一面纳罕米尔福德港还会给英格兰存下什么进一步的命运。事不过三——祸事尤其如此——这道水湾升起的第三道诅咒会是什么？
风恰在涨潮转弱时劲了起来，我们转身抢风出港。下午过了一多半，我们才出到外海，风偏西略北，于是出港的最后一边成了一次顺风畅航。

===Original===

We took her well outside so as to be able to set a straight course for Lundy and beyond, intending a passage to Bideford River, and the ending of this voyage.
The wind dropping somewhat, it was evening before we had well passed Saint Goven’s lightship, and all that night we went quietly over the seas with a gentle following wind until, before morning, we raised Lundy Light full and clear. But the wind failed us at dawn, falling first to irregular puffs, and at last, when we were about a mile and a half northeast of the island and pointing right for Appledore Bar, it fell off altogether. Little patches of slightly ruffled water, here and there, fewer and more distant one from the other, at last faded out to nothingness; and we lay helpless, hardly moving, until the set of some slight current drew us half a mile further on into that strange piece of water called the Great White Horse Race.
What this may be in heavy weather or even in a strong breeze I cannot tell, for I have never been through it before or since. As things were, in that dead calm, it was a perpetual succession of fairly even, long, smooth, watery hills, with troughs astonishingly deep and rounded crests astonishingly high for a morning without wind. There was no way at all on the *Nona*; she lay buoyant enough, but like a log; broadside on and rolling damnably for hour after hour; shaking her spars with every lie-over, as though to so many hammer-blows; throwing the boom with a bang, although I had made it as fast as I could, and giving a violent jerk at the mainsheet each time she ended a swing. It was a miserable ten hours or so that we endured, gazing at Lundy from the top of each passing heave, and drifting there paralysed under the useless sky.
Lundy is one of those neat things which you get now and then in Nature, and which look as though they had been specially designed on a rather careful plan with one simple object. One would think Lundy could do no harm﻿—yet I always remember the Montague.
It is so placed that it gives shelter in any weather. It is a sort of little breakwater set up in the midst of the Bristol Channel, offering security to all craft, coming up or down; for there is good holding-ground everywhere about, and you can lie under the lee of the land whichever way it may be blowing.

===Chinese===

我们把她好好带到外面，以便对伦迪岛和更远处取直航线，打算去比迪福德河，为这次航行收尾。
风又落了些，等我们驶过圣戈文灯船，天已擦黑；那一夜我们乘着轻柔的尾风安静地驶过一片片海，天亮前，我们清清楚楚地望见了伦迪灯塔。可黎明时分风又弃我们而去，先是落成一阵阵不齐的阵风，最后，在我们位于岛东北约一英里半、船头正指阿普尔多尔沙洲时，风彻底停了。一小片一小片微皱的水面，这里一块那里一块，越来越少、相隔越来越远，终于褪成虚无；我们无助地躺着，几乎不动，直到一股微弱的流又把我们朝前拖了半英里，拖进那片古怪的水域——大白马急流。
这地方在大风天、甚至强风天是什么样子，我说不上，因为此前此后我都没走过。就当时那种死平如油的情况而言，它是一列接一列颇为均匀、绵长、光滑的水山，浪谷深得惊人，浪峰圆得高得惊人——对无风的清晨而言。诺娜号一点速度也没有；她浮性倒好，却像根木头；横着身子，一个钟头接一个钟头地该死地摇晃；每一次倾侧都震得索具哗哗响，像挨了一记记锤击；帆桁尽管我已尽力缚牢，还是砰地甩来甩去，每荡完一回，主帆索就狠狠一顿。我们受了可怜的十个来钟头，从每一列涌的顶上眺望伦迪，在那无用的天空下瘫痪似的漂着。
伦迪是自然界时不时赏给你的那种精巧东西，看上去像按一张相当细心的图纸特别设计出来的，只有一个简单的目的。人们会以为伦迪岛干不出坏事——可我总记得“蒙塔古”号。
它的位置使它任何天气里都能给出庇护。它像一道小小的防波堤，立在布里斯托尔海峡当中，为来往的一切船只提供安全；因为周围处处是抓底良好的锚地，无论风怎么刮，你都可以躲在岛的下风面。

===Original===

The only drawback of the neighbourhood for small craft is this same White Horse Race, in which we had been caught by the calm. Even in that oily weather, its great swells passed us halfway to the height of our jaws, and our topmast, excessive for the little craft, made her roll all the worse, while the motion was so jerky that at times I feared for the fastenings of the shrouds at the sides. But a larger craft would think nothing of this patch of sea and, save for it, all the water round Lundy is exactly made for security.
I have never landed on the island. I have heard many legends about it, none of which I repeat for fear that they are false. But what I should most like to know (only I have never met anyone who could tell me) is whether the pleasure of isolation which these places afford increases with the years, or at last becomes intolerable? I knew a man once who, during all the latter part of his life, was torn between the desire of possessing an island, and his fear lest, once he had bought it, he should find that he had purchased misfortune. All his friends told him that islands were like those legendary objects of bad luck which one man has to pass on to another and which each new holder soon finds unendurable. To be king of one’s own land, to be quite cut off from the complexity and futilities of the world, to have a little country all of one’s own, neatly bounded, inaccessible if one chooses to make it so﻿—that is enough to tempt anyone who has lived among men. But I am assured by many who have not themselves tried it, that after a very short experience in such things one has no desire but to be rid of them. A community might find it endurable enough and, indeed, religious communities have often found it so; but not individuals, nor one family. Lundy, however, is larger than most, and perhaps the sense of isolation is less.
When we had lain tossing in the White Horse Race all through that morning and on to well past noon, there came, to our great joy, about half-past one, a little breath, a little slant of wind, from off Hartland Point. For all those hours we had had no steering-way at all, merely drifting and tossing to the loud clapping of empty canvas.

===Chinese===

这一带对小船唯一的毛病，正是我们被平静困住的那片白马急流。即便在那油亮的天色里，它的大涌擦过我们时也高到我们下巴的一半，而那根对小小船体来说过分的中桅更让她摇得厉害，颠簸又那么突兀，有几回我都担心侧支索在两舷的固着点。可大一点的船就不把这片水放在眼里；除了它，伦迪周围的水简直是为安全量身打造的。
我从没上过这座岛。我听过许多关于它的传说，一个也不敢转述，唯恐是假的。而我最想知道的（只是一直没遇上能告诉我的人）是：这些地方给人的那种孤绝之乐，是随着年岁与日俱增呢，还是终究变得无法忍受？我认识一个人，后半辈子一直被两种欲望撕扯着：想拥有一座岛，又怕买下之后发现自己买来的是灾祸。他所有的朋友都对他说，岛屿就像那些传说中带来厄运的物件，得由一个人传给另一个人，而每个新到手的人很快便发觉无法忍受。做自己土地上的王，彻底切断尘世的纷繁与徒劳，拥有一个完全属于自己的小邦国，边界齐整，只要你愿意便无人能至——这足以诱惑任何在人堆里活过的人。可许多没亲身试过的人都向我保证，这种事尝过一小口，人就只想脱手。一个共同体也许受得了，宗教团体就常常受得了；个人受不了，一家人也受不了。不过伦迪比多数岛大些，孤绝感也许淡些。
当我们在白马急流里翻腾着熬过一整个上午、又熬到午后许久，约摸一点半，来了一丝气息、一缕斜风，从哈特兰角外吹来，令我们大喜。此前那些钟头里我们毫无舵效，只是伴着空帆劈啪的响声漂流、颠簸。

===Original===

But now the sails filled and, weary of the sea, we put her nose round for Bideford River, with this pleasant little breeze slightly blowing and coming right from the starboard quarter, so that she sailed at her best. The tide was at the neaps, there was little current, we had a straight course to steer.
The wind rose, the sun was clear and shining, and all the sea sparkled and was alive. It was a weather in which to remember the little sacred places of the shore; the little towns with their own gods of the sea, and springs tumbling through them from the cliffs above: the little places that preserve their own happenings. And one such place of which I had very often heard was there, far off upon the starboard quarter, and I knew it by the chart for Clovelly.
There are places holy to Apollo, Delian places, protected tiny townships of the sea.
So from the tumbling water between Bideford River and Lundy, that wooded coast, so isolated, remote, sacred, appeared the touch of white in the green which marked Clovelly. Looked on thus over the water that rushed by, and in the swirl of the ship’s way, Clovelly seemed what I had read in books as a boy it was, and what I suppose it once may have been, when England was England.
But England today from the land is a very different thing to England forever from the sea. Some years later I had again to seek Clovelly from the land, and I shall no more forget it than did that Northumbrian man whom God raised from the dead, and whose experience is told us by the Venerable Bede, could forget his glimpse of hell.
I had come with friends in a motorcar, because I had been told a man should see Clovelly before he died. I had come from the noble isolation of Hartland, where the great seas were running in upon the rocks after a gale and thundering at the doors of that good inn which faces ocean there. Our road ran high above the sea, and as we came near to the turning beyond which the cleft of Clovelly would open below us, and its vision of peace, we heard a murmur like that of bees swarming. As we came nearer it was a confused clamour of human beings, and as we came nearer still we saw the dreadful thing in its entirety.
A great mass of chars-à-bancs were parked on the road itself, on the steep edge of the hill and cleft whence one looked down on the village and the sea below.

===Chinese===

可此刻帆吃饱了；我们对海也腻了，便把船头转向比迪福德河，这阵可爱的小风轻轻吹着，正来自右舷尾，她跑出了最好的状态。潮在小潮期，水流微弱，我们要走的又是一直线航路。
风起了，太阳清亮地照着，整片海波光粼粼，活了起来。这样的天气，正宜想念岸边那些小小的圣地；那些有自家海神的小镇，泉水从崖顶翻腾着穿镇而过：那些守着自己那份遭际的小地方。其中一个我常听人说起的地方就在那里，远在右舷尾方向，我在海图上认出它是克洛弗利。
有些地方属于阿波罗，是得洛斯般的所在，受着庇护的海畔小小城邦。
于是，在比迪福德河与伦迪之间翻腾的水面上，那片林木葱茏的海岸——那样隔绝、偏远、神圣——露出了绿意中的一点白，标出克洛弗利的位置。隔着奔涌而过的海水、在船行的涡流里望去，克洛弗利就像我少年时在书里读到的那样，像它或许曾经的样子，在英格兰还是英格兰的年代。
可如今从陆上去的英格兰，与从海上永远望去的英格兰，是截然不同的两样东西。几年之后，我不得不从陆路再访克洛弗利，那一幕我将永志不忘，正如那位被上帝从死里复起的诺森伯里亚人忘不了他那一瞥地狱——他的经历，可敬的比德讲给我们听过。
我是和朋友们乘汽车来的，因为有人对我说，人死前该看一看克洛弗利。我从哈特兰高贵的孤绝中来，那里大风刚过，巨浪正卷上礁石，在那家面朝大洋的好客栈门前轰鸣。我们的路高悬在海之上，快到那个转弯——过了它，克洛弗利的裂谷便会在我们脚下敞开，连同它安宁的景象——我们听见一阵低语，像蜂群分巢。再近一些，那是人类一片混乱的喧嚷；再近一些，我们把那可怕的东西看了个全貌。
一大排游览车就停在路面上，停在小山与裂谷陡峭的边上，人从那里俯瞰下面的村庄与海。

===Original===

There, blocking the ridge road, an immense mass of lost souls struggled and fought for the most part, or stood squeezed in a sullen despair. Their function was﻿—not their intention, but the use to which the irony of Divine Vengeance had put them﻿—their function was to form a solid wall by which all access from the village below, all further opportunity of climbing, should be forbidden. Meanwhile up that cleft swirled, surged, pushed, strenuously and unfailingly, one mass of packed, dark-clothed mortality, closely hemmed in by the cottages (I wondered that their walls stood the strain!), and looking from where we stood very much like black pressed German caviar, the acid stuff which is sold for the destruction of the race. This wedge had its base upon the seashore and was filled with a communal desire, with a mass mind, impelling it to attempt the height﻿—a hopeless task! For the charabanc crowd above could not have been pierced by cavalry, let alone by an untrained infantry such as was this mob working hopelessly uphill.
So far so bad; but worse remained behind. For on the calm sea of that bay were to be discovered three steamers, each of them looking like a piece of cigar-shaped board on which innumerable black ants had swarmed. Of these the one nearest to the shore was still discharging in its boats further human beings, who were fighting to land on whatever little strip of foothold could be found, while the two other ships stood by ready to reinforce the attack.
So true is it that men desire beauty before all things and that great beauty draws all men to it.
The atheists, of whom some few stalwarts remain, strong against the pantheist flood; those who despise mankind when they are healthy and when they are unhealthy despair of it; the men who make nothing of life and think it immaterial whether man be or no﻿—these are, in my judgment, of a clumsy habit in argument. For they talk in mere negations. But if they would breed (as is their intention) a proper contempt for the supposed Divine in man, a proper negligence of our fellow beings and a right view of the whole insignificant human affair, they ought not to confine themselves to negatives, they ought to present vivid images, concrete and striking, and say: “Look there and judge mankind!”

===Chinese===

就在那里堵塞着山脊路，一大团迷失的灵魂多半在挣扎推搡，或者闷闷地绝望地挤作一团。他们的功能——不是他们的意图，而是神圣复仇的讽刺加给他们的用途——是组成一堵人墙，堵死从下面村子里来的一切通道，堵死再往上爬的一切可能。与此同时，在那道裂谷里，一大团挤得密密实实、穿深色衣装的凡人，涌着、挤着、推着，顽强不懈，两边紧夹在小屋之间（我真奇怪那些墙竟吃得住！），从我们站立的地方望去，非常像压紧的黑色德国鱼子酱——那种用来毁灭种族的酸东西。这枚楔子的底座踩在海滨上，内里充盈着一种共同的欲望、一种群体心智，驱策它去攻占高地——一桩无望的任务！因为上头那群游览车客，骑兵也冲不透，更别说这样一群绝望地往坡上蹭的乌合之众了。
到此为止已够糟；更糟的还在后头。因为那海湾平静的海面上，可以数出三艘轮船，每一艘都像一块雪茄形状的木板，上面密密麻麻爬满了黑蚂蚁。其中离岸最近的一艘还在用小艇往下卸人，人们争抢着在任何能找到的一小条立足之处登陆，另两艘则候在一旁，随时准备增援攻势。
人在一切之上渴求美，大美把所有人都引向它——此话何其真切。
无神论者——如今还剩下少数几员悍将，顶住了泛神论的洪流；那些健康时蔑视人类、病时又对人类绝望的人；那些把生命不当回事、以为人类有无都无关紧要的人——依我看，论辩起来手法笨拙。因为他们只会说否定的空话。他们若真想（照他们的本意）培育出对人类身上所谓神性的应有蔑视、对同胞的应有怠慢、对整个人类小破事的正当看法，就不该局限于否定的空话，而应拿出鲜活的形象，具体而触目，说：“看那里，评判人类吧！”

===Original===

I suggest to them Clovelly on a holiday; also the sands of Weston-super-Mare; also Saint Lazare Station in Paris, the Frederick Street Station in Berlin, or Liverpool Street in London, just after office hours on a weekday; or again, a crowd in Catalonia, outside the ring, when by some accident the bull gets loose.
There are other brilliant images all ready to the atheist’s hand: a dining-table of the rich, with some thirty guests, men and women, exactly alternate, like the red and the black cards in a patience, the wretched couples turning ten minutes to the left and ten minutes to the right, like jacks; the wine of only one kind﻿—yellow and acid with bubbles in it. Or again, they might give the same effect with the vision of any public vehicle, designed to hold twenty-four but packed with forty. Or again, they might do no more than distribute snapshots, judiciously snapped, of the men who are most talked about in this our day.
The truth is the atheists do not know their job; for it has been clearly proved upon the thumb and the four fingers of the left hand that men cut off from the Divine are also cut off from reason.
So we sailed on, leaving that tiny white point of Clovelly far behind in its combe, dark against the afternoon sun, and ran straight for Bideford River, till we came to the fairway.
We had also a mark to steer by which was unmistakable; it was a gate of dark smooth water in a mass of white surge. For rollers, though there was but a hearty breeze, through some mysterious process of the sea, were bursting with violence upon the bar of the river as though it blew a gale. The bar looked like one tumbling wall of angry white, even from a long way off, and, between that wall and the east side of the harbour-mouth, was this one narrow gateway of smooth water. We passed through it safely enough, steering by the leading marks, which are here quite clear, and brought up in the deep pool off Appledore, waiting for the tide to take us up to Bideford.
All that bit of coast is Charles Kingsley, with his violent fanatical genius (for it was no less) and his power of imprinting himself upon the place. Fanatical men are not usually happy; but I think Kingsley must have been happy, because he had the tide under him all the time. Everything in his own lifetime was going his way.

===Chinese===

我向他们举荐假日的克洛弗利；还有滨海韦斯顿的沙滩；还有巴黎的圣拉扎尔车站、柏林的腓特烈大街车站、伦敦的利物浦街车站，就挑个工作日下班后的时分；或者，加泰罗尼亚斗牛场外的人群，赶上哪头公牛失手跑了出来。
还有别的妙极了的形象，都为无神论者现成备着：富人的宴席，三十来位宾客，男男女女严格相间，像纸牌戏里的红黑两色，可怜的一对对轮流向左转十分钟、向右转十分钟，像千斤顶；酒只有一种——黄色带泡发酸的那种。再不然，随便哪辆公交车的景象也能收到同样效果：设计载二十四人，塞了四十。再不然，只需分发几张抓拍得恰到好处的快照，拍的是我们这个时代被谈论得最多的那些人。
实情是无神论者不称职；因为早已在左手的大拇指和四根手指上证明得明明白白：人一旦与神性切断，也就与理性切断了。
于是我们继续行驶，把克洛弗利那一点白远远抛在身后山谷里，在午后的太阳下黑成一粒，直奔比迪福德河，直到来到航道口。
我们还有一个错不了的导航标志：那是白色碎浪堆里一道深色平滑的水的门户。因为尽管只有一阵痛快的小风，海却经由某种神秘的程序，把浪弄成顶头狂风的样子，在河口沙洲上猛烈地炸开。那道沙洲远看就像一堵翻滚的白色怒墙，而在那堵墙与港口入口东侧之间，正是这一道窄窄的平滑水的门。我们循着叠标——这里十分清楚——稳稳穿了过去，泊在阿普尔多尔外的深水潭里，等潮送我们去比迪福德。
那一带海岸处处是查尔斯·金斯利，带着他狂热的天才（确实是天才）和他把自己的印记烙进这片土地的力量。狂热的人通常不快活；可我想金斯利一定快活，因为他脚下时时有潮水推着。他一生一世，样样事情都顺着他。

===Original===

The cause to which he was attached, the destruction of the Faith, appeared to be triumphing more and more; though the slightest accident the other way (such as Newman’s conversion) was an intolerable irritant to him. He lived in that part of the earth which he most loved, he enjoyed all its habits, he was supported by everything around. You could get no better and, at the same time, no more comic criticism of that Kingsleian mood than what has happed to the name “Westward Ho,” which Kingsley rendered famous. “Westward Ho” simply means the westerly of two landing-places. “Ho,” or “Hoe,” is a wharf: but that “Ho” has been spread broadcast throughout the world of English as a sort of “Halloa,” or “Yoicks,” to call the adventurous overseas; and not one man in a thousand, I suppose, but thinks of those two little letters as a hoot (perhaps he gives them a nobler name); as a call, resonant of the Elizabethan sailings.
One of the saddest things I know about the beach near Bideford River is the deadly hatred with which the dons have persecuted poor, dear Kenwith. Kenwith is a place where a few boatloads of Danes landed in the Dark Ages, and were defeated by the English. The name is quite clear, the tradition is equally clear, and the description of the position is unmistakable. Therefore have the learned, as is their wont, insisted with the utmost virulence that the tradition, the name, the description, are all a popular error, and that the place where the little scrimmage really came off was miles away.
It is interesting to analyse the motives of this sort of thing. We are all familiar with it. The universities of all countries, but especially of our own, are a regular hothouse for breeding it; but when one first comes across it, one is puzzled why it should come into being at all. Whence springs this lust for saying that the Gospel of Saint John was not written by Saint John? that Homer was not written by Homer? that the Battle of Hastings was not called the Battle of Hastings﻿—although all the people who fought there called it the Battle of Hastings? that William the Conqueror had only a handful of men there﻿—though his secretary, who saw them, and read all the documents connected with them, gives us fifty thousand? that Julius Caesar’s Gallic War was written by his tutor﻿—and all the rest of the nonsense?

===Chinese===

他所依附的那桩事业，摧毁信仰的事业，眼看节节胜利；虽然反向的些微意外（比如纽曼的皈依）会叫他无法忍受地烦躁。他住在他最爱的那片土地上，享用那里的一切习惯，四围的一切都撑着他。对金斯利这种心境，你找不到比“Westward Ho”这个名字的遭遇更好、同时也更滑稽的批评了；正是金斯利让这个名字出了名。“Westward Ho”不过是两个登陆地里偏西的那一个的意思。“Ho”（或“Hoe”）就是一道码头：可这个“Ho”已经在整个英语世界里播散开来，成了一声“呵嗬”、一声“呀呼”，召唤冒险者扬帆出海；我想千人中无一人不把这两个小字母当作一声呼哨（也许他还给它更神气的名号）；当作一声回荡着伊丽莎白时代启航的呼唤。
关于比迪福德河附近那片海滩，我知道的最可悲的事情之一，就是学究们对可怜又可亲的肯威思的死命仇视。肯威思是黑暗时代几船丹麦人登陆、被英格兰人击败的地方。名字清清楚楚，传说同样清楚，地势的描述也不会认错。于是学究们便按他们的惯例，以最恶毒的口气一口咬定：传说、名字、描述统统是民间的谬误，那场小小遭遇战的真正地点在几英里之外。
剖析这类行径的动机很有意思。我们都熟悉它。各国的大学，尤其是我们本国的，是滋生它的温床；可人第一次撞见它时，总纳闷它究竟因何而生。这种欲望从何而来——非说圣约翰福音不是圣约翰写的？荷马不是荷马写的？黑斯廷斯战役不叫黑斯廷斯战役——尽管在那儿打过仗的人全都管它叫黑斯廷斯战役？征服者威廉在那里只带了一小撮人——尽管他的秘书亲眼见过他们、读过相关的一切文书，给我们的数字是五万？恺撒的高卢战记是他老师写的——以及那一整套胡说八道？

===Original===

The powerful force urging dons to make fools of themselves in this way seems to me to come from a convergence of three currents. First of all there is the vanity of the learned man, who has all the better opportunities for action because few have any knowledge at all of the matter, and he is fairly safe from criticism﻿—or thinks himself so. His fellows will not give him away. It is clearly a flattering thing to think that one is right where all the world has been wrong, and, in a time like ours, when there has been accumulated such a mass of special technical knowledge, people are ready to swallow almost any assertion, because they know not what new evidence may have appeared.
The second source is that very human thing, the love of the marvellous﻿—though it is the love of the marvellous appearing in a very degraded form. Your pedant says: “All the apparent evidence, all tradition, all that you would call common sense, would make out Little Muddipool to be that same Little Muddipool where the treaty of Little Muddipool was signed. It is called in plain words ‘The Treaty of Little Muddipool,’ and its last words are ‘Made by us at Little Muddipool.’ But *I* tell you that it was not Little Muddipool at all, but a place a hundred miles away with a different name.” The spirit at work there is exactly the same spirit as that which says: “Common sense and your own experience will tell you that the bishop must have crossed the river either by a bridge or by a boat. *I* tell you that the holy man sailed across it on his coat. What do you think of that?”
And the third cause is a negative one. The perpetual substitution of hypothesis for fact (which is the great mark of dons today) ends by getting men into a state of mind where they can no longer weigh the proportions of evidence: they can no longer distinguish between the certain, the probable, and the absurd. Thus, it was but a little time ago that an Oxford don came out with a miracle. He said he had discovered any number of classical passages containing concealed anagrams, furnishing the most astonishing information; for instance, that Euripides, when he was a little boy, wrote the plays of Aeschylus.

===Chinese===

驱使学究们这样出丑的那股强大力量，依我看来自三股潮流的汇合。其一是学者的虚荣，他干这营生的条件得天独厚，因为几乎无人真正了解此事，他也相当安全，不怕批评——或自以为安全。同行不会拆穿他。自己对了、全世界都错了，这想法显然很受用；而在我们这样的时代，专门的技术知识积了山一样多，人们几乎什么断言都肯吞，因为谁也不知道又冒出了什么新证据。
第二个源头是很人性的东西：对奇事的爱——尽管以非常退化的形态出现。你的学究说：“一切表面的证据、全部传说、你所谓常识的一切，都指明小马迪普就是那个签了《小马迪普条约》的小马迪普。条约白纸黑字叫《小马迪普条约》，末一句是‘订于小马迪普，由我们签署’。可是我告诉你，那根本不是小马迪普，而是一百英里外另一个名字的地方。”在底下作祟的心绪，与说下面这话的心绪一模一样：“常识和你自己的经验会告诉你，主教过河要么走桥、要么坐船。可我告诉你，那位圣者是披着他的外衣渡过去的。你以为如何？”
第三个原因是负面的。永远拿假设顶替事实（这正是当今学究的大印记），到头来把人弄成一种再也掂量不出证据分量的心态：再也分不清何为确定、何为或然、何为荒诞。于是，就在不久前，一位牛津学究抛出了一个奇迹。他说他发现了数不清的古典段落里藏着颠倒字谜，提供着最惊人的信息；比如，欧里庇得斯小时候写下了埃斯库罗斯的剧本。

===Original===

A stopper was put on him, however, by a man who wrote to the *Spectator* (of all papers), proving that the said don’s name was but thinly concealed in an anagram of the opening lines of the *Iliad*, so that he must have written that excellent poem, not when he was a little boy, but long before he was born. And so much for that.
Another pleasing thing about Bideford River is the startling contrast between Appledore, on the business side of the harbour over against the sea, and Instow, all so genteel upon the further shore: Appledore frankly a lair, and Instow a desirable resort. Appledore for beer, Instow for wine; and the English talked in the one almost incomprehensible to the other. I have sometimes thought that whenever the foundations of society shall be shaken, the strong men of Appledore will sail across (you can almost walk it at low tide but for one thin channel) and storm and loot Instow. Pretty well every modern set of human habitations has this contrast of rich and poor quarters, but when there is water flowing in between them it is the more striking: with hard work on one side of the stream and the fruits of it in leisure upon the other.

===Chinese===

不过有人给他塞了个塞子：有人写信给《旁观者》——报纸里偏偏是它——证明这位学究的大名，恰恰薄薄地藏在《伊利亚特》开篇几行的字谜里，可见那首好诗不是他小时候写的，而是他出生很久以前写的。此事就此了结。
比迪福德河另一桩可喜的事，是阿普尔多尔与因斯托之间的惊人对照：一个在港口迎海的那一侧，做营生；一个在对岸，一派斯文，是个可意的度假区。阿普尔多尔坦坦荡荡是个巢穴，因斯托则是雅地。阿普尔多尔喝啤酒，因斯托喝葡萄酒；两边说的英语，几乎互不能懂。我有时想，一旦社会的根基动摇，阿普尔多尔的好汉们就会渡过来（退潮时除了那一条细水道，几乎可以走过去），攻进因斯托，劫掠一番。现代的人居群落差不多都有这种贫富两区的对照，可中间隔着一道流水时就更扎眼：河的一岸是劳作，另一岸是劳作的果实化作闲暇。

---

===Original===

My voyage was over. I had brought up in Bideford River before the long bridge of Bideford, and I made all quiet for the night, and for a long time to come. For there would I leave the *Nona* in charge, till I could take her out again.
Swaying in the cabin that night also, I finished writing the “Chanty of the *Nona*,” of which if any man say that it is a vile piece of verse, I am ready to agree with him, but if another man say that it heartens him, and fills him with the sea, why, I am ready to agree with him also. It is all one. Bideford River is the end of my passage and a place of rest. We come to death at last, as is our due, and we come into port out of the sea, and all things reach their end.
I have always thought it a very ridiculous thing to bother too much, after thirty, over literary fame: if it comes, let it come, but for God’s sake let us have no publicity with it, for to be pointed at and known is to live in a glass cage. And very much do I applaud those men who do what in youth I myself desired to do, but was unable to do, that is, to write consistently under a false name, and carefully cover one’s tracks. I wanted to write verse under one name, history under another, travel under another﻿—but fate was too strong.
Of the men who have done this in my time the most successful was Dodgson, the mathematical don at Christ Church﻿—that is, of The House. He had calculated well; and the immense fame of Lewis Carroll and of *Alice in Wonderland* left him private and secure.
Mention of that book also makes me consider how false is nearly all literary fame, or, rather, how great an element of falsity there is in nearly all such fame. *Alice in Wonderland* is a very remarkable book, as are, indeed, other books of Dodgson’s, though not all. But a large element in its vast diffusion was its consonance with a particular “drawing-room” mood of a particular day. I shall be called blasphemous, I know, but I am perfectly certain that *Alice* will not long survive the ease and unquestioned security of the England of her day. For every implication in morals, and even in humour, with which Dodgson’s books are crammed, sprang from that security and that ease.

===Chinese===

我的航行结束了。我把船停在比迪福德河上、比迪福德长桥之前，收拾停当过夜，也收拾停当往后很长一段日子。因为我要把诺娜号托付在这里，直到我再带她出去。
那夜在船舱里随波摇荡，我写完了“诺娜号船歌”；谁若说它是烂诗，我乐于同意；谁若说它让他振奋、让他满心是海，嗐，我也乐于同意。都是一回事。比迪福德河是我这段航程的终点，是一处安息。我们终究要赴死，这是我们应得的份；我们出得海来，进得港去，万事皆有终结。
我一向认为，过了三十还为文名操心是很可笑的事：它若来，就让它来；但看在上帝分上，别让它捎带出风头，因为被人指点、被人认得，就是活在玻璃笼子里。我非常佩服那些人——他们做到了我年轻时想做而没能做的事——始终用假名写作，并且小心抹去自己的脚印。我想用一个名字写诗，用另一个写史，再一个写游记——可命太强。
在我这个时代做成这件事的人里，最成功的是道奇森，基督堂——也就是“学堂”——的那位数学学究。他算计得好；刘易斯·卡罗尔与《爱丽丝漫游奇境记》的巨大名声，把他留在了私人与安稳之中。
提起那本书，也让我想到几乎一切文名何其虚假，或者不如说，几乎一切名声里都掺着多大的假。爱丽丝是一本非常出色的书，道奇森别的书其实也是——虽然不是每一本。但它风行一世，很大一个成分是它与某个特定日子里客厅趣味的投合。我知道我会被骂作亵渎，但我确信，爱丽丝在它那个安逸而高枕无忧的英格兰消逝之后，活不长。因为道奇森那些书里塞满的道德含义、乃至幽默，全从那份无忧与安逸里生出来。

===Original===

Nor is there a stronger symptom of so abnormal a passage in human affairs as was the English gentry’s generation of 1833﻿–﻿99 (abnormal, for normal men live under a strain and a peril, and in active defence against the barbarian or the menacing enemy) than the delight of Dodgson’s vast audience in nonsense: in the humour which is founded upon folly as contrasted with the wit that is founded upon wisdom. Even the facile composition of odd childish words, which had a slightly reminiscent sound, was sufficient to delight that generation. But when the terrors and the heroisms permanently return, the sawdust will run out of such things.
And, after all, what is the possession of literary fame? Can dead men use it? Does any man possessing it, unless he be asinine in his vanity, think himself the true creator of some really high line his pen has happened to set down? Is there any writing man with a true judgment of great things in writing who does not doubt his own achievement? Or is there any who, when some particular piece of verse or rhetoric which he has written convinces him of its merit, does not admit to his own soul that not *he* was the true author, but rather some force working through him?
Again, does not any writer who is also reasonably manly prefer any one of man’s virtues to an accident of verse or prose? Who with stuff in him would not rather be a brave boor than a cowardly poet?
So much for literary gents. Excluding my own self, I should like to see them all driven aboard an immense barge, and the same towed out to sea and there scuttled. Their absurd little quarrels, their posings, their insufficiency, the chaos of their minds﻿—all these things are a burden, and there is not one day’s riding upon a horse or sailing upon the sea (let alone still better things which, for the honour I bear you, I will not mention here) that is not worth the whole tonnage and shiploads of their printed stuff, save here and there a piece of lyric verse, or here and there a good run and rhythm in half a page of prose.
But, I say again, that’s all one. Here am I in Bideford River, at anchor and come to the end of the seas, leaving her, the *Nona*, my companion and my home, my very loyal boat, under the tutelage of the long bridge of Bideford, which is notoriously the end of the world.

===Chinese===

英格兰士绅那一代人（1833至1899）是人类事务中一段反常的插曲——反常，因为正常的人是活在紧张与危险之中、对蛮族与威胁之敌积极设防的——而比这更强烈的病征，就是道奇森那庞大的读者群对“无意思”的沉迷：那种建立在痴愚上的幽默，对照那建立在智慧上的机智。甚至随手拼凑几个带点怀古腔调的怪稚词语，也足以取悦那一代人。可一旦恐怖与英勇重新长驻，这类东西里的锯末就会流光。
再说了，文名这东西占了又如何？死人用得上吗？享有它的人，除非虚荣到蠢驴的地步，有谁认为自己是自己笔下偶然写下的某行真正高妙文字的真正创造者？对写作中的大境界有真鉴力的写作人，有谁不怀疑自己的成就？或者，当他写下的某一节诗或某一段雄辩让他自己也信服了它的好处，有谁不肯对自己的灵魂承认：真正的作者不是他，而是某种借他做工的力量？
再者，一个还算有丈夫气的作者，难道不更看重人的任何一样德性，胜过诗或散文的一次侥幸？肚里有货的人，谁不愿做勇敢的粗汉，而不做怯懦的诗人？
文学绅士们就说到这里。除我自己不算，我很乐意看见他们统统被赶上一条巨大的驳船，被拖到海上凿沉。他们那些可笑的小小口角、他们的装腔作势、他们的不济、他们头脑里的混乱——这些都是一副重负；骑一天马、驶一天船（更别提那些更好的事，为了我对你的敬意，这里就不提了），哪一样不抵他们那一整船一整船印出来的货色——只除偶有一两首抒情诗，或偶有半页散文里一段好节奏好奔涌。
可我再说一遍，都是一回事。此刻我在比迪福德河上，抛了锚，来到了海的尽头，把她——诺娜号，我的伴侣、我的家，我忠诚不贰的小船——托付给比迪福德长桥看管，而那座桥，众所周知，就是世界的尽头。

===Original===

Now a day came when I had some freedom again. So I set out at once from Devon with one companion, and, finding all on board as I had left it, I set sail to leave Bideford River. I proposed to myself to explore the outer sea beyond Hartland, which till then I had not seen, though now I know its character and its harbours familiarly enough.
The tide so served that it was evening when I set out, nor was it easy to shoot the channel at the bar of Appledore, in spite of the strong ebb, because such slight wind as there was had too much north in it. For the same reason we had to beat all that night, fetching up towards Hartland Point in two long boards; helped at first by the tide, but later, of course, finding it against us. It was daylight again before we came to the headland, and opened the coast beyond.
The ancients made a solemn and special sanctity, it seems, of this barrenness and strength and height of Hartland. The Romans called it the Point of Hercules. They felt, I think, that when they rounded it, they were coming into another sea; for they were much alive to the mystery of things. If they thought this of Hartland, that it was a boundary, they were right. Within Hartland, you are under the wing of England, and in homely water; outside Hartland, you have all the West before you, and the power of Ocean. And so with us that morning. Even as the wind died down there came a mighty succession of slow heavings, which grew more regular and defined until at last the smooth water, just crisped here and there, by mere breaths of air, ran in giant folds﻿—parallel, regular, unceasing, enormous. All that sea, I say, is odd, and I have felt spells upon it.
The long rollers came prodigious out of the southwest under almost a calm. There was not wind enough to do more than give the boat steerageway﻿—it was lucky we had that, for if it had been a dead calm she would have rolled broadside on and shaken herself badly with that too tall topmast of hers, as she had done on an earlier day in the White Horse Race. The rollers came on and on; not the relic of a gale (for there had been none anywhere), but raised by one of those innumerable mysterious influences which between them make the sea. They lifted one after the other, hiding the horizon; we topped them, we sank into the trough; they heaved majestically on, to thunder upon the savage grandeur of Hartland.

===Chinese===

后来终于有一天，我又得了些空闲。于是我当即带一名同伴从德文出发，见船上一切如故，便扬帆驶出比迪福德河。我打算去探一探哈特兰以外的外海——那片海我此前还不曾见过，虽然如今我对它的脾气和它的港湾都已了如指掌。

潮水正合我意，出发时已是傍晚；虽有强劲的落潮，闯阿普尔多尔栏门水道却不容易，因为仅有的那点风里北风成分太重。为着同一缘故，我们不得不整夜抢风行驶，走了两趟长程才拤近哈特兰角：起初有潮相助，到后来自然发觉潮转而与我们作对。等我们抵达那处海角，望见角外的海岸，天又亮了。

古人似乎把哈特兰的这份荒瘠、雄强与高峻，奉为一种庄严而独特的圣地。罗马人叫它海克力斯角。我想，他们感觉到，绕过此角，便是驶入另一片海了；因为古人对万物的神秘之感异常敏锐。倘若他们这样看待哈特兰，视它为一条界线，他们是对的。哈特兰以内，你在英格兰的羽翼之下，在居家过日子的水里；哈特兰以外，整个西方在你面前展开，是大洋的威力。那天早晨我们正是如此。风息方止，便涌来一列缓慢的巨浪，一阵接一阵，渐渐规律而分明，到末了，平滑的海面——只被几缕微风在这里那里吹起细纹——化作一道道巨幅的褶皱奔流——平行、规律、不休、浩大。我说，那片海整个是怪的，我在它上面感到过魔咒。

长长的涌浪从西南方滚滚而来，天却几乎是死的平静。风小得只够船掌住舵——幸而有这一点，若是完全无风，她便会横着身随浪打滚，带着她那根过高的中桅把自己颠坏，就像早先在白马急流那天那样。涌浪一波接一波地来；不是哪场大风剩下的余澜（哪里也没有刮过大风），而是那无数神秘的、共同造就了大海的影响之一所掀起的。它们一座座隆起，遮没地平线；我们登上浪峰，又跌进波谷；它们威严地滚滚向前，去轰击哈特兰蛮荒的壮丽。

===Original===

Our time has discovered so many things in the material world (and has grown paralysed to so many other more important things) that we profess to explain everything with perfect links of cause and effect. It is an occasion to use the phrase “We miserable sinners.” For we shall never know anything at all compared with the unknown. What makes such waves?
For that matter, why is one sea so different from another? You measure the amount of salt, the weight of the water, the strength of the wind. Though you had all the factors we can measure, these three and a score of others (the pressure of the air, its wetness, its heat, and so on), yet you could never say why the difference between one sea and another appears. All measurable factors might be the same, and yet the seas quite different. You can, indeed, hardly describe that difference any more than you can describe the difference between two instruments of music, the flute and the trumpet; or the difference between two materials in portraiture, marble and bronze. We only know that it is there. But our stupidly proud time misses such essential categories.
Why is it that what they call “The New World” is something utterly different in quality, taste, intimate soul, from the old? We have here not only the conflict between England and America, but between Spain and the Argentine, and between England herself and the white colonies, and between the French and the French Canadians. It is not newness; it is not lack of tradition; it is difference of timbre﻿—as violent as the difference between a railway whistle and the note of a thrush, or between wood and iron.
Modern men knowing a dozen out of a million causes reason downwards from the rules they have, instead of trusting their perceptions as their fathers did.
I take it that the great differentiations in human history proceed from these uncharted depths of cause, and that one civilisation splits apart from another through stirrings in the very root of the soul. Meanwhile, men prophesy and the future makes fools of them.
The great administrators of wholly foreign lands are men who have not pretended to understand these differences, or to combat them, still less to suppress them. They are men who have recognised the alien blood and, as it were, entered into it. That is government; that is Empire: to get into the shoes of the governed.

===Chinese===

我们的时代在物质世界里发现了这么多东西（又对那么多更重要的东西变得麻木不仁），以致我们自称能用一环扣一环的因果关系解释一切。这正是用得上“我们这些可怜的罪人”这句话的场合。因为同未知相比，我们将永远一无所知。这样的浪是什么造成的？

再说，为什么这片海同那片海这样不同？你量出盐分、水的重量、风的力量。哪怕你掌握了我们所能测量的一切因子——这三个外加二十个（气压、湿度、气温等等）——你也永远说不出海与海的差别从何而来。一切可测的因子可以完全相同，海却截然两样。的确，这种差别你几乎无从描述，正如你无从描述两件乐器——长笛与喇叭——的差别；或两种造像的材料——大理石与青铜——的差别。我们只知道它在那里。可我们这个愚蠢而自负的时代，偏偏错过了这类本质的范畴。

为什么他们所谓的“新世界”在品质、滋味、内在灵魂上同旧世界全然两样？这里不仅有英格兰与美国的对立，还有西班牙与阿根廷的对立，英格兰与她的白人殖民地的对立，法兰西与法裔加拿大人的对立。不是新，不是没有传统，是音色的不同——强烈得如同火车汽笛与画眉鸟啼之间的不同，木头与铁之间的不同。

现代人知道了百万个缘由中的一打，便从手头的规则向下推理，却不肯像父辈那样信自己的知觉。

我认为，人类历史上的大分化，正发源于这些未经测绘的因果深水；一种文明与另一种文明之所以裂开，靠的是灵魂根柢里的搅动。与此同时，人们作着预言，而未来叫他们出丑。

治理全然异域的那些伟大的行政者，正是些不曾装作懂得这些差异、不曾与之对抗、更不曾压制它们的人。他们是认出了异族之血、并且可以说是投身其中的人。那才是治理；那才是帝国：站进被治理者的鞋里。

===Original===

In the strange, successful, perhaps ephemeral, adventure which has put our high European culture in command over the lives of others in the East and in Africa, not a few men have arisen tested by circumstance and conforming to this idea. I have heard with respect the judgment of others upon half a dozen Englishmen, Scotsmen, Irishmen of the sort, though I have not seen their work. Russians have told me of compatriots of their own who have achieved such things beyond the Urals and the Caspian. Of Germans I never heard that any were so much as capable of beginning the great task. That the Spaniards changed half a continent we know. But the only instance that ever came under my own eyes, and that but for a few days, was the instance of Marshal Lyautey. Lyautey’s Morocco is an astonishing affair.
It is true that there lay behind his great achievement more than a lifetime of foundation﻿—the steady French transformation of North Africa. It is like a coral reef, solid, detailed, infinitely particular: the work of innumerable units, sacrificed, obscure, tenacious, creative, permanent. None the less, what Lyautey has done is a building by itself. It is like the new Gothic of Brazenose or Magdalen at Oxford: one may say that there lay behind this also the multitudinous, patient work of a lifetime or more since Pugin began: or it is like Parsons’ turbine, which came after so much experiment, but stands by itself all the same.
It is not belittling Lyautey’s great work in Morocco to say that these things are only possible when there is frankly recognised the essential principle of personal responsibility in governing; or (to put it in Colonial phrasing), “one-man control,” or, to put it in Aristotelian terms, “**Monarchy**.”
There is no other working form of administration over multitudes of men, except the aristocratic; and the aristocratic form of administration﻿—which ruled for nearly three hundred years over Protestant England after the Reformation had destroyed popular monarchy and made wealth supreme﻿—is only possible in those rare communities where men enjoy being governed by a clique and look up to, and revere, a special class whom they think of as their natural masters.

===Chinese===

在那场把我们的欧洲高级文化推上东方与非洲万民之主的奇异、成功、或许也短命的冒险里，不乏经事势锤炼、合乎这一理念而崛起的人。我怀着敬意听过旁人对六七个这类英国人、苏格兰人、爱尔兰人的评断，虽然他们的功业我没有亲眼见过。俄国人对我讲起他们自己的同胞在乌拉尔与里海之外成就的这类事业。德国人，我从未听说有哪一个哪怕配开始这桩大业。西班牙人改变了半块大陆，这我们知道。但真正落在我自己眼里的唯一一例——而且只有寥寥数日——是利奥泰元帅的例子。利奥泰的摩洛哥是一件惊人的事业。

不错，垫在他这桩大成就底下的，不止是一辈子的奠基——法兰西对北非稳步的改造。它像一座珊瑚礁，坚实、细密、无限具体：是无数个体的作品，牺牲、无名、坚韧、创造、恒久。尽管如此，利奥泰所成就的，仍是一座自成一体的建筑。它像牛津布雷泽诺斯或莫德林的新哥特式：你可以说这背后同样有着自普金以来一代人乃至更多年月的繁重而耐心的工作；又像帕森斯的汽轮机，尽管此前有过那么多试验，它本身仍卓然独立。

说这些事业唯有在坦率承认治理中的个人负责这一根本原则时方才可能——用殖民地话说，“一人统制”；用亚里士多德的话说，“君主制”——并不贬损利奥泰在摩洛哥的伟业。

统辖芸芸众生，除了贵族式的政体，再没有别的可行形式；而贵族式政体——宗教改革摧毁了民众君主制、使财富称王之后，它统治新教英格兰将近三百年——也只在那些罕见的共同体里才行得通：那里的人乐于受一个小集团统治，仰慕并敬重一个他们奉为天然主子的特殊阶级。

===Original===

What Marshal Lyautey has done in the organisation of French Morocco has been based upon two foundations which, when they are absent from the effort to govern aliens﻿—as one or both commonly are﻿—make such government increasingly difficult; until, at last, it has to be abandoned. The first of these foundations is *working with* the alien people whom one administrates, not *against* them, nor even *above* them. The second foundation is the deliberate investment of one’s own capital and energy *for the benefit of the alien governed* rather than for one’s own benefit: that is, the development of communications and of building, the extension of instruction, the improvement of waterways, afforestation﻿—all manner of expensive functions which you can only get from a government directly concerned for the good of *all* the people and which will never be undertaken by the private home trader working for his pocket alone.
It is the absence of this sympathy with, and eager help of, the governed which prevents a trading community from ever biting into alien soil. For, in a trading community, the administration of a foreign dependency is directed to helping the home merchant or settler. The road is driven, the river is bridged, not primarily for the benefit of the governed, but primarily for the immediate profit of the governors. With this there goes a contempt of the governed, which is a dangerous sign, and later, that more dangerous sign, a hatred of them.
The foreign rule, therefore, gets more and more distasteful to the governed. For half a lifetime they wonder at it: then they grow restive, finding themselves despised to no purpose. Then they permanently rebel. At last the unnatural tyranny breaks down. When it breaks down the governors gradually depart and little impress of their foreign rule remains.
It was so apparently between Carthage and her dependencies.
But when the governors expend themselves for the good of the governed, an amalgamation takes place between the national spirit of the one and of the other, and the superior trains and shepherds his subjects into a common civilisation. One of the tests of success in alien government is to note whether the governors despise or admire the governed. If they despise them it is proof that they are unfit to govern. If they admire in them what is to be admired, all goes well.

===Chinese===

利奥泰元帅在法兰西摩洛哥的组织工作上所做的一切，立足于两条根基；治理异族的努力一旦缺了它们——往往缺其一，甚至两者皆缺——这种治理便日益困难，直到最后不得不弃守。第一条根基，是同所治理的异族人民一起干，而不是对着他们干，甚至也不是凌驾于他们之上干。第二条根基，是有意把自己的资本与精力投在受治理的异族的利益上，而非自己的利益上：也就是修通路、兴营造、广教育、治水利、植林木——种种花销浩大的事业，只有真心关切全体人民之善的政府才拿得出来，专为自己钱袋打算的本土商人永远不会去办。

正是因为对被治理者缺少这种体恤与热心扶助，一个商业共同体永远咬不进异国的土壤。因为在商业共同体里，对海外属地的治理，是为着帮本土的商人或移民。路修过去，桥架起来，首先不是为被治理者的利益，而是为治理者眼下的进项。与此相随的，是对被治理者的轻蔑——那已是危险的征候；再往后，是更危险的征候：对他们的憎恨。

于是外人的统治在治下之民眼里越来越可憎。有半辈子他们对此纳罕；随后发觉自己白白受人鄙夷，便躁动起来。然后他们永远地反了。到头来，这违悖自然的暴政崩塌。崩塌之时，治理者渐渐散去，外来统治的印记所剩无几。

迦太基同她的属地之间，看来正是如此。

但治理者若肯为被治理者的善而耗尽自己，两造的民族精神便会彼此交融，优者便把属民训练、引领进一种共同的文明。检验异族治理之成败，有一条是看治理者鄙夷还是钦佩被治理者。若他们鄙夷，便证明他们不配治理。若他们钦佩对方身上当钦佩的东西，一切都会顺遂。

===Original===

I found in Morocco that a contempt for Islam (I do not say a difference from Islam, but a *contempt* for it, a contempt which would regard the Moor as a “native” and the French as in some way a “superior race”) was only to be discovered here and there among the worst of the colonists. It was your half-educated, lower-middle-class immigrant who took this attitude and gave all the trouble. The higher you went in the scale, the greater was the liking for the Moor and for his civilisation, the greater the desire to understand him and to know his past, the greater the admiration of his art, his dignity, his faith. When one got to the head of things, in the Residency-General and in the Marshal’s own household, there was an understanding of the whole Moorish past and present like the understanding which a scholar has of his beloved text; it was an intimate and all-pervading familiarity.
I carried away with me from that glimpse of the Atlas and of the treeless run of burnt land beyond (for I travelled while the heat was still upon us) vivid pictures in the mind: first of a vast tract of dead upland, utterly bare, between Oudja and the Moulouya (I had come from Oran); this tract had about it the accursed inhuman effect of the great American deserts in the West of the United States; then, next in order, of Taza on the edge of the hill fighting, and of the story I was told there of two Moorish brothers. One of these would fight under the French for six months and then desert. Thereupon the other brother would enlist in his place. So much was the object of each the pleasure of fighting and the advantage of pay, and so little the political idea of independence.
Nor could anyone forget who has seen it (and it is now become or will soon become, a commonplace of travel) that great new road which I saw in its last making, but which was already nearly completed. It is driven right through Barbary like a stay, like a backbone, from the Bay of Carthage to the river-mouth of Rabat on the Atlantic tide; near one thousand miles in its actual trace, and following the great ride which the first Mohammedans took when they overwhelmed the West﻿—when that horseman rode into the sea surf on the sands by the bar of Rabat River and cried out: “Had you not set bounds, O God, to the earth, with this your sea, I would ride forever westward and continue to conquer in Your Name.”

===Chinese===

我在摩洛哥发现，对伊斯兰的轻蔑（我说的不是与伊斯兰之别，而是对它的轻蔑——一种把摩尔人当“土人”、把法国人当某种“优等种族”的轻蔑）只在最糟糕的那类殖民者中间才见得到，东一处西一处。抱这种态度、惹出一切乱子的，正是那班受过半吊子教育的小市民移民。在阶梯上走得愈高，对摩尔人及其文明的喜爱愈深，愈想了解他、了解他的过去，对他的艺术、他的尊严、他的信仰的钦佩愈甚。到了阶梯顶上，到了总摄政官署和元帅自己的幕府里，对摩尔人古今上下的了解，便如学者之于一册心爱的典籍：是一种亲昵入骨、无处不在的熟稔。

从那对阿特拉斯的一瞥、以及山外那片焦土的不毛奔涌之中（我是趁暑气未消时旅行的），我带走了几幅鲜活的画图：第一幅，是乌季达与穆卢耶河之间一片辽阔的死高原，寸草不生——它带着美国西部大荒漠的那种可诅咒的非人之感（我从奥兰来）；其次，是居于山地战事边缘的塔扎，和我在那里听来的一对摩尔兄弟的故事。兄弟中的一个会在法军旗下打六个月的仗，然后开小差；于是另一个兄弟便顶替他入伍。各自图的主要是打仗的快意和饷银的好处，所谓政治上的独立，在他们是微不足道的。

见过那条大道的人也不会忘记（如今它已成、或不久将成为旅行家的老生常谈）——我见它时正值最后收尾，却已近于竣工。它像一根撑条、一条脊梁，笔直贯穿巴巴里，从迦太基湾直抵大西洋潮头的拉巴特河口；实际路线将近一千英里，循着头一批穆斯林当年席卷西方时走的那条驰道——当年那位骑士策马冲进拉巴特河口的沙间浪沫，高呼：“上帝啊，若非您用这片海给大地设了界限，我将永远向西驰骋，继续奉您的名征服。”

===Original===

A man may take his motorcar on the boat today from Marseilles to Tunis and follow that strict and graded broad way uninterruptedly till he comes to the Atlantic and, turning south to Casablanca, ship it again for Europe.
The surprise of that Moroccan feat was the way in which it was handled during the Great War. When war was already certain in the last days of July, 1914, the Government in Paris, the politicians, sent orders to the Resident-General that all troops should be withdrawn to the coast, the interior to be abandoned, and reconquest (if possible!) to be achieved after the Peace. We who know what those words “after the Peace” have come to mean, know also that it would have been the end of the French in Morocco. It would have been the return to the old pressure upon the Algerian frontier and evidence to all Islam that Europe had failed.
What the soldier did was to maintain his garrisons in spite of the politicians. What was astonishing, he continued the development of Morocco during all those four years of mortal strain, as though the peace had not been broken. Still was the railway laid, and still the new road completed, and still were the buildings set up, and still was the wild hill country contained by its ring of troops; until, when the curtain lifted in ’19, Europe saw, not the Morocco there had been when the Occident entered on its great struggle, but another Morocco, wealthier, stronger and more settled, with great new European towns side by side with the Muslim, and with the whole territory organised.
These new great towns are planned and are built under a military rule﻿—and that is very wise, for the anarchy of individual mercantile effort would have made the experiment break down. No house may be built in the European fashion to jar against the spirit of the place. Everywhere the roofs must be flat and the outline preserved. Also the European town is kept wholly distinct from the Mohammedan. Thus in Meknes you may hear Mass in a chapel which lies within the very thickness of the city wall﻿—it is the old chapel of the Portuguese prisoners during the past of the great Sultans﻿—but you may not enter that chapel from *within* the city; the door faces outward, upon the country side of the wall.

===Chinese===

今天，人可以在马赛把汽车开上船，到突尼斯下船，循着那条严格分级的大道一路不停，直抵大西洋，再折向南到卡萨布兰卡，重新装船回欧洲。

摩洛哥这桩伟业更令人惊异的，是它在世界大战期间的经营。一九一四年七月末，战争已是定局，巴黎的政府、那些政客们，给总摄政官下命令：全部军队撤到海岸，放弃内陆，等“和约之后”再（若是可能！）重新征服。我们这些知道“和约之后”四字后来意味着什么的人，也知道那将是法国人在摩洛哥的末日。那将是阿尔及利亚边境重新吃紧，将是向全体伊斯兰昭示：欧洲失败了。

那位军人所做的，是顶住政客，保住了他的守备。更惊人的是，在四年生死存亡的苦撑里，他照旧经营摩洛哥，仿佛和平从未破裂。铁路照铺，新路照成，楼宇照建，莽荒的山区照旧被驻军之环圈住；直到一九一九年大幕掀起，欧洲看见的不是西方投入大搏斗之前的那个摩洛哥，而是另一个摩洛哥：更富、更强、更安堵，崭新的欧洲大城与穆斯林旧城并肩而立，全境井然有法。

这些新的大城是在军事管制下规划营造的——这很明智，因为个体商业经营的无政府早晚会把这场试验断送。谁也不许盖欧洲式的房屋来刺犯此地的精魂。处处必须平顶，处处必须保全轮廓。而且欧洲城与穆罕默德的城判然两分。于是在梅克内斯，你可以在一座嵌在城墙本体厚度里的小教堂里望弥撒——那是历代伟大素丹年间葡萄牙囚徒的旧教堂——但你不得从城内进入它；门开向外侧，朝着城墙的乡野一面。

===Original===

Upon the day before I left Morocco, a soldier who was driving me upon the heights above one of the new cities in his car said to me musingly, as we had halted to look down upon the view of mixed Europe and Africa and of all that vast new wealth: “It is just ten years ago that the Sultan of the day, annoyed with his brother, had him devoured by lions.” Those who love contrast will not find it stronger anywhere than in this strange land.
But these distant memories of Morocco go ill with the presence of a Devon sea under the breaking of a chill dawn. Those African pictures in my mind, hot sand and palm, painted by memory in the night, grew pale and disappeared in the northern daylight, as do the coloured flashes of the headland lights with the coming of the day.
There was still no more wind than would just make the *Nona* feel her helm. My companion slept below. The heave of the sea grew less; and then, to my disgust and dread, over the sea, already too hazy (so that the high land some three miles away was like a ghost), a fog came drifting up and enveloped us altogether.
I put her up a point more westerly, so as to be on the safe side, for I did not know what current there might be here, and I had to give the land a good offing. I did not want to come up through the fog in such calm weather to hear, close at hand, the treacherous murmur of slightly moving water upon rocks.
Of all experiences at sea in a small boat, under sail, there is none in these days of steam and petrol more trying (after a certain length of it) than being thus half-becalmed with barely steering-way in a part of the sea where the great ships ply up and down, they themselves independent of the stillness that holds you fast. You can hear (as I did on that morning) the beat of the propellers, miles away or close at hand. You hear the hooting from time to time of a ship’s siren﻿—but there is nothing to tell you where your danger may be, nor upon what line it may be approaching you. Some with more experience could, perhaps, have judged that these occasional sounds were at such and such a distance, each or none of them threatening; but to me it seemed as though every steamer in the Bristol Channel was atop of us.

===Chinese===

我离开摩洛哥的前一天，一位军人开车载我登上一座新城上方的高地；我们停下车，俯瞰欧非杂陈的景色和那一整片崭新的巨富，他若有所思地对我说：“整整十年前，当时的素丹跟他的弟弟怄气，就把他喂了狮子。”爱好对照的人，走遍天下也找不出比这奇异国土上更强烈的对照了。

可这些摩洛哥的遥远记忆，同寒凛破晓下德文的海面放在一起，未免格格不入。我脑海里那些非洲的画图，滚烫的沙与棕榈，是记忆在夜里描画的，在北方的天光里渐渐褪色、消散，正如海角灯号那斑斓的闪光，随着白昼来临而隐没。

风仍然小得只够“诺娜”号觉出舵。同伴在下面睡着。海的涌势渐渐弱了；随后，令我懊恼又心惊的是，海上——本已过分迷蒙（三英里外的高地已像一具鬼影）——竟又漂来一场雾，把我们整个裹住。

我把她再偏西让一个罗经点，求个稳妥，因为这里可能有什么水流我不清楚，必须同陆地拉开足够的距离。我不想在这样无风的天气里顶着雾钻上来，却在近旁听见流水在礁石上轻轻挪动的、奸诈的低语。

在小帆船上、扬着帆，海上的一切经历里——在这汽船与汽油船的时代——没有比这更熬人的了（旷日持久之后）：半死不风地漂着，勉强一点舵速，偏偏又在大船南来北往的航路上，它们自己全不受困住你的这份死寂。你听得见（那天早晨我便听见）螺旋桨的击水声，远在数英里外，或近在咫尺。你时不时听见轮船汽笛的鸣叫——却没有一点东西告诉你，危险在哪里，正沿着哪条线朝你来。经验更足的人，或许判断得出这些断续的声响各在多远、哪个有威胁哪个没有；可在我看来，仿佛布里斯托尔海峡里每一条轮船都压在我们头顶上。

===Original===

I have no doubt that had the fog lifted I should have had nothing in sight but a couple of outward bound tramps halfway to the horizon, and perhaps one small steamer in from the Atlantic seas, and going up to Avonmouth, rather nearer to our shore so as to save the strength of the tide. Indeed, when the fog did lift towards noon, the sea was of that appearance, with not half a dozen craft to be seen, and none near. But in the white, cold blankness of the mist (with nothing whereby to judge save the ear), the rhythmic beating of the blades seemed anywhere close at hand; and if the hooting of the steam whistles was dull and dim, that seemed due not to distance, but to the blanketing of the dense cloud. It rose at last, first in patches and wreaths, then altogether, and a pale sun showed over distant land.
There is one thing that has always puzzled me about the Devon and Cornwall peninsula, and that is why the entry into it, up to quite late in history, seems to have been from the north.
The deep harbours and secure anchorages are today on the south. There is a whole string of them; and it is the south that faces towards the main civilisation of the continent. You have the profound inlet of Falmouth, you have Fowey, you have the miles of inland water at Plymouth, you have the Yealm and Salcombe, and the Dart and Teignmouth. Yet the legends, and even the history of Dumnonia are more concerned with such shallow, small, and difficult places as Saint Ives, Padstow, and Bideford River. The wealth seems to have been more on the north than upon the south, for you will note that the pirate raids principally struck there, and the shrines also, the ancient shrines, are rather on the north than on the south; and on the north also is Tintagel, of which it was said by Tristram: “Blessed be Tintagel and all that dwell therein.” The relics of the saints were established in the northern harbours. The seats of government were on the north: there, in the north, is the story of Arthur; and when Cornwall communicates with Brittany in the early Dark Ages, men seem to sail round the end of the land﻿—though the southern harbours are there, pointing right at the further shore.
Did that peninsula swing as on a pivot axis? Did it deepen to the south during the long, hardly-recorded ages?

---

===Chinese===

我不怀疑，倘若雾散了，我望见的不过是一两条出港的货船，在半路到地平线的地方，也许还有一条从大西洋进来的小轮船，正上阿冯茅斯去，为省潮力离我们这边岸近些。果然，将近正午雾散时，海面正是这副样子，望得见的船不满六条，没有一条在近处。可是在那又白又冷的茫茫雾中（除了耳朵一无凭据），螺旋桨有节奏的击水声仿佛处处都在近旁；汽笛的鸣声若是发闷发暗，那也不像是距离所致，倒像被浓云捂住了。雾终于散了，先是一团团一缕缕，随后整个揭开，一轮苍白的太阳照在远处的陆地上。

德文与康沃尔这半岛有一件事永远让我纳罕：为什么直到历史上很晚，进入它的门道似乎都在北面。

深水良港与安稳锚地如今都在南面。它们连成一串；而且朝南面对的正是大陆的主要文明。你有法尔茅斯深深的湾澳，有福伊，有普利茅斯数英里的内水，有耶尔姆与索尔科姆，还有达特与廷茅斯。然而杜姆诺尼亚的传说、乃至历史，更牵挂的却是圣艾夫斯、帕德斯托、比迪福德河这类浅、小、难走的去处。财富似乎北面多于南面，因为你会注意到海盗的劫掠主要打在那里；古老的圣祠也多在北面而不在南面；北面还有廷塔杰尔，崔斯坦曾为它祝福：“廷塔杰尔有福了，愿居于其中的都蒙福。”圣徒的圣髑安供在北方的港里。政府的所在也在北面：亚瑟的故事就在北面；当日耳曼人在黑暗时代之初同布列塔尼往来时，人们似乎总是绕行陆地的尽头——虽然南方的港口就在那里，正指着对岸。

莫非那半岛曾像绕着一根枢轴旋转？在漫长而记载寥寥的世纪里，莫非它渐渐向南沉降？

---

===Original===

I knew nothing of these harbours of North Devon and Cornwall, not even by hearsay. I read up diligently in my *West Coast Pilot* that river which seemed the best for entry along these coasts, and hoped to make it before evening. But the wind died down altogether, and we drifted aimlessly enough back upon the flood, then down again on the ebb, through the night, and all through the morning and the noon, and on into the afternoon again of the second day.
It was full evening, and the sun within an hour of its setting, the air much warmer now and the light mellow with a powdery gold, when a strong breeze came up out of the very sun, as it were; out of the glory. The *Nona* heeled to it, and was now to be handled at will, making a good run a little north of west, with the opportunity for beating as we chose. We beat up towards the river-mouth, sure, as we thought, that the wind would hold, and that we could easily run up the stream and so to the town beyond before darkness. What we had not reckoned with was a sheer cliff lying along the river-mouth to the west, overhanging it and cutting off the wind. On the eastern shore was an immense stretch of half-covered sand, bearing a name of disaster, and famous for wreckage. Having lost the wind, we could no longer manoeuvre, and, what was worse in that narrow passage, we found that the tide did not run true to the sea-tide outside.
We had counted upon the flood making; but, whether because there was more water than usual in the river, or from some odd effect of the wind, or because locally it is always so (though the *West Coast Pilot* told us nothing about it, nor the chart either), the river was still running out violently, and there seemed no sign of an upward-going stream before darkness.
I had almost determined to let her drift out again, catch the sea wind when I got beyond the wall of the cliff, find anchoring-ground well offshore, and wait there for the day, when there appeared, coming down the river at great speed, a white motorboat, large and exquisitely apparelled, splendid with burning bronze, regally accoutred in unspotted gleaming mantle of the foam-colour, of the cloud-colour, of the snow-colour, and a line of gold all around.

===Chinese===

北德文与康沃尔的这些港口，我一无所知，连传闻都没有。我捧着《西海岸航路指南》，用功研读那一条河——它似乎是这一带海岸上最宜进口的——指望天黑前赶到。可风完全死了，我们随波逐流，涨潮漂上来，落潮又荡下去，整整一夜，又是一个整上午、整中午，直到第二天下午。

正是黄昏，太阳离落山还有一小时，空气暖了许多，光线带着粉金色的柔熟，这时一股劲风几乎是从太阳里、仿佛是从那片光耀之中刮出来的。“诺娜”号倾身受风，如今可以随意驾驭了，向西略偏北跑出一段好航程，想抢风便随我们挑。我们朝河口抢上去，自以为风一定稳住，天黑前轻易就能溯流而上，直达城边。我们没有料到的是：河口西侧沿着一面陡崖，崖悬在河上，把风遮了个干净。东岸则是一大片半淹的沙滩，顶着个灾祸之名，以船难著称。风一失，我们再无法机动；更糟的是，在那条窄水道里，我们发现潮同外海的海潮并不同步。

我们本指望涨潮；可是——也许因为河里水比平常多，也许因为风的某种怪作用，也许因为当地素来如此（虽然《西海岸航路指南》没告诉我们，海图也没有）——河水仍在汹汹外泄，天黑前毫无上行的迹象。

我几乎已经打定主意，让她再漂出去，越过崖壁后接上海风，到离岸远远的地方找锚地，在那里等天亮；这时河上飞驶下来一艘白色摩托艇，宽大而装束精雅，焕着灼灼的古铜色，披着一尘不染、莹然生辉的斗篷——泡沫色、云色、雪色——周遭还镶着一线金。

===Original===

It was indeed a boat apparelled, not only as to itself, but as to its two humans; and shameful did the *Nona* appear, homely, draggle-tail, sordid, even after so very calm a passage, when contrasted with that dandy of the inland waters. Alas! That dead wealth should dig such gulfs between mortals and that poverty should lessen virtue!
But, to be just, it is not only wealth that breeds these fine sights, it is also a certain temper in the owner. And I knew very well that though I had three million pounds and some odd pence over I should never have a tidy boat. I could not sleep in such a thing.
Anyhow, there she came, thundering down the tide. I had thought she would pass me in her rush of speed, and I held up until she should have done so, for it would have been dangerous to have tried to go about with such a weight of movement coming on us, and our own course half out of control, dependent only upon occasional slight splashes of wind from over the cliff. But the motorboat slowed down, and its owner asked us if we wanted a tow up to the town. We met him with gratitude: he was of that very considerable class known as the Good Rich, with whom are the Penitent Thieves, the Reformed Drunkards, the Sane Professors, the Womanly Furies, and all other candidates for heaven. We threw a line aboard him and made fast, and so went up just as the darkness came, and dropped anchor in the pool under a neat little city, which lived by the traffic of that difficult waterway with its strange tide. But, then, all tides are strange.
My companion and I sat in the hatch that evening under quiet stars, smoking pipes, watching that most pleasant of sights, the home lights of a little harbour town, and their trembling reflections in the water, the slight swaying of the masts of fishing-boats moored by the quay. We wondered together at the way the tide had behaved when it tried to baulk us of our entry, and we decided before we slept that it was better never to reason upon tides, nor even to accept print upon them, but to take them blindly as they came, for “No man living can understand the tides,” said my companion, and, as he said it, he sighed; and he was right.
No man living can understand the tides.

===Chinese===

那真是一条装束齐备的船——不但船本身，连带船上那两个人；相形之下，“诺娜”号显得多么寒碜：家常、邋遢、腌臜，虽然经过这么一趟风平浪静的航行。呜呼！死去的财富竟在凡人之间掘出这样的鸿沟，贫穷竟削减了德行！

不过说句公道话，养成这些好看景象的不单是财富，还有船主的一份脾性。我很清楚，哪怕我有三百万镑外加几个零头，我也绝不会有条整洁的船。在那样的东西里我睡不着。

且不说这个。她来了，顶着潮轰隆隆冲下来。我本想她会从我们身边一冲而过，便稳住等她过去：那样一股运动的重量朝我们压来，而我们自己的航向又半由不得主、只靠崖头偶尔洒下的一点点风，此时掉舵未免危险。可那摩托艇慢了下来，船主问我们要不要拖到城里去。我们满怀感激应了：他属于人数相当可观的那一类——“好心的阔人”，与之并列的还有悔过的盗贼、戒了酒的酒徒、头脑清醒的教授、女人气的泼妇，以及一切候补天堂的人。我们把缆绳抛上他的船系牢，赶在天黑时上了去，在城下的一方锚泊池里落了锚——那是一座整洁的小城，靠这条难走水道同它那怪潮的营生过活。可是话说回来，一切潮都是怪的。

那晚，同伴和我坐在舱口边，头顶静默的星斗，抽着烟斗，看那最悦目的景色：一座小港城镇的万家灯火，和它们在水里摇曳的倒影，泊在码头的渔船桅杆微微地晃。我们一起纳罕方才拦我们进口的那潮的行径，睡前议定：对潮这种东西，永远不要推理，连书上印的也不要全信，只管闭着眼照单全收——“活着的人没有一个能懂潮。”我的同伴说。说这话时，他叹了口气；他说得对。

活着的人没有一个能懂潮。

===Original===

And the mystery of the tides is as good a corrective as one could find to our deadening pride in exact measurements, and to the folly of attempting to base real knowledge upon mere calculation: our pretence to a universal science, and to a modern omniscience upon the Nature of Things.
The bungling of landsmen in the matter of tides begins with Galileo. That worthy had a talent for getting off the rails and he bungled on the tides as he did on comets. Then came Newton. Why does the tide flow every twelve hours on the average, instead of every twenty-four? If it be true, as I suppose it roughly is, that the moon is the main puller of the tides (helped and hindered a little by the sun, and certainly by other forces of which we know nothing), then there ought to be a tide every twenty-four hours. Put a lot of fluid round a solid sphere, and set a pull, working from some point well outside the fluid. It looks as though the fluid would heap up towards the pull. It being discovered that the fluid heaped up not only towards the pull, but also on the other side of the solid sphere, away from the pull, the mind of man could not rest without proclaiming an explanatory dogma, and we were told (by Newton among others) that the pull not only heaped up the fluid towards itself, but also dragged away the earth from the water on the far side. Indeed, Newton worked it out and proved that it was so.
If the phenomenon had been the other way about, if the fluid had behaved as one would expect it to behave, and had heaped up only on the side towards the pull﻿—oh! believe me! a formula would have been discovered to explain *that* with just as much ease as the formula which has been drawn up to explain the real state of affairs.
But this original false dogma is not of very much interest compared with the immense complexity of the tides, and the inability to follow them with any sort of completeness by reason of that complexity.
For the tides behave in the most inexplicable fashion. They follow, of course, certain habits, which we know (from the observation of liquids everywhere) to be in the nature of a liquid in movement. They pile up higher as they rush up funnels. They are checked by ridges, they form eddies﻿—and all the rest of it.

===Chinese===

潮的神秘，是对我们那死气沉沉的自负——自负于精确测量、自负于妄图把真知识建立在纯计算之上、自负于握有一门包罗万象的科学和一种关于万物本性的现代全知——最好的清凉剂，再好不过的清凉剂。

外行在潮上的胡说，从伽利略开始。那位可敬的人物有种脱轨的天才，潮上他弄砸了，彗星上也弄砸了。然后来了牛顿。为什么潮平均每十二小时涨落一次，而不是每二十四小时？倘若属实——我大致猜想是属实的——月亮是牵潮的主力（太阳略帮略阻，此外肯定还有我们一无所知的别种力量），那么潮应当每二十四小时一次。给一个实心球包上一圈流体，再从流体之外远处某点施加一个引力。看起来流体该朝引力那边堆起来。既然发现流体不但朝引力那边堆，也在实心球背向引力的另一边堆，人心便不肯安宁，非要宣布一条解释的教条不可，于是（牛顿也在内）有人告诉我们：引力不但把流体朝自己那边堆，也把地球从远侧的水那边拖开。牛顿当真把它推演出来，证明必定如此。

倘若现象反个方向，倘若流体照人料想的样子行事，只在朝向引力的一边堆起来——哦！请相信我！人们照样会毫不费力地发现一条公式，把那也解释一番，就像眼下为解释实情而立的那条公式一样。

但这头一条虚妄的教条，比起潮的浩大复杂、比起人因这复杂而无法将潮跟到底，实在算不得怎么有趣。

因为潮的行止最不可解。它们当然遵循某些习性——我们从各地对液体的观察知道，这些习性乃运动中的液体之本性。它们涌进漏斗形水道时堆得更高。它们受海岭阻滞，它们形成回流——诸如此类。

===Original===

But they are continually playing anomalous tricks; and anyone who tries to work up a theory of the tides makes a fool of himself: still more does any poor sailing-man do so who uses science to discover what the tide may be doing in a place he has not yet tried. He makes not only a fool of himself, but also, very probably, shipwreck.
