I have been half a night passing Saint Alban’s with a leading wind﻿—light, it is true, but sufficient; and that only just after the neaps, when the flood should be running slack.

===Chinese===

我本打算诅咒那套荒唐的安排：潮水不好好做一个通情达理的人间的潮，六小时这边、六小时那边地跑，而是十二个钟头里有九个钟头从湾的两侧向南跑，只给你留三个钟头绕行。我本打算再添很多。
可我写不下去。让我用一句忠告作结。
永远不要信任任何没有乘十吨以下的小东西绕过波特兰角的人。永远不要让任何人担任于国家紧要的职位，除非他驾着自己的帆、乘十吨以下的东西绕过波特兰角。而最要紧的是，永远不要相信任何自称做过这件事的人——不，哪怕你看见它印在这一页上。
过了波特兰激浪，顺那海岸下行，只再有一片水（因为你会避开香布尔斯礁）是个麻烦，那就是圣奥尔本斯急流。你刚穿过波特兰，几乎不会留意圣奥尔本斯；可你若向西行、圣奥尔本斯先到，你就不只留意一点了。因为沿英格兰一路下行，从北海与海峡过来，你没碰见过这一类东西。东边的一切多少讲道理。海起了，是因为有风；你受阻了，是因为一股已知的潮。可在圣奥尔本斯角外，你闯进了一片没有规矩的水。
你先是因跟不上海的节律而疑心有异。到那时为止你一直走得顺——比如说乘着南风，规规矩矩对每一个浪迎一迎、又在浪谷里荡开；一道道浪脊规律的拍子已成你的同伴——突然，一个不知从哪儿冒出来的小浪在你船腰上跳起来，掴你一记，随即你落进一片没有韵也没有理的乱水里——不危险，但不可容忍地没有下文。你会突然发现舵柄无缘无故地死沉，又突然一点抓握也没有；而与此同时，你那股水流移动的快慢全无准谱。我有过乘引导风花半个夜晚过圣奥尔本斯的事——风轻，不错，但够用；而且恰在小潮之后，涨潮本该跑得很缓。

===Original===

I have watched there in the darkness hour after hour the blacker line of cliffs against the blackness of the sky, and the shore, apparently immovable, refusing to slip by. What is worse, Saint Alban’s Race is a sentient thing. It knows all about you, and whether in its heart it only desires to play, or whether it has a wickeder mind, a mind it certainly has.
Some years ago I was running down this coast with many companions﻿—too many for so small a craft. All was with us: an excellent wind, bright sun, and a clear air. I warned my companions about this mischievous patch (which is also haunted), and I said we would go right outside and cheat it. So we put the bow a point or two off the course we were making, so as to get right out into the open and leave the exasperation of Saint Alban’s inshore in the place to which his bad temper belongs. But not a bit of it. Even as we were looking landward, and laughing to see the tumble of water between us and the cliff, which tumble we thought to have thus escaped, even as we thought we had passed it, *the thing ran at us*.
It came on in a long line of white, just like a lot of dogs running up to play. It was abominably conscious and alive. It had said, “Here is a boat which thinks that, because it has gone outside, it can escape me,” so it galloped up in a rush, and swarmed all around us, and we were in for an hour of it before we got to the regular water beyond.
I know a man who so dislikes this patch of curse upon the sea that he boasts of passing it as of a feat, though, in truth, there is no feat in it at all, but only an annoyance. So much did he pride himself upon the passage of it once that, in coming into harbour, and being asked to write something in the visiting book of the inn, he put down his name, and the date, and this poem:

> “I made my passage through Saint Alban’s Race

And came to anchor in this bloody place.”

===Chinese===

我在黑暗里一个钟头接一个钟头地望着黑色天幕下更黑的崖线，望着那显然纹丝不动的岸不肯滑过去。更糟的是，圣奥尔本斯急流是个有知觉的东西。它对你知根知底；它心里是只想玩耍呢，还是存着更坏的心，说不准，可心，它肯定有一颗。
几年前我带着许多同伴沿这条海岸下行——对这么小的船来说太多了。样样都顺：好风，亮太阳，晴空。我警告过同伴这片促狭的水（它还闹鬼），我说我们径直往外走，骗过它。于是我们把船头偏开航向一两点，好完全出到空阔处，把圣奥尔本斯的恼人留在近岸、留在它坏脾气该待的地方。可一点也不。我们正望着陆那一侧，笑看我们与崖壁之间翻腾的水——我们以为已经逃脱的那片翻腾——正自以为过去了，那东西朝我们扑来。
它排成一道长长的白线压上来，活像一群狗跑上来玩耍。它可憎地有意识、有生命。它说过：“这儿有一条船，以为跑到外头就能躲开我。”于是它疾驰而来，把我们团团围住，我们足足受了一个钟头，才到外面的规矩水里。
我认识一个人，他如此厌恶海上这块被咒之地，竟夸耀驶过它如同夸耀一项功业——尽管实际上其中毫无功业，只有一场烦恼。有一回他对这次通过如此得意，进港后在客栈的旅客留言簿上应邀写点什么，他写下自己的名字、日期，和这首诗：

> “我闯过了圣奥尔本斯急流，

来到这鬼地方抛锚停宿。”

===Original===

The person who owned the inn was very angry on seeing this poem, and asked that it might be rubbed out. This the man of whom I speak very humbly did, and substituted for the offensive couplet a long, long poem in the heroic style, all in rhyming decasyllabic couplets, and iambic at that, which poem is to be read there to this day.
You gentlemen of England﻿—if gentlemen I may still call you﻿—who travel about in mechanical ships as big as a street of houses, know nothing of these things.
But I will tell you one last thing about Saint Alban’s Race. I have said that it is haunted. Well, what do you think of this? As I was passing there once, there came up to me, catching me up, at a pace far faster than the dear *Nona* could ever sail, such a fine rich little boat, with canvas so new and so tight, and so white, and painted and enamelled, and gear well trammelled, and the brasswork shining like the sun, and the cordage new, and varnish upon all the combings, and on the tiller, and at her helm a man dressed as though for Cowes Week in opéra bouffe. She foamed, and was running past in a streak. The day was bright, well past its noon, and the shores of England stood clear all abeam. There was the Wight, with its brilliant chalk cliffs taking the sunlight, and there, very clear, the Hampshire coast, and right at hand, Purbeck: all as neat as a picture. But the man in the exhibition-shop-window boat hailed me, and shouted through a horn, pointing fiercely eastward, as he stood up and steered with his knee, “Are those the Needles?”
Now what could such a portent mean? What was the explanation of that mystery? The sea brings all adventures, but what adventure was this? Whence did this man come? How could any man so lay a course from the Bill eastward without knowing what the Needles were, and without recognising them when he saw them? It is true we were end on; but they are the most conspicuous rocks, by their shape, in all the Channel, and they are as familiar as Piccadilly. Even a man who had never seen them must know by his chart where they lay, and by the obvious, unmistakable, glaring white point of the Island. To ask “Are those the Needles?” was like asking “Is that the Eddystone?” when one had been sailing with a good breeze southward from Plymouth, and perceived a tall tower standing up utterly alone in the midst of the seas, very far from land.

===Chinese===

客栈的主人见了这首诗大为光火，要求把它擦掉。我说的这个人便恭恭敬敬地擦了，换到那对冒犯的对句的位置上，一首英雄体的长而又长的诗，通篇押韵的十音节对句，还是抑扬格的；那首诗至今还在那里供人诵读。
英格兰的诸位绅士——倘若我还可以称你们为绅士的话——你们坐着跟一条街的房屋一样大的机器船四处跑，对这类事一无所知。
但关于圣奥尔本斯急流，我还要告诉你最后一件。我说过它闹鬼。好，你听听这个如何？有一回我经过那里，一条船赶上我、超过我，速度远非亲爱的诺娜号所能及；那是一条精致阔气的小船，帆崭新、绷得紧、白得亮，油漆上釉，索具收拾得妥妥帖帖，铜件亮得像太阳，缆索全新，舱口围板和舵柄上都罩着清漆；舵柄前站着一个人，穿得像来演轻歌剧里的考斯帆船周。船犁着白沫，一溜烟跑过去。那天天气晴朗，午时早过，英格兰的两岸在两舷清清楚楚。那边是怀特岛，白垩的崖壁辉映着阳光；那边，非常清楚，是汉普郡海岸；而就在手边，是珀贝克：一切都像画一样齐整。可那个站在展览橱窗船里的人朝我喊话，举着话筒，狠狠指着东边，站着用膝盖掌舵，问：“那些是尼德尔斯（针岬）吗？”
这样一个怪现象能是什么意思？那桩神秘作何解释？海带来一切奇遇，可这是什么奇遇？这人从哪里来？一个人怎么能从波特兰角向东定下航路，却不知道尼德尔斯是什么，见了又认不出来？不错，我们是端对着它们；可就凭形状，它们是整个海峡里最惹眼的礁石，熟悉得像皮卡迪利。哪怕从没见过它们的人，凭海图也该知道它们在哪，凭岛端那处明白无误、白得刺眼的点也该知道。问“那些是尼德尔斯吗”，就像乘着好风从普利茅斯向南航行，看见一座高塔完全孤零零地立在茫茫海心、离岸极远，却问“那是埃迪斯通灯塔吗？”

===Original===

This man could not have crossed the Atlantic (though men have done so in boats no larger), for she was spick and span. And even had he crossed the Atlantic, he would have a chart. Was he, perhaps, a Western man, who had lived all his life as in Devon or Cornwall, and had set out thus in middle age to explore the strange Eastern people beyond Portland Bill?
There may be some few such remaining who wisely root themselves in their native place, and such a man might for so short a course take no chart﻿—in which case he would have only himself to blame for running through the Shambles, and drowning if God so willed. At any rate, there he was, asking poignantly whether, indeed, those were the Needles.
For a moment I hesitated whether I should not tell him it was the Old Man of Hoy or the Giant’s Causeway, but the spirit of truth entered into me, and I answered, “Yes.”
He remains a problem, and to me, therefore, fascinating; for there is nothing so holds the mind﻿—my mind at least﻿—as a problem connected with reality. I know that many men get an equal pleasure out of problems set them in fiction; that is why a good detective story very properly commands a large sale, especially among the intelligent: that is why a good detective story is bought by men as well as by women, whereas your ordinary bestseller, so full of what they call “psychology,” is fed to the other sex, not mine. Bismarck also, intelligent among the Germans, delighted in the detective stories of fiction; but, for my part, though I take pleasure in them also (especially when they are of first-rate construction and diction, like *Trent’s Last Case*, Mr. Bentley’s book, which is, without a doubt, the best detective story in English today), yet it is a pale pursuit compared with problems set in the midst of reality: it is all the difference between dreaming and doing. Moreover, the wretched writing man can never forget that a story written is at the author’s pleasure. That author knew the solution from the beginning﻿—as is proper to a creator﻿—and as we read we know that he can make the evidence fit any way he chose. But when you are dealing with reality, the evidence upon either side of the problem is not made to fit any end. You have to reconcile as best you can two opposite sets of equally certain things, which seem the one set to contradict the other.

===Chinese===

这个人不可能横渡过大西洋（虽然确有人乘不比它大的船渡过），因为它浑身上下崭新笔挺。即便渡过大西洋，他也该有海图。莫非他是个西方人，一辈子活在德文或康沃尔那样的地方，人到中年才动身去波特兰角以东探访陌生的东方人群？
这样的人也许还剩几个，他们明智地扎根本乡；这样一个人跑这么短的航程也许不带海图——那样的话，穿香布尔斯礁而亡，若上帝如此安排，只能怪他自己。无论如何，他在那儿，恳切地问：那些究竟是不是尼德尔斯。
有一瞬我犹豫要不要告诉他那是霍伊老人或巨人堤道，但真理之灵进了我的心，我答：“是。”
他至今仍是个谜，因而对我魅力无穷；因为没有什么比一个连着实在的谜更能攫住心智——至少攫住我的心智。我知道许多人从虚构的谜里得到同样的乐趣；所以好的侦探小说理直气壮地大卖，尤其在聪明人中间：所以好的侦探小说男人也买，女人也买，而你们那种塞满所谓“心理学”的寻常畅销书，则只喂给另一个性别，不喂给我这一性。俾斯麦——德国人里的聪明人——也喜欢虚构的侦探故事；至于我，虽然我也从中取乐（尤其当它们结构文采俱一流时，比如本特利先生的《特伦特的最后一案》，无疑是目前英文里最好的侦探小说），可比起设在实在之中的谜，它是一种苍白的消遣：这就是梦与做的分别。何况这可怜的写字人永远忘不了：写下的故事随作者高兴。那位作者从一开始就知道谜底——这是造物者的本分——我们读的时候也知道，他可以让证据随他挑的任何路数凑拢。可你面对实在时，谜两侧的证据不为任何结局而设。你得尽力调和两套同样确凿、彼此看来互相抵触的东西。

===Original===

If the problem as to who that Needles man was, and where he had come from, had involved great issues, racial hatreds, the fate of a fortune, or the destiny of a realm, it would have become an historical problem; and historical problems are the most fascinating of all.
There come first in order those involving human motive, and the known probabilities of human action. Take three of them. The affair of the Diamond Necklace, the Tichborne case, and the Dreyfus case. In each of these the difficulty of finding the solution lies in the discovery of motive, and in the estimate of truth proceeding from a group of witnesses, or sometimes from only one witness, and in each of these there is an opposing set of accounts, and of depositions contradictory the one to the other.
In the affair of the Diamond Necklace, you have either to believe that a man, living in the heart of the court life of Versailles, accepted a ludicrous signature as that of the Queen; or else you have to believe that about twenty people, who had no connection one with the other, and were separated by every conceivable kind of social circumstance, place and time, conspired to tell unshaken the same lie. If you ask whether a man living in the heart of the rich society of London would accept as normal the signature “The Hon. Henry Hound, Esq.” at the end of a letter (he having known for many years the said Henry Hound, let alone the fantastic nature of such a signature) you would certainly say that the thing was morally impossible. But what if you had to decide between such a moral impossibility and the necessity of believing that a railway porter at North Berwick, a small farmer in County Clare, a dilettante friend of the said Henry Hound, and Henry Hound himself, and a chance taxi-man who had once driven him, and a policeman who had noted the taxi go by, and about a dozen others equally separate, did all swear and remain unshaken under examination, and after a long process of time, to testimonies which, combined, made the conclusion inevitable that this man, accustomed to all our social usages, and living in the heart of our rich society, did regard such an absurd signature as normal?
The Tichborne case depends upon contradictions in which I have little reading; but, if I remember right, the main contrast in it lay between two sets of undoubted facts.

===Chinese===

倘若“尼德尔斯人是谁、从何处来”这个谜牵涉重大利害——种族的仇恨、一笔财产的归属、一个王国的命运——它就成了历史之谜；而历史之谜是一切谜中最迷人的。
头一类是那些牵涉人的动机、牵涉人类行动之已知或然率的。举三件：钻石项链事件、蒂奇伯恩案、德雷福斯案。三件之中，求解之难都在于动机的查明，在于从一组证人——有时只从一个证人——口中估量真伪；三件之中都各有一套针锋相对的陈述、彼此矛盾的证词。
在钻石项链事件里，你要么相信一个生活在凡尔赛宫廷生活中心的男人，把一个荒唐的签名当成了王后的；要么相信大约二十个彼此毫无干系、被一切想得到的社会境况、地点与时间隔开的人，串通起来在盘诘之下、在长年累月之后不动摇地说同一个谎。你若问：一个生活在伦敦富庶社会中心的人，会不会把信末“尊敬的亨利·豪恩德绅士”这样的签名当作正常（他认识这位亨利·豪恩德已有多年，更别提这种签名本身的荒唐）？你必定说这在情理上不可能。可倘若你必须在这样的情理不可能，与另一个不得不信之间作扶择——相信北贝里克的一个铁路搬运工、克莱尔郡的一个小农、那位亨利·豪恩德的文人朋友、亨利·豪恩德本人、一个偶然载过他的出租司机、一个记下那辆车驶过的警察，还有大约一打同样各不相干的人，全都起誓，而且在盘问之下、在长久的岁月之后毫不动摇，证词合起来必然推出：这个对我们的一切社会惯例烂熟、生活在我们富庶社会中心的人，确实把那样一个荒唐签名当作正常——你又如何？
蒂奇伯恩案的矛盾，我读书不多；但如果我记得不错，其中的主要对立在两套无可怀疑的事实之间。

===Original===

On the one hand, a man’s mother recognised him; his distinguished advocate preferred ruin to the concealment of his own passionate conviction of the man’s identity﻿—and, after seeing all the evidence, and hearing everything in confidence, deliberately broke the most sacred rules of his lawyers’ trade union, inviting certain disaster in order to leave conviction to posterity. As against a set of things like that you had the unaccountable ignorance of a man of middle age upon habits and words, and the rest, in which his youth must have been steeped, and many other contradictory points as well. There has been formed a comfortable legend upon this particular problem: a legend which affirms the certain justice of the verdict.
Personally, I mistrust such legends. The tendency of men to believe what it makes them happier to believe is so strong that one should lean with all one’s weight against it in public as in personal affairs. But that does not mean that the verdict was not just: it only means that the accepted legend has no authority in itself. During my boyhood the opinion of men amply qualified to judge, men eminent in the legal profession, men of the Tichbornes’ social standing and religion, was as violently divided as were later men of the same capacity for judgment in the matter of the Dreyfus affair.
Though I was too young to remember the heat of the quarrel, yet I always listened with interest to the violent disputes I heard between my elders, especially those which regularly took place in one of the Sussex houses, the squire of which had no doubt at all of the claimant’s right; and my reason for such interest in a matter to me remote is this: that the Tichborne verdict is the first thing I remember. We were living in a house in Westminster, and my nurse, who was excited in the matter, as all folk then were, rich and poor, took me into the press of people at Westminster Hall to hear the result. Of course, very early memories like this (I was but three years old) are falsified by the continual repetition of the subject by one’s elders, but I am as certain as anyone can be of such a memory, and I believe it is the earliest thing of which I have any clear memory left.
Upon a similar very early memory depends one of the longest links with history with which I am acquainted.

===Chinese===

一边是：那人的母亲认出了他；他那位杰出的辩护人宁可身败名裂，也不肯掩下自己对此人身份的炽烈信念——他在看尽一切证据、听取一切机密之后，蓄意破了他律师行会最神圣的行规，自邀必至之祸，只为把信念留给后世。与这样一套事实相对的，是那个中年人对一些习惯、言词等等莫名其妙的无知——他的青年时代必定浸透在其中——以及许许多多别的相抵触之点。关于这个特定的问题，已经形成了一个舒舒服服的传说：一个断言判决必然公正的传说。
我个人不信这类传说。人倾向于相信信了更痛快的东西，这倾向如此之强，以至无论公事私事，都该把全部体重压过去顶住它。但这不等于判决不公正：只等于那个公认的传说本身并无权威。我少年时代，那些充分有资格判断的人的意见——法律界的显赫人物、与蒂奇伯恩家同等社会地位与信仰的人——分歧之烈，一如后来同等判断力的人在德雷福斯案上的分歧。
我年纪太小，不记得那场争吵的热度，可我总是饶有兴味地听长辈们的激烈争论，尤其在一座萨塞克斯宅子里定期发生的那几场，宅子的主人对冒认者的权利毫无怀疑。我之所以对这件与我相距遥远的事怀有兴趣，原因是：蒂奇伯恩案的判决是我记得的第一件事。我们当时住在威斯敏斯特的一所房子里，我的保姆——她像当时所有人一样，无论贫富，为此事兴奋——带我挤进威斯敏斯特大厅的人群里听宣判结果。当然，这样极早的记忆（我那时才三岁）会被长辈们对这件事的反复讲述弄混；但我对这一记忆的确定，已到了人对这类记忆所能达到的极限，而且我相信，这是我留有任何清晰记忆的最早的事。
凭着一桩类似的极早记忆，系着我所知的最长的一环历史链条。

===Original===

Mademoiselle de Montgolfier, the daughter of the man who made the first balloons, lived as a lifelong companion of my Irish grandmother in France, and died well after her ninetieth year, when I was a boy of ten or eleven. That woman as a child of four was present in Paris when the mob poured up the Faubourg of Saint Antoine to the capture of the Bastille in 1789, and I, as a child of seven, eight, and onwards, was brought to her time and again to hear her tell the story. I am now in my fifty-fifth year, and the stretch of time is already remarkable. Were I in extreme old age, and told a child of this incident, that child himself, living to a similar old age, would be able to say that he had spoken to one who had heard of the fall of the Bastille from an eyewitness; and that would be as though some very old person today were to tell one that he had spoken to one who had known a page at Charles II’s Court, and had seen as a child the funeral of General Monk; or, again, it is as though some very old person today were to remember having met in childhood a person who had talked to one who had seen John Milton.
The Dreyfus case, after all these years, still rouses such passions that I tremble even to mention it. I have myself (so many years after!) been subject to mournful rebuke in public from the excellent pen of Dean Inge, who has reproached me in well-knit prose for being the only man in England who did not take the side of the accused. He is wrong there; for I could cite the names of half a dozen prominent Englishmen who had the right to judge through a knowledge of European affairs, and who remained in doubt of Dreyfus’ innocence, notably Lord Russell of Killowen, the Lord Chief Justice, and Mr. Labouchère﻿—and there were many others.
Here also the difficulty lay in weighing opposing sets of undisputable facts. The nearer men were to a position where they could fully judge all the technical details of that highly technical evidence, and the probable motives of the clashing witnesses, the more were they divided; they remain divided today upon the issue after more than thirty years. On the one hand was present an overwhelming motive to secure the conviction of a man against whom there was strong prima facie evidence. That motive was the desire to preserve the Intelligence Department of the French Army intact, though rival powers were bent on its destruction.

===Chinese===

蒙戈尔菲耶小姐——造出首批气球的那人的女儿——终身做我爱尔兰外祖母在法国的女伴，活过了九十好几，我十一二岁上她才去世。她那个女人，四岁时正在巴黎，亲见1789年人群涌上圣安托万郊区、攻取巴士底狱；而我，七岁、八岁以及后来，被一次次领到她跟前，听她讲那个故事。如今我已在第五十五个年头上了，这时间的跨度已经可观。倘若我活到极高的寿数，把这件事讲给一个孩子听，那孩子自己又活到同样高寿，便能说：他同一个听过目击者讲巴士底陷落的人说过话；那就好比今天某位很高龄的人告诉你，他同见过查理二世宫廷侍童、孩提时见过蒙克将军葬礼的人说过话；又好比今天某位很高寿的人记得幼年曾遇见一个同见过约翰·弥尔顿的人谈过话。
德雷福斯案事隔这么多年，仍能激起这样的激情，我甚至提一提它都要发抖。我自己（也是多年之后！）领受过英奇座堂主任牧师那支出色的笔当众的沉痛训斥，他用结实的散文责备我，说我是英格兰唯一不站在被告一边的人。这一点他错了；因为我能举出六七位有资格凭对欧陆事务的了解下判断的显赫英国人的名字，他们都对德雷福斯的清白存疑，尤以首席大法官基洛文的拉塞尔勋爵和拉布谢尔先生为著——此外还有许多别人。
这里的困难也在于掂量针锋相对的两套无可置疑的事实。人们越是接近能够充分判断那高度技术性证据的一切技术细节、以及冲突证人各自的可能动机的位置，分歧越大；三十多年之后，人们在这个问题上仍然分裂。一边是一种势不可挡的动机：要坐实一个表面上证据确凿的人的罪。那动机是保住法国陆军情报部不被拆散的愿望——尽管对立的诸势力正一心要毁掉它。

===Original===

On the other side was another motive: the overwhelming motive of freeing a man who was believed innocent, and the lesser, but still very strong, motive of defending the immunity of that small immensely wealthy, very powerful clique of international financiers, one member of which﻿—the first and the last in our time﻿—had been challenged by the authority of a modern state. Such men regard themselves, and are, in practice, usually treated, as though they were above the law.
I, for my part, pretend to no certain conclusion in the matter, for I doubt whether any man could do that who was not on the bench in court, and physically confronted with the witnesses, and acquainted with all the documents, and able to weigh them all﻿—even the use of the most technical terms in gunnery. But it is to be remembered that there was division upon that bench itself, and that a minority of the five judges﻿—two out of three﻿—were (I am told) for acquittal.
Of my own intimate acquaintance who were on the spot and competent to judge, most were for the innocence of Dreyfus: but the rest, fully competent also, were, and are, convinced of his guilt.
There are in England today two Englishmen whose wide knowledge of Europe and especially of Paris, and the French tongue and society, enable them to judge. They are both close friends of mine. One is for, the other against.
I believe that, when the passions have died down, the Dreyfus case will remain for history very much what the Diamond Necklace has remained, or the Tichborne case; that is, there will be a popular legend, intellectually worth nothing; and, for the historian, the task of criticising that legend, but hardly of solving the problem.
The historian will have one point to make in connection with it, and that a point of capital importance. It is to the Dreyfus case that we owe the four years of war, 1914﻿–﻿18: for it destroyed the French Intelligence Bureau and so permitted the German surprise on Mons and Charleroi.
Perhaps problems depending upon human psychology can never be finally determined. But there are others equally absorbing which involve no such heats, and no such moral doubts: only physical and mathematical oppositions.

===Chinese===

另一边是另一种动机：还一个被认为无辜的人以清白的势不可挡的动机，和一种较小却仍极强的动机：护卫那个规模极小、财富极巨、权势极强的国际金融家小集团的豁免权——它的一个成员——我们时代的第一位也是最后一位——受到了一个现代国家权力的挑战。这类人把自己看作、实际上也通常被看作凌驾于法律之上。
至于我，在此事上不冒充有确定的结论；因为我怀疑，凡不曾坐在法庭审判席上、不曾与证人当面对质、不曾遍览全部文件并有能力一一掂量——包括火炮学里那些最技术性的术语的用法——的人，谁也给不出确定的结论。但应当记住，就在那个审判席上也有分歧，五名法官中的少数派——三比二中的二——（我被告知）是主张无罪的。
在我亲自结识的、身在现场且有判断资格的人里，多数认为德雷福斯无辜：但其余那些同样完全有资格的，过去相信、如今仍相信他有罪。
今天的英格兰有两个人，他们广博的欧洲知识、尤其对巴黎、对法语与法国社会的了解，使他们有资格判断。他们两位都是我的挚友。一位站在他一边，一位反对。
我相信，等激情退去之后，德雷福斯案留给历史的，将同钻石项链事件或蒂奇伯恩案留下的大致一样：就是说，会有一个流行的传说，智识上一钱不值；而留给历史学家的，是批评那个传说的任务，却几乎不是解开问题的任务。
历史学家在此事上将有一点可说，而且是要害的一点：1914至1918那四年战争，正得归咎于德雷福斯案：因为它毁掉了法国情报局，才使德国人在蒙斯与沙勒罗瓦打成了奇袭。
或许，凡系于人类心理的问题永远定不了案。但也有另一些同样迷人的问题，不惹这样的火气，也没有这样的道德疑团：只有物理与数学的对立。

===Original===

It would be a worthy task to draw up a list of such essentially historical problems not involving guesswork upon human motives, but dealing with plain, physical facts; problems which have remained unsolved, and which do not seem to be in the way of being solved either. Your academic writer shirks them nearly always; he writes as though they had been solved, and as though he knew the solution; so that his readers go on imagining they have been given an explanation which as a fact has been carefully avoided.
For instance, when you read of a fleet in antiquity “drawn up” upon a shore, how was it done? The fleets of antiquity preferred to use, but did not depend upon, harbours; they were able to use a beach. Their large armies﻿—for instance, a Carthaginian army of one hundred and fifty thousand men﻿—were landed in this fashion. Caesar landed at Deal his small but respectable force in this fashion, not through a harbour, but on a beach; and civilian sailing and piracy all used the same method. The ship of antiquity could ride, and also be moored in harbour, but was also beached. It came up to a shelving shore *and was pulled up on to the land*. It was pulled up out of reach of the waves; and it will be remembered how Caesar got into trouble during his first expedition (and second!) because he had not allowed for an exceptionally high tide accompanied by a storm. He got his ships on shore all right, but he had not pulled them up far enough.
How was it done? You cannot say it was done by capstans, because that could only apply to beaches already prepared. If you say it was done by hauling upon ropes over rollers the answer is not sufficient. How was the boat kept upright while this was going on, and what numbers would not be required to beach thus even a single ship of three or four hundred tons, and, having so beached it, by what means could one rapidly float it again?
The ancients had some method which they took for granted, for they knew the difficulty of it, and which landsmen had seen practised a thousand times, and yet of which no description has come down to us in our fragmentary records of the past. The poets tell us that the ships stand upon the shore, and they leave it at that.

===Chinese===

开一张单子，列举这类不牵涉对人类动机的猜测、只涉及确凿物理事实的历史之谜——那些一直没有解开、眼下也看不出解法的问题——会是一桩有价值的工作。你们的学院派作者几乎总是回避它们；他写得仿佛它们已经解开了，仿佛他知道谜底；于是他的读者们继续以为得到了一个解释，而那个解释实际上被小心地绕开了。
比如，你读到古代的一支舰队在岸边“拉了上来”，那是怎么做到的？古代的舰队爱用港，却不依赖港；它们能够用海滩。他们的大军——比如一支十五万人的迦太基军队——就是用这个法子登陆的。恺撒在迪尔登陆他那支虽小却像样的部队，用的也是这个法子，不经港口，直接上滩；民间的航行与海盗营生用的也是同一个法子。古代的船能骑在锚上，也能系泊在港里，但也会被拖上滩。它开到一面斜滩跟前，被拉上了陆地。它被拉到浪够不着的地方；人们记得，恺撒头一次远征（第二次也一样！）怎样陷进麻烦，就因为他没有料到一场伴着风暴的异常高潮。他把船弄上了岸，没错，可拉得不够远。
这是怎么做到的？你不能说是用绞盘，因为那只适用于事先备好的滩。你若说是靠垫木上拖拽绳索，这回答也不充分。进行当中船是怎么保持正立的？把哪怕一艘三四百吨的船这样拖上滩，得多少人手？拖上之后，又用什么法子迅速让它再浮起来？
古人有一套他们习以为常的方法——他们知道其中难处——陆居的人也见过它施行千百回，然而在我们残缺的古代记载里，关于它的描述一条也没有传下来。诗人们告诉我们，船立在岸上；到此为止。

===Original===

They seem to tell us also that the boats were helped up stern foremost, but how on earth the thing could be done with a very large armament rapidly, and upon a beach unprepared, baffles us.
There is another problem I have never seen solved. How did Barbarians organise and commissariat the enormous bodies which came across the Alps for the plunder of Italy, and were as a rule destroyed by the Italian armies in antiquity. How did they get such a force as that of Radagasius across the Alps, or that of Brennus? I choose the Alps in particular because it is an extreme case, but it is almost as difficult to understand how they moved across flat country. In the case of the Alps, you had narrow tracks for much more than a day’s march, often for a week’s march; tracks from which it was impossible to deploy to right and left. There must, therefore, have been a very long column in file, a column of many, many miles. It is easy to calculate, in the case of Radagasius, that he could not possibly have extended over less than thirty miles, and probably his column covered a good deal more. Our academic writers seem to think that a body of this sort moves as a matter of course, like an individual, or like a small body which can easily organise its movements: a dozen people out for a walk. But here you have numbers in respect of which anyone with an elementary knowledge of military history, knows that high organisation alone could prevent disaster.
Ask a modern staff with every modern advantage, and even with a good modern road on which the columns could advance on a front of four, to move by a single road a quarter of a million human beings, half of them fighting men, from Augsburg to Milan. Consider the preparation such a march would mean; the exact forethought required, establishing the position of each column at the end of each day’s march; the calculation of provisions, the arrangement for the passing up and down of wagons or of pack animals, the strict discipline necessary to prevent bunching throughout the whole column; the confusion﻿—these and a hundred other points. We all know that very good staff work, taking plenty of time, and with every modern advantage of map and transport, will do it in such and such a fashion.

===Chinese===

他们似乎还告诉我们，船是被尾朝前推上去的；可这么庞大的军械怎么迅速办成、又是在没有准备的滩上办成，把我们难住了。
还有一个我从未见人解出的问题：蛮族是怎样组织、怎样补给那些翻越阿尔卑斯来劫掠意大利、而通常又被古代意大利军队歼灭的庞大人众的。他们怎么把拉达盖苏斯那样一支武力、或布伦努斯那样一支武力弄过阿尔卑斯？我特意选阿尔卑斯，因为那是极端的例子；可他们如何横穿平地，也几乎同样费解。就阿尔卑斯而论，窄径一走远不止一天，常常一走一个星期；窄径两侧无从展开。因此必定是一条极长的纵队，一条绵延许多许多英里的纵队。就拉达盖苏斯而论，容易算出，他的队伍不可能铺开在三十英里以下，多半还要长得多。我们的学院派作者似乎以为，这样一个人数的人群像一个人、或像一小群轻轻松松就能协调行动的人——十来个出门散步的人——那样，想走就走。可这里的人数，任何一个对军事史有起码知识的人都知道，唯有高度的组织才能免于灾祸。
你去问一个拥有现代一切便利的参谋部——甚至有一条良好的现代公路、纵队可以四路并进——请他们经一条单独的公路，把二十五万人、其中一半是战斗人员，从奥格斯堡调到米兰。想想这样一次行军意味着怎样的准备；需要怎样精确的预见，确定每个纵队每天行军结束时的位置；给养的计算，车辆驮兽上下迎让的安排，为防止全纵队拥塞所必需的严格纪律；还有混乱——这些以及一百样别的事。我们都知道，一个非常好的参谋部，时间充裕，又有地图与现代运输的一切便利，能按某种章程办成它。

===Original===

We also know that a few mistakes on the staff might easily so bungle the affair that only a dribble would get across in time, and the bulk would starve and die. Yet these huge barbaric forces, in some undescribed way, so traversed the mountains and debouched upon the plains of Italy, having evidently thought out some form of screen or advance guard﻿—otherwise they would have been blocked on emerging from the pass﻿—having some clearly elaborate system of commissariat, and so on. Not a word of all that has come down to us from antiquity. We do not know how it was done. What is more, we cannot see *how* it was done by men who had no writing, and only the vaguest notions of topography.

---

===Chinese===

我们也都知道，参谋部出几个岔子，就很容易把事情搅得一团糟，结果只有涓滴按期赶到，大队饿死。然而这些巨大的蛮族武力，竟以某种未获记载的方式翻过了山，涌上了意大利的平原，而且显然想出过某种屏护或前卫的形式——否则一出隘口就会被堵住——又有某种显然繁复的给养体系，等等。这一切，古代没有一句话传给我们。我们不知道那是怎么做的。更糟的是，我们看不出没有文字、对地形只有最模糊概念的人，是怎么做成的。

---

===Original===

The harbour of Poole, for which we were making during that run along the Dorset coast when we were hailed by the Needle man, has a problem of the sort attaching to it.
You come round the Anvil Point, and there, if you are lucky, you catch another tide. There are many such points on the coast where you can carry the tide with you much longer than the due six hours or so, by coming into the midst of a fresh tide at the very end of the old one. The main stream seems to shoot past Anvil Point, towards the Island. If you come to Anvil Point and the corner of Dorset just at the very top of the flood and have the good fortune to get round and open Swanage Bay, you are out of this main stream just before the ebb begins, and another, younger flood takes you up past Old Harry and towards Poole.
Old Harry is an isolated chimney of chalk rock which still stands, expecting doom. He had a wife standing by him for centuries﻿—a lesser (but no doubt nobler) pillar. She crashed some years ago and now he is alone. He cannot wish to remain so much longer, staring out to sea without companionship. I think he longs for his release.
Beyond Old Harry you come to that odd entry of Poole, the history of which is, I say, inexplicable to me. It was a main harbour for the Romans; and when the Scandinavian pirates came down to destroy this outer province of Christendom, they used it as a known base. It was sailing out of Poole that a great fleet of their ships﻿—ninety I am glad to say﻿—coming to pillage this Roman land, were piled up and destroyed in Swanage Bay; and it was from Poole that they went up water to Wareham, and thence made their foray by land to Exeter.
Yet Poole, thus easy to the ancients, could never be entered without local knowledge. It is a trap, baited and set. As you look from overseas, there stands clear before you the entry to a great harbour. You see plainly a narrow gate, an obvious door into a wide sheet of sheltered water within. But if you steer for that entry, you strike. For, running down parallel with the coast for more than a mile, is a long, high, hidden sandbank, so that you must get into Poole today by making for the shore more than a mile below the entry to the harbour, and then running up a trench of which you can see nothing from the surface of the sea, but which you must keep to or wreck your ship.

===Chinese===

普尔港——我们在多塞特海岸那一段航行的目的地，也就是被尼德尔斯人喊话的那次——身上正系着这样一桩谜。
你绕过铁砧角，运气好，又接上一股潮。海岸上有许多这样的角：赶在旧潮最末尾进入新潮的中段，你就能把潮带在身上远超该有的六个来钟头。主流似乎擦着铁砧角射向怀特岛。你若恰在涨潮顶点抵达铁砧角与多塞特的拐角，又有福气绕过去、敞开斯沃尼奇湾，那你赶在落潮开始之前就出了这股主流，另一股更年轻的涨水便把你送过老哈里，送往普尔。
老哈里是一根至今还立着的孤零零的白垩岩烟囱，等着末日。他身旁几百年来立着他的妻子——一根较细（但无疑更高贵）的柱子。她几年前坍了，如今他独自一个。他不可能愿意再这么久地孤零零眺望大海。我想他渴望解脱。
过了老哈里，你就到了普尔那处古怪的进口，我说，它的历史让我没法解释。它是罗马人的主要港口；斯堪的纳维亚海盗南下摧毁基督教世界这个外围行省时，把它当作熟知的基地。正是从普尔驶出的一支他们的庞大舰队——九十艘，我很高兴地告诉你——来劫掠这片罗马土地，在斯沃尼奇湾被堆叠摧毁；也正是从普尔，他们溯水而上到韦勒姆，再由陆路奔袭埃克塞特。
然而普尔这样便于古人，却非有本地知识不能进。它是一个下了饵、上了簧的陷阱。你从外海望去，一座大港的入口清清楚楚立在眼前。你分明看见一道窄门，一扇通向里面宽阔静水的明白无误的门。可你朝那入口驶去，就会触礁。因为与海岸平行地、向下延伸一英里多，横着一条又长又高的暗沙脊；所以你今天进普尔，必须朝港入口以下一英里多的岸边去，然后沿一条沟漕上行——那沟漕从海面上一点也看不出来，可你必须贴住它，否则船毁。

===Original===

Today that strange approach is amply buoyed with a bright light at the seaward end, and no one can miss it. But what did the ancients do, who had, it may be presumed, no charts﻿—certainly the pirates had none﻿—and who could not, save in some very special weather, have determined the exact point of entry by the lead? The savages from Norway may have depended upon captured pilots, or upon men who had purchased their knowledge of the fairway by disasters of their own in earlier pirate raids. But we hear nothing of all this in the records. We are simply told they came to Poole continually as though it were as simple a matter as going up Spithead.
Poole harbour has traps within as well as this grinning trap of an entry, and the worst of these traps is the patchiness of the holding-ground. Unless you know where to drop anchor, you may be dragged in Poole, upwards, upon as fierce a tide as I know, with the flukes of your anchor dragging as easily through the soft mud almost as they would through water. But with all that, and although the *Nona* has caught fire there (the sea brings all adventures), Poole is a harbour that will always have good memories for me; and perhaps the *Nona* will go there at last to die.
Now that I am upon the home seas steering eastward out of Poole after rounding that last great buoy of theirs (for I dare not try the Swatchway), I am concerned to defend the proposition that there is more exploration to be done in things familiar than in things unfamiliar.
With what hesitation do I not set down those words! For they are paradox, and paradox is to my personal taste as detestable as advertisement. I will even confess to a little distaste of too much metaphor﻿—or, indeed, of any trick or hook for catching the attention of the reader. Why should I suppose him jaded when I have so many interesting things to say?
But here paradox is unavoidable; for it is clear that in the plain sense of the words the exploration of a place unknown must yield more matter than the exploration of a place familiar. What I mean is that where one knows a thousand things, each of those thousand leads on to another thousand, and so quickly makes you a million. Whereas where you know nothing, you go on from your first discovery to one or two more, and then to ten or a hundred all in a chain.

===Chinese===

如今那古怪的进路浮标密布，海端尽头还有一盏明灯，谁也不会错过。可古人怎么办？他们——可以推定——没有海图；海盗肯定没有；而且除非赶上非常特殊的天色，也无法凭测深锤断定确切的进口点。挪威来的蛮人或许依赖俘虏的引水员，或依赖那些在早年的海盗劫掠中吃过亏、从而用自身的灾难买来航道知识的人。可这一切记载里一个字也没提。只简单告诉我们：他们不断来到普尔，仿佛这事跟上行斯皮特黑德一样简单。
普尔港里面也有陷阱，跟入口这个頀着牙的陷阱一样；其中最糟的是抓底的斑驳不匀。你若不知道该在哪儿下锚，就可能被往上拖——普尔的潮猛得不下于我知道的任何地方——锚爪像穿过水一样轻易地犁过软泥。尽管如此，而且尽管诺娜号在那里起过火（海带来一切奇遇），普尔于我永远是一座留着好记忆的港；也许诺娜号最后会去那里终老。
此刻我在家海上，绕过他们那最后的大浮标、驶出普尔向东（因为我斗胆不敢试斯瓦奇水道），我想为这样一个命题辩护：熟悉之物的探索空间，大于陌生之物。
我写下这几个字时何等踌躇！因为它们是悖论，而悖论之于我的个人口味，可憎不下于广告。我甚至承认，我对过多的隐喻也有几分厌恶——其实，对任何钓取读者注意的钩子和花招都厌恶。我有这么多有意思的事要说，凭什么假定他已经看腻了？
可这里的悖论避不开；因为按字面的本义，未知之地的探索当然比熟悉之地出产更多。我的意思是：在你认识一千件事的地方，这一千件的每一件都引出另外一千件，于是很快让你有了百万。而在你一无所知的地方，你从第一个发现走到一两个、再到十个百个，始终是一条链。

===Original===

So that the exploration of the known is like multiplication, and the exploration of the unknown is like addition. Or again, the survey of the known is like the sowing of a field with a manifold harvest; whereas the surveying of the unknown is but the lengthening out of a trail.
And all this mass of words am I tempted to because I am entering the home seas, which I count from the Anvil to the South Foreland as being my garden or back yard; though it is true that my general estate extends much farther afield. What is more, the home seas themselves expand a little during these last years of too much sailing along the coast of our Great Island, and I am not sure that Portland Bill is not now my gatepost, instead of the Anvil. But all this matters not at all.

===Chinese===

所以，对已知之物的探索像乘法，对未知之物的探索像加法。或者说，对已知的勘察像在一块田里播下多倍的收成；而对未知的勘察不过是把一条踪迹延长。
我说了这么一大堆，全因为我在进入家海——从铁砧角到南福尔兰，我把它算作我的花园、我的后院；尽管我的一般产业延伸得远得多。何况，这些年沿着我们大岛的海岸航行得太多，家海本身也向外扩了一点，我说不准如今波特兰角是不是已经取代铁砧角，成了我的门柱。可这些都全不相干。

===Original===

The Bay between Poole and the Wight is so domestic and inward a thing that many would laugh to hear it called the sea at all. But let them be as I was on that night and they will find it is the sea right enough, and that it can raise a fine hubbub, and that the salt water of it can tumble about with the best of them.
I take it that there is no trial more trying in the sailing of a little craft than taking her through blinding weather at night close inshore﻿—whether that weather be blinding through feather-white slants of snow or through violence of sudden rain.
Well outside you make your course and keep to it indifferently, for you have all the room you need; but when you are running from a coast point to a coast point with the wind on shore through the darkness it is another matter.
The wind that night had backed to the east of south; having so far favoured me, however, that it never backed round enough to make me go about. I was abominably short-handed, and on that particular occasion could only count upon myself, for reasons which I will not debate. My course was for the North Channel under Hurst Castle, where there is a passage so narrow that even the *Nona* could hardly beat through it between the shingle bank and that odd spit of land on which Hurst Castle itself stands, and where Charles, the last true King of England, lay a prisoner after having lost his power. That channel has to be made with skill. You must lie close to the land, but if you lie a thought too close you touch a hump of gravel which lies off the point. Your business in shooting at that mark on such a blinding night is to keep Hurst Light just on your landward bow until you judge that you are perhaps a mile off. Then you must pass it over your bow just to the starboard side, and then, when you are close up against it, so that it seems you might strike almost, you must pay away again, put the light to port, and skirt the land by half a cable or less, and so you will round the long spit of Hurst and find yourself inside the sheltered water of the Solent; all this if you have a leading wind.
At the setting out from Poole you must make a course lying well outside Hengestbury, not going too close inland, for there are shoals and, I think, rocks.

===Chinese===

普尔与怀特岛之间的这片湾，是这样一件家常而内闭的东西，听人叫它“海”，许多人会发笑。可让他们像我那一夜一样试试，他们就会发现它十足是海，会闹起一场好大的喧腾，它的咸水翻腾起来不输给任何海域。
我看，驾驶小船的种种考验里，没有比在令人目盲的天气里贴着岸边夜航更磨人的了——那目盲，或来自羽毛般斜飘的白雪，或来自骤雨的猛烈。
在外海远处，你定下航向漠然守着它就行，因为你有的是余地；可当你在一个海岸点与另一个海岸点之间、顶着向岸风在黑暗里赶路，就是另一回事了。
那夜风已逆转到南偏东；不过它一直照应我，从没转得叫我抢转过头。我的人手少得可怜，而那一回尤其只能指望我自己，原因我不去细说。我的航向是赫斯特城堡下的北水道；那里有一段水道窄得连诺娜号都很难在砾石洲与赫斯特城堡所立的那条古怪沙嘴之间抢过去——最后一位真正的英格兰国王查理，就是在失了权柄之后被囚在那座堡里的。那条水道要凭本事走。你必须贴近陆地，可若贴得近了一丝，就会碰上角外卧着的一堆砾石。在这样一个目盲的夜里朝那目标打，你的活计是：让赫斯特灯恰好在你向陆的船首方向，直到你估摸自己离它约有一英里。然后让它越过船头偏到右舷一侧；等你贴近它了、近得仿佛几乎要撞上，再荡开去，把灯让到左舷，贴着陆地走半链或更少，这样你便绕过赫斯特的长沙嘴，发现自己已在索伦特海峡受庇护的水面之内；这一切的前提是有一股引导风。
出普尔时必须取一条充分绕到亨吉斯特伯里之外的航线，不要太靠内陆，因为那里有浅滩，我想还有礁石。

===Original===

It is only when you have the mass of Hengestbury Head standing up like a sort of dull island against the night and a mile or two north of your beam that you can begin, or a little later, to make the Hurst Light your mark; and it was there on this stormy night that my troubles began; for both the Needles far away a-weather and Hurst itself were continually obscured by the most violent sheets of rain, which came in gusts driven before the wind.
I would not go about into the open and try the Needles channel itself for three reasons: first, that it would have meant a long beat to windward; next, that in the Needles channel I should have had to meet all the outward and inward shipping, which on a night like this might have put me in some danger; and, lastly, that I should have missed my tide and, I think, met the ebb; that is why I made for Hurst and for the narrow passage between that light and the Shingles Bank: a shifting mass of gravel that shows its changing back like a whale above the seas. Now that passage demands skill.
For the first five miles or so beyond Hengestbury Head it was easy going, for after each heavy gust of rain the light appeared again, and I had not veered too much in the interval. (Whether there were an inset to this bay I had forgotten, and I could not leave the tiller to go below and read my directions. But if there were it had not affected me, though we were on the flood.)
As we came nearer to the light the difficulty increased, because it was essential to keep in that direction, and the recurrent blankets of rain blotted out the mark for five minutes and more at a time. Happily, however, the wind, though increasing, went a little more southerly, and left me free to play with the direction. She was a trifle over-canvassed, and was leaning heavily over, stiff boat though she is, and the beam-sea over that low freeboard of hers took me on the face and body time and again with little packets of salt water and spray.
It was important to get through the North Passage with the flood, for the ebb runs furiously through that trench, and I had known half a dozen times the necessity of dropping anchor outside in a seaway if one failed to get through in time, and of waiting through six hours of darkness, during which the wind could not stem the tide.

===Chinese===

只有当亨吉斯特伯里角那团黑影像一座暗岛似的在夜里立起来、位于你船腰以北一两英里时，你才能开始——或稍晚一点——拿赫斯特灯当标志；正是在那里，在那个风雨之夜，我的麻烦开始了；因为远处上风侧的尼德尔斯和赫斯特本身，都不断被一阵阵狂风驱赶而来的暴雨遮没。
我不肯抢转出去走外海、试尼德尔斯水道本身，理由有三：其一，那意味着漫长的一段迎风抢航；其二，在尼德尔斯水道里我得迎会一切进出港的船只，这样的夜里那可能给我带来危险；其三，我会错过潮，而且我想会碰上落潮；所以我奔赫斯特去，奔那盏灯与辛格尔斯沙洲之间的窄水道：那是一大团游移的砾石，把它变幻的脊背像鲸鱼一样露在海上。那条水道要的是真功夫。
过了亨吉斯特伯里角头五英里上下还算好走，因为每阵大雨过后，灯又现出来，间歇里我也没有偏出太多。（这湾里有没有一股内插流，我忘了；我又离不开舵柄下去查我的航行指南。不过就算有，也没影响我，虽然我们在涨水上。）
离灯越近，困难越大，因为必须钉住那个方向，而一阵阵兜头罩下的雨幕会把标志一次抹掉五分多钟。幸而风虽在增，却又偏南了一点，容我在方向上从容回旋。她的帆多出了那么一点，压得她重重倾斜——她本是条硬朗的船——横浪越过她那低矮的干舷，把一小包一小包的咸水和浪花一次次打在我脸上、身上。
赶在涨水上过北水道是关键，因为落潮在那条沟漕里跑得凶猛；我有过半打次这样的经验：一旦没能及时穿过，就只好在外海的浪里抛锚，在六个钟头的黑暗中干等，任风顶不住潮。

===Original===

That night, however, I had good fortune, for there was no rain during the last mile of my approach. I took the *Nona* quite close to the Shingles, and then round through the racing channel into the calmer water beyond.
It was as well; for all that ground of big round stones is bad for holding an anchor. The good holding-ground in this part is round the back of Hurst Spit, in what they call the Roads; and if with your anchor on that bottom the wind rises and it drags, then, whether from the north or from the south, you are done; for you will either come up against Hampshire or break up at your leisure upon the Shingles, which shifts with every gale, and has upon it in the least sea a boiling of white water all around.
In the Solent I carried the flood with me and, at the same time, the wind diminished and all rain ceased, so that when day broke, which it did before I had come near Little Yarmouth (the stream was then just turning), the first dull light appeared in a watery but open grey sky to the eastward, over the hills of my own county far away.
There was a great number of craft about, for it was the season of the year, and their riding lights swung and twinkled like a dance of stars. They were so beautiful that I forgave the rich their spickness and their spanness, and their great yachts kept like toys. Yet neatness is a virtue of a kind: though the *Nona* will have none of it, but keeps as plain (and dirty) as a fishing-boat, to which sort of ship she is first cousin, but no relation at all to the R.Y.S.
This character of smartness in certain externals is one of the things which separates modern nations most sharply one from another. It is based, of course, upon states of mind, and these in their turn, ultimately, upon religions; but there are cross categories.
If you dig down to the fundamental, the real difference is not so much that one mood goes in everywhere for smartness, and the other allows all things to get dirty and slipshod, as that one mood wants smartness in one kind of thing, and the other wants it in another kind of thing; one mood wants accuracy and the satisfaction of proportion in one set of things, and another wants it in another set of things. The difference to the eye is very striking. I fancy it is the difference which the average casual and rapid observer notes most strongly in travel.

===Chinese===

那一夜我却走了运，因为最后接近的一英里里没有下雨。我把诺娜号带到紧贴辛格尔斯的地方，随即穿过奔流的水道，绕进里面平静些的水面。
这样也好；因为那一带大圆卵石的海底抓不住锚。这一带抓底牢的地方在赫斯特沙嘴背后，在他们称作“锚地”的那片水里；可倘若你的锚下在那样的底上、风起了、锚走了，那么不管风从北还是从南，你都完了：你要么一头拱上汉普郡，要么不慌不忙地在辛格尔斯上解体——那沙洲每一场大风就挪一次窝，哪怕最小的海况，四围也是一片白水沸腾。
进索伦特后我乘着涨水，风同时小了，雨也停了；天亮时——它在我到小雅茅斯之前就亮了（水流恰好正在转向）——第一线钝白的光出现在东面、我家乡郡的远山之上，隔着一片水汪汪却敞开的灰色天空。
四周船极多，因为正是当令的时节，它们的锚灯摇曳闪烁，像一场星舞。它们那样美，使我原谅了富人的崭新锃亮，原谅他们把大游艇当玩具一样收藏。整洁终究也是一种美德：尽管诺娜号不搞这一套，她保持得跟渔船一样朴素（一样脏）——渔船这一类船是她的嫡堂亲，而与皇家游艇中队，她沾都不沾亲。
在外表的某些方面讲究“光鲜”，这种品性是把现代民族彼此分开得最厉害的东西之一。它当然建立在心绪上，而心绪又最终建立在宗教上；但也有些交叉的门类。
你若挖到根本，真正的分别与其说是一种心绪处处追求光鲜、另一种听任一切变脏变邋遢，不如说是：一种心绪要这一类东西里的光鲜，另一种要那一类里的光鲜；一种心绪要这一套事物里的精确与匀称的满足，另一种要另一套里的。眼见的差别非常触目。我想，这正是随便一瞥、行色匆匆的旅行者在旅途中注意得最强烈的差别。

===Original===

Everyone has remarked it in the contrast between Belfast and Dublin, between an English ship and a French one, between a Basque village or road, and a Castilian or Galician one over the boundary: between the Swiss-German railway management and the Italian. I am told, by those familiar with those countries, that there is a similar sharp change when you pass over the border of Finland from Russia.
It is exceedingly difficult in so subtle a matter of human motive to ferret out the root, though obviously that root must lie in the mind, and we have to seek for some mental need which is being satisfied. It is no good, for instance, saying that the Italian railway looks more slipshod than the German-Swiss railway because the Italian is lazy and the German-Swiss is industrious. We all know that both are industrious, and that of the two the Italian is, on the whole, the harder worker. It would not be true to say that Englishmen aboard ship were men of more energy than Frenchmen aboard ship. The whole point of a Frenchman is the intensity of his energy﻿—indeed, its excess﻿—which is what accounts for most of the achievements of the French, and also for all their disasters. What one has to do in examining the thing is to find out what it is which makes the Englishman, say, or the Dutchman or the Prussian uncomfortable in circumstances which he feels to be dirty and slipshod, so that he cannot rest until they have been put right, while the other sort of man is equally uncomfortable in another set of slack circumstances, and cannot rest until they are put right.
What are the two categories?
I say again it is exceedingly difficult to judge, and still more difficult to put into words, but they seem to me in my travels something of this kind. The one set of people feel their whole community to be dependent upon a “gentry,” a class possessed of more than the average of wealth in their society. When people take such a standard, an appetite spreads throughout the whole of the community to reduce all, as far as possible, to the conditions under which a wealthy person would live. While in the other set of people you have a conception that the community must be taken as a whole. Their centre of gravity is necessarily much lower than a gentry: it is lower even than a middle-class. It is the standard of the people.

===Chinese===

贝尔法斯特与都柏林之间、英国船与法国船之间、巴斯克的村庄或道路与界外卡斯蒂利亚或加利西亚的之间、瑞士德语区铁路与意大利铁路之间——这差别人人议论过。熟悉那些国家的人告诉我，从俄国跨过芬兰边界时，也有类似尖锐的一变。
在人心动机这样微妙的事情里挖出根来，极其困难，尽管那根显然必在头脑里，我们必须找出某种正被满足的心理需要。比如，说意大利铁路比德瑞铁路显得邋遢，是因为意大利人懒而德瑞人勤，这毫无用处。我们都知道两者都勤，而且两者之中，总体上更肯下力气的是意大利人。说英国水手在船上比法国水手更有精力，也不真实。法国人的要害正是他精力的强度——实在是过剩——这才解释了法国人大多数的成就，也解释了他们全部的灾祸。考察这件事要做的，是弄清是什么让英国人——或荷兰人、普鲁士人——在一种他觉得又脏又邋遢的境况里浑身不自在，不弄妥帖便不得安生；而另一种人在另一套松弛的境况里同样不自在，不弄妥帖同样不得安生。
这两个门类是什么？
我再说一遍，判断极难，落成文字更难；但照我游历所见，它们大致是这样。一种人觉得他们的整个共同体系于一个“士绅阶层”，一个占有社会中超出平均财富的阶级。人一旦取了这样的标准，一种胃口便弥漫全共同体：把一切尽可能地降到富人的生活条件。而在另一种人那里，你有这样一个观念：共同体必须作为整体来对待。他们的重心必然大大低于士绅阶层：甚至低于中产阶级。那是人民的标准。

===Original===

Thus, in the one sort of community certain forms of decoration in a room, such as having a carpet and curtains, will seem essential. If that standard cannot be maintained, then one must expect degradation and dirt﻿—as in our lodging-houses of London. But in the other sort of community they aim at the cleanliness of a very poor room, of which the floor is bare boards. They wash and scrub the bare boards, as in every cottage of Normandy.
Yet that difference in social ideals is not really the heart of the difference. The heart of the difference lies surely in the relative values which people put upon different human activities. In the one community people will take it for granted that a certain smartness of externals is essential to what they call efficiency: in the other the actual working of the instrument towards its end is alone considered. The impression produced upon the citizen of the one type when he sees untidy accessories, even where these are of no service at all to the end the instrument has to serve, is that such untidiness connotes inefficiency throughout. But the other kind of man disassociates those two ideas, and is contemptuous of anyone who confuses such different things. In Picardy they keep out cows with chance thorns, in England with a three-pound gate. Both keep out cows.
I have had sharp personal experience of the matter in one definite instance, and that is a battery of field artillery. In the ideas of the English service, a French field battery is extremely slack. The harness is dull, often old, and pieced together, sometimes shamefully tied up with string. The uniforms are not over-clean. There is nothing too smart about the pieces themselves, even when they are prepared for a review. The conclusion is immediately arrived at that these foreigners ought not to have guns at all, seeing they do not know how to treat them. It is the same connotation of ideas as makes one think that a man without a collar is poor, though he may be a millionaire who has just taken it off to ease his neck. Now the other commentary is worth remarking, for I have heard it from the lips of many men, and it shows the difference of mood. The smartness of the English batteries I have always heard vastly admired by the French gunners, *but never in connection with gunnery*.

===Chinese===

于是在一种共同体里，屋子里某些形式的装潢，比如地毯和窗帘，会显得必不可少。这标准维持不住，就必定指望堕落与污秽——像我们伦敦的寄宿公寓那样。而在另一种共同体里，人们追求的是一间极穷的屋子的干净，地面是光板。他们冲洗、擦刷那光板，像诺曼底的每一座村舍。
然而社会理想上的这重分别并不是分别的心脏。分别的心脏，当然在于人们给不同的人类活动摆的相对价值上。在一个共同体里，人们想当然认为外表的某种光鲜对他们所谓的效率必不可少；在另一个里，只有工具朝其目标的实际运作被考虑。前一种类型的公民一看见不整洁的配件——哪怕它们对工具要达到的目标毫无用处——所得的印象是：这种不整洁意味着全面的低效。而另一种人把这两样分开来看，并且看不起混淆这两样东西的人。皮卡第人用随手砍的荆棘拦牛，英格兰人用三英镑一扇的栅门拦牛。两头都拦得住牛。
这件事我有一桩确凿的亲历，那就是一个野战炮兵连。按英国军队的观念，法国野炮连松弛之极。挽具晦暗，多半旧了、拼拼补补，有时用绳子捆着，丢人。军服不甚干净。炮本身也没多少讲究，哪怕检阅之前。于是立刻得出结论：这些洋人根本不该有炮，既然他们不知怎么伺候炮。这与另一个联想是同一路：看见一个不戴领子的人就认定他穷，尽管他可能是位刚把领子解下来让脖子松快松快的百万富翁。而另一种评语值得注意，我亲耳从许多人嘴里听过，它显出心绪的分别。英国炮连的光鲜，我总听法国炮兵大大地称赞——但从来不曾联系到炮术。

===Original===

They regard it as an adjunct, as a sort of ornament which a wealthy nation possessed (as England normally is in peace time) of but a small armed force can afford. They think it rather like wearing a top hat, or like a woman wearing jewels; something having no relation whatsoever to the function of a gun and its gunners, which is to deliver projectiles accurately and rapidly rather than to look pretty.
In other words, the French gunner looks at an English battery very much as you or I would look at a good poet who had been something of a dandy. It would never occur to us that his clothes were a function of his poetry, and, on the whole, we would more likely connect the idea of good verse with slackness in clothes than with neatness in clothes.
Still, neatness is a virtue, and an excess of polish, even at sea, must be forgiven.
What is less forgivable in the rich is their contempt for the usage of the sea, and their forgetfulness of its brotherhood. I minded me as I ran down that water under the dawn, of how, when I was still young, I picked up a buoy in Cowes Roads after a long night outside, and in desperate fatigue I made fast to it and slept. I had not slept three hours when I was woken by a man so rich that he must have stolen it. He came up, rowed in a boat﻿—I had almost written “of pure gold,” anyhow all glittering with wealth, and rowed by many serfs in white raiment shining like the sun. He hollered and he swore like Mrs. McKinley in the song, and his face was purple with passion; all because I had picked up a buoy which he had bought and paid for. Now this is not the custom of the sea. One may pick up a buoy at need, though one must let go when they ask you. As for this man, his monstrous great ship soon steamed away down westward, and I sincerely hope that he struck that honest reef, the reef called Calvados, in a fog, making for Deauville, and was drowned.
But of all the picking up of buoys and moorings, the most astonishing cast I ever made was in Orford River. There was a tearing wind upstream, and I was bowling along in front of it with a tall great noisy comber of foam at the bows, and straining my eyes for something to round up to, as it was at Orford that I had planned to lie for the night. There was a crowd of men upon the shore.

===Chinese===

他们把它看作一种附属品，一种装饰，一个富国——英国平时正是这样一种国家——豢养一支不大的军队才养得起的东西。他们觉得那有点像戴大礼帽，或女人戴珠宝；与一门炮及其炮手的功能毫无关系——炮与炮手的功能是又准又快地发射弹丸，而不是好看。
换句话说，法国炮手看英国炮连，很像你我看一位多少算纨绔的好诗人。我们绝不会想到他的衣裳是他诗艺的一部分；而且总体上，我们更可能把好诗的念头与衣着的松弛相连，而非与衣着的整洁相连。
不过，整洁终归是一种美德，过分的抛光，即便在海上，也该原谅。
富人身上更不可原谅的，是他们对海上惯例的轻蔑，是对海上兄弟情谊的忘却。我在那片水下、那片曙光里驶过时，想起我年轻时的一件事：在外海熬了一整夜之后，我在考斯锚地拾起一只浮标，疲惫欲死地系上它睡了。睡了不到三个钟头，一个人把我弄醒，他富成那样，那钱必是偷来的。他驾到，乘一条小艇——我差一点写成“纯金的”——总之浑身闪着财气，由许多奴隶划桨，一袭白衣亮得像太阳。他又吼又骂，活像歌里的麦金莱太太，脸孔气得发紫；全因为我拾了一只他花钱买下的浮标。可这不是海上的规矩。人需要时可以拾一只浮标，只是人家叫你松开时你得松。至于这个人，他那艘巨怪轮船很快便突突地往西去了，我衷心希望他顶着雾去多维尔时，撞上那座老实的礁——名叫卡尔瓦多斯的礁——淹死。
但在一切拾浮标系泊的事里，我干过的最惊人的一手，是在奥福德河。上游刮着扯旗的风，我顶着它前面一路飞奔，船头卷起一大垛喧腾的泡沫，眼睛紧张地搜寻什么可以系靠的东西，因为我原计划在奥福德过夜。岸上有一群人。

===Original===

They shouted to me and pointed, and I took their cries to be an invitation, for there, sure enough, was a little buoy of a fine red colour. My companion put up the helm, but not so abruptly as to lose all way, and, when I came near to the buoy I picked it up and made sure that she would swing round head to wind when she felt the strain of the moorings. Far from it: there was no strain. The little buoy came up as light as a cork, and, as the poet has it, “the waves still broke about the bows and the ship sailed on.” The people on the shore were behaving with frenzy, and even at that distance I gathered that they were threatening us. The more readily, therefore, did I beg my companion to put her right before it, and go upstream into the night as fast as we could. And so we hung until the darkness fell, when we brought up outside Aldborough. Then it was we learned what we had done; for the buoy was a marking-buoy for a race, laid down with the most loving precision, and showing to an inch where the winner should pass. Nor have I since dared to land at Orford.

---

===Chinese===

他们朝我喊、指指点点，我把那喊声当作邀请，因为那里明明白白有一只鲜红色的小浮标。我的同伴把舵转过来，又不至于转得失去船速，等我靠近浮标，我把它拾起，确信她一吃上系泊的力道就会头迎风荡住。远非如此：一点力道也没有。小浮标轻得像软木塞一样浮上来，正如诗人所说，“浪仍旧在船头碎开，船继续前行。”岸上的人们狂乱起来，隔着那么远我也听出他们在威胁我们。于是我更痛快地请同伴把船头正对风，尽快往上游赶进夜色里。我们就这么吊着，直到夜幕落下，才在奥尔德堡外抛锚。这时我们才知道自己干了什么：原来那浮标是一场赛船的标志浮标，以最疼爱的精密度布下，分毫不差地标出领先者该经过的位置。此后我再没敢在奥福德上岸。

---

===Original===

There is no piece of inland water in the world so crowded, so too well known, as this three-spoked lake of the Solent, Spithead, and Southampton Water. There is none upon which so many thousands for a lifetime past have spent their leisure, and none which is thought to be more exhausted in all that can be known of it. But those who think so, neglect the dimension of time. For, though you should know every entry and every sounding, the first hour in every flood when your craft can safely go over the banks, the dredged channel under the Hampshire coast, and Lymington River, and Cowes Roads and Ryde Sands, and Beaulieu and Buckler’s Hard, where I am told they launched great men-of-war, and Portsmouth Harbour right up to Fareham, and the Hamble to its bridge, and all the yard and wharfs of the Test and the Itchen, yet you do not possess that piece of water unless you see moving upon it the fullness of its past. You must see the last tragedy of the Civil Wars: the craft that might have taken Charles away from Southampton to freedom, and his young son cruising with the loyal fleet (which had declared for the King against the oligarchy) cruising just outside the Wight, but unable to save the King at Carisbrooke. You must see that which I shall always regret that I am twenty years too young to have seen﻿—the great ships under full sail making out through Spithead in line for the open water. You must see the pirate boats of a thousand years ago stranded on the Bramble, because they did not know the water, and Alfred’s men capturing them so, and taking them off to Chichester to be hanged: a proper end for all Vikings. And you must see the great fleet of Roman transports coming in by an August night with a comet in the sky, for the recovery of Britain seceded and turned into a separate realm; the German mercenaries landing and pouring up the Winchester road, and meeting their fellow Germans in battle before London, and the Emperor Theodosius riding up Ludgate Hill in triumph after the usurper’s death.

===Chinese===

世界上没有哪片内水像索伦特、斯皮特黑德与南安普敦水道这三叉的湖这样拥挤、这样被了解过头。没有哪片水上，这么多千计的人在过去一辈子又一辈子地消磨闲暇；也没有哪片水，被认为在可知的一切上更被榨尽。可这么想的人忽略了时间的维度。因为，哪怕你认得每一个进口、每一处水深，认得涨潮头一个钟头里你的船能安然越过哪些浅洲，认得汉普郡岸下的疏浚水道、利明顿河、考斯锚地与赖德沙滩、博利厄与巴克勒哈德——听说那里下水过大战舰——还有朴次茅斯港直到法勒姆，直到桥边的汉布尔河，以及特斯特河与伊钦河全部的船坞码头，你仍然不占有那片水，除非你看见它的全部过去在它上面流动。你必须看见内战的最后一场悲剧：那些本可以把查理从南安普敦带往自由的船，和他那年轻的儿子——率着向国王效忠、反对寡头的舰队——巡航在怀特岛外，却救不了卡里斯布鲁克里的国王。你必须看见我永远引憾晚生二十年没赶上的那一幕——大帆船满帆鱼贯驰出斯皮特黑德，去往开阔水面。你必须看见一千年前的海盗船搁浅在布兰布尔浅滩上，因为它们不识水路，阿尔弗雷德的人就这样俘获了它们，押到奇切斯特绞死：维京人个个该得的下场。你还必须看见罗马运输船的庞大船队，在八月的一个夜里、天顶挂着彗星，驶进来收复叛离自立的不列颠；日耳曼雇佣兵登陆，沿温彻斯特大道涌上，在伦敦城外与他们的日耳曼同族兵戎相见，僭主死后，狄奥多西皇帝策马登上卢德门山，凯旋而入。

===Original===

Because of its size and of its security, because of its nearness to Europe, because of its many harbours, and one great harbour, this patch of water has been packed with history as no other in Britain, except London River; and now in our time the great London Dock Strike, more than thirty years ago, has brought Southampton Water back to the mass of modern traffic, and made it the great port of entry from the West.
For it was the great London Dock Strike of ’89 did that, of which you may say, as of all the industrial battles of our time, that the victors lost.
Well do I remember the fevers of that struggle! I was but nineteen years of age; it was my delight to follow the intense passions of the time; and those passions were real. It was before the socialist creed had been captured for sham battle at Westminster. The leaders *did* desire, and *did* think they could achieve an England in which the poor should be poor no longer, and in which there should be sustenance and happiness for all. They *did* still believe the amazing proposition that what they called “the community”﻿—that is, in practice, the politicians﻿—could own all we have and handle it with a superhuman justice. Great God! They believed it!
I well remember that I was myself near to believing it, but I had the excuse of youth. They had also another excuse: not intellectual, indeed, but moral; for, intellectually, the socialist has not a leg to stand on, seeing that his theory is but a piece of arithmetic, and its application, coincidently with honour and freedom, a contradiction in terms. Morally I say, those men, those socialists of ’89, were justified by the imbecility of their opponents.
Nor has that imbecility grown less. The arguments for the capitalist scheme have today, after half a lifetime, the same bewildering fatuity they had then. In the interval the power of combined capital, the control exercised over us by a few exceedingly unworthy men, has multiplied I know not how many fold; but the valueless defence of such a condition goes on. One might imagine, to hear them, that the defenders of capitalism honestly believed their ephemeral and empoisoned chaos to have been present amongst us from the beginning of time, and to be normal to the human race.

===Chinese===

由于它的大小与安稳，由于它贴近欧陆，由于它众多的小港与一座大港，这片水承载的历史之密集，不列颠境内除伦敦河外无出其右；而我们这个时代，三十多年前那场伦敦大码头罢工，又把南安普敦水道带回了现代交通的大流，使它成为西方进来的大门。
因为成就这件事的，正是1889年的伦敦大码头罢工；对于它，也像对我们时代一切产业战役一样，你可以说：胜者败了。
那场斗争的热病我记得多清楚！我才十九岁；追着那个时代的炽烈激情是我的一大乐事；而那些激情是真的。那还是在社会主义信条被捉去威斯敏斯特打假仗之前。领袖们确实渴望、也确实相信他们能造就一个穷人不再穷的英格兰，一个人人有衣食、有幸福的英格兰。他们竟然还相信那个惊人的命题：他们所谓的“共同体”——实际上就是政客们——能拥有我们的一切，并以一种超人的公正支配它。伟大的上帝！他们信！
我记得清楚，我自己也差一点信了，但我有年轻的借口。他们还有另一个借口：不是智识上的，而是道德上的；因为智识上，社会主义者一条腿都站不住，他的理论不过一道算术题，其应用与荣誉和自由并存，是措辞上的自相矛盾。道德上我说，那些人，'89年的社会主义者，是被对手的低能证成了的。
而那种低能并未减少。半辈子之后，资本主义方案的论证今天仍带着当年那副令人目瞪口呆的昏聩。此间，聚合资本的权力、几个极不配的人对我们行使的支配，增长了我不知多少倍；而对这种状态的毫无价值的辩护仍在继续。听他们的口气，你会以为资本主义的辩护士们诚心相信他们那朝生暮死的、下了毒的混沌，开天辟地以来就在我们中间，而且是人类的天经地义。

===Original===

It is no little irony to remember that those who have to state the most contemptible of such arguments in the press are themselves men commonly indifferent, or even hostile, to the interests they are paid to serve. It is not your sudden millionaire out of the gutter who writes in the paper he owns; he could not write ten lines if he tried. It is not your monopolist in ships, who, bleeding his country dry during her life-and-death struggle in the Great War, sets out to prove that his vile wealth is of divine origin. It is not the great bankers, getting their usury out of nothingness, who could put forward that apologetic which is necessary for the continuance of the affair﻿—even for its doubtful and uncertain continuance today. No; it is a scribbling proletariat, insecure and exigent, which regularly turns out column by column daily to explain how right and just it is that he should himself remain insecure and exigent. It is as though the torture were applied to some poor prisoner to compel him to proclaim the beauty of torture.
That old socialist fervour of the great Dock Strike of ’89 and its time is dead: I regret it, for there was something very noble in its folly. A good half of it or more was made up of the love of men, and of a passionate hunger and thirst for justice. There was also in it that Apocalyptic, that Messianic, expectation of a new heaven and a new earth, and of its near approach, which is out of place in men of European blood, for it is not native to them, but it moves them all the same with the flame of its enthusiasm. In those days one paper at least, and that a paper of power in the London press, supported the revolt of the poor. No one has such enthusiasm today.
It was a time when men who have since been caught into the net of professional politics, and have lost their souls, went down to speak fiercely at street corners, and to light in the hearts of broken men the flame of human dignity.
I have seen in those days a young man, the heir to a great fortune (later a minister of sorts) standing under the flaring naphtha lamps of a muddy London evening, calling out the new gospel and the promised land. I remember the eager, stupid, upturned faces of the men and women who had come there from bestial depths of the slums to hear him; to go back into those depths, and there to remain. They lie in those depths today.

===Chinese===

一个小小的讽刺是：在报刊上不得不陈述这类最可鄙论证的人，自己通常对雇他们辩护的利益漠不关心，甚至怀有敌意。在自家的报纸上写文章的，不是你那从阴沟里暴发的百万富翁；他想写也写不出十行。不是你那船业垄断者——他在国家生死存亡的大战里把她榨得出血，如今又来论证他那些肮脏财富源于神授。也不是那些大银行家——他们从虚无里榨取高利——能够端出那一套维系此局所必需的辩护，哪怕为它今天这种可疑而岌岌的延续。不；是一支捉笔的无产阶级，朝不保夕、欲望重重，按日一栏一栏地生产，解释他之继续朝不保夕、欲望重重，是多么正当、多么公道。这就像把刑具加在某个可怜的囚犯身上，逼他亲口宣告刑具之美。
'89年大码头罢工及其年代的那股旧日社会主义热忱死了：我惋惜它，因为它的愚妄里有很高贵的东西。它足足一半以上由对人的爱、由对正义如饥似渴的渴求构成。它里面还有那种天启式的、弥赛亚式的、对新天新地及其迫近的期待——这在欧洲血统的人身上是错位的，因为不是他们的土产，却照样以它热情的火焰打动他们。那些日子里，至少有一家报纸——而且是有实力的伦敦报界的一家——支持穷人的造反。今天谁也没有这样的热忱。
那是一个时代：后来被职业政治的罗网捉住、丢掉了灵魂的人们，那时还走上街角激昂演说，在破产的人们心里点燃人的尊严的火焰。
我在那些日子里见过一个年轻人，一大笔财产的继承人（后来当了某种大臣），站在泥泞的伦敦傍晚一片摇曳的石脑油灯下，喊着新的福音与应许之地。我记得那些从贫民窟的兽性深处赶来听他、随后又回到那深处并留在那里的男女，一张张仰起的、热切而愚蠢的脸。他们今天就躺在那些深处。

===Original===

It is not such a creed that will save them. I remember the great mobs that followed John Burns, and how I myself would go miles through the East End to hear him; and I remember that great whirlpool of men in Trafalgar Square on the most critical day when he and others accepted imprisonment. There is nothing now for which men would act so; no one now has a creed; therefore, I call that time of my youth a better time.
Of all that enthusiasm and conviction nothing came; the mill turned noisily enough, but it ground no corn. Those fevered exaltations were transformed within a few years to a contemptible shrieking and raving called “imperialism,” and that also went its way, and worked itself out to its ignoble end.﻿ ﻿… We survive today to hear a perpetual flattering of America, a perpetual grumbling against the workers, their sullenness, and, overseas, the increasing peril of control over the alien races once so easily governed. The vulgarians who invented this music-hall cry of empire now suffer the vacillation and the increasing peril into which their own base bullyings have led them. They must live a little time longer and dree their weird, and learn what happens to those forms of pride which have not even the merit of dignity.
Of those years, one thing remains: the dock strike made Southampton.
I see the monstrous ships of the Atlantic passage pass up and down the dredged channel to Southampton Water, and, as I look at them earning their millions for the few masters of such machines, I say to myself: “This was the fruit﻿—here is the only fruit﻿—of those nightly enthusiasms by the London Docks, and of the cry for freedom, and of the passionate belief that within a few years after 1889 the poverty of London would have passed into an evil memory.”

---

===Chinese===

能救他们的不是这样的信条。我记得追随约翰·伯恩斯的那些大群人，记得我自己怎样穿过东区走上好几英里去听他；记得那个最要紧的日子，他与同伴们接受监禁时，特拉法加广场上那人潮的巨大漩涡。如今没有任何事物能让人这样行动了；如今谁也没有信条了；所以，我称我青年那个时代为更好的时代。
那一切热忱与信念一无所成；磨坊转得够响，却没有磨出面粉。那些狂热的高扬，几年之内就蜕化成一种称作“帝国主义”的可鄙叫嚣与谵妄；而它也走完了它的路，走到了它不光彩的尽头。……我们活到今天，听见的是对美国的无休止奉承，对工人——对他们的阴沉——的无休止抱怨，还有海外那越来越大的险：对一度轻易统治的异族的驾驭。发明“帝国”这首杂耍场叫喊的暴发户们，如今正受着他们自己的卑劣威吓引来的摇摆与日增的危险。他们还得再活一阵，熬他们的命数，看看连体面之利也没有的种种骄傲会落得什么下场。
那些年留下了一样东西：码头罢工造出了南安普敦。
我看着大西洋航线的巨轮沿疏浚水道上上下下进出南安普敦水道；望着它们为那几个机器主子挣着百万百万的钱，我对自己说：“这就是果实——这就是那些伦敦码头旁夜夜热忱、那声自由的呐喊、那腔炽热的信念——相信1889年之后不出几年，伦敦的贫穷就会化作一段恶梦般的记忆——唯一的果实。”

---

===Original===

I carried on between the forts at Spithead under the new day, with the wind steady from just west of south until I was relieved at the helm, and hour by hour we made for the Looe.
Now, with the Looe Stream, which is the gateway to the Sussex seas, I knew not whether I should take the coast in its order between the Owers and the Forelands, or carry on day and night to the straits. For I have cruised up and down that garden wall of my own land so much that it stands in my mind as a perpetual come and go, sometimes far out into the salt beyond sight of England, sometimes a run in before too much wind, sometimes a beating up for Beachy, sometimes just missing, and at others just making, any one of the impossible entries of my harbours.
For the county of Sussex﻿—and Kent, too, for that matter﻿—up to Folkestone and beyond has had tricks played with it by the Creator of the sea and land. There has been done to it what is often done to a human life﻿—that is, the granting of an insufficient opportunity; opportunity enough to compel the use of a function, not enough opportunity for the fullness of that use. All the harbours are such that they can be made, but only on sufferance; Chichester Harbour (which is also Bosham), Littlehampton, Shoreham, Newhaven, Folkestone in its day, are not to be approached at low tide save for the work of man, and Pevensey is dead, and what was once the shelter of Hastings has been worn down by the seas, the chalk reef which came out like an arm to keep off the southwest weather; nor is the breakwater they have made there sufficient. Newhaven is dredged, indeed, and they have pushed out a great pier of stone into the sea to stop the eastward making of the bank at its mouth, and Folkestone has a roadstead made for it though it is incomplete and open, but in their nature all these harbours are to be used by the favour of the tide, and that is why it is so awkward to be caught in a gale outside while Arundel River, or the Bramber River, is still ebbing out over Littlehampton or Shoreham Bar; for both those bars show out at a low spring tide. Sussex in this is less favoured than the Caux Country of Normandy, which is her opposite number. For Trouville and Dieppe, of course, and Fécamp, and even Saint Valery, a boat like mine can get into at any time.

===Chinese===

新的一天里，我乘着稳稳的南偏西风驶过斯皮特黑德的炮台之间，直到有人来替我掌舵，我们一个钟头一个钟头地奔卢水流道去。
现在到了卢水流道——萨塞克斯诸海的门户——我不知道该把奥厄斯礁与福尔兰诸角之间的海岸按次序走一遍，还是日夜兼程直奔海峡。因为这道我自己土地的花园墙，我上下巡航过太多回，它在我心里成了一场永远的来来去去：有时远远出到望不见英格兰的咸水里，有时趁风没变大之前抢进港，有时朝比奇抢风上行，有时差一点、有时刚好赶进我那些不可能的港口进口中的这一个或那一个。
因为萨塞克斯郡——肯特郡也一样，就此事而论，直到福克斯通及更远——被那造海的造陆的造物主捉弄过。它所遭受的，正是人一生常遭的：给你的机会不够；机会刚好够逼你用上一门本事，却不够这门本事用个尽兴。所有的港口都是那种可以进、却只在潮的恩许下可进的：奇切斯特港（也就是博沙姆）、利特尔汉普顿、肖勒姆、纽黑文、当年的福克斯通，低潮时没有人工就靠不近；佩文西死了；黑斯廷斯昔日的庇护被海磨掉了——那道像手臂一样伸出来挡西南天气的白垩礁——后来人工筑的防波堤也不够用。纽黑文确实疏浚了，他们还向海里推出一道巨大的石堤，堵住口门沙洲的东移；福克斯通有了一个半成而敞开的锚地；但这些港口在本性上都要凭潮的恩宠使用，这就是为什么，当阿伦德尔河或布兰伯河还在利特尔汉普顿或肖勒姆沙洲上往外淌的时候，在外面被大风逮住会那样狼狈；因为那两道沙洲在大低潮时都会露出来。萨塞克斯在这方面不如诺曼底的科乡——那是它隔海的对位。因为特鲁维尔和迪耶普、费康、甚至圣瓦莱里，像我这样的船随时可进。

===Original===

This is for two reasons: first, because the Norman streams do not come from the country beyond the chalk hills as do our Sussex rivers, but are mere brooks, so that there is no weight of water to bring the clay silt down through the harbour-mouths; next, because they are not open to the southwest wind and to the eastward drift.
The Sussex rivers left to themselves get a bar piled up in front of their mouths by the southwest wind and the inshore tide, so that a bank of gravel grows up eastwardly continually in front of them. Thus the mouth of Shoreham river, the Adur, travelled eastward for three miles and more between the Conquest and the civil wars; until at last it came out into the sea right up near Hove. But later a great storm burst through the bank and made the present entry, halfway down.
Exactly the same thing happened with the Ouse. Its bank of shingle crept out eastward until, in the Middle Ages, the entry was at Seaford, and that is why Seaford was called “Sea” Ford, and that is why the French would attack Sea Ford, and that is why, when they so attacked, “this Pelham did repel ’em back aboard,” as you may read in Lewes Church. After that a storm broke through the shingle bank, making a new entry right down to the westward, and so brought into being what is still called the “New Haven.”

---

===Chinese===

这有两个原因：其一，诺曼底的那些水流不像我们萨塞克斯的河那样来自白垩山后头的乡野，只是一些小溪，所以没有大水量把黏土淤泥带下港口口门；其二，它们不朝西南风和向东的漂流敞开。
萨塞克斯的河听其自然，就会被西南风和沿岸潮在口门前堆起一道沙洲，于是一道砾石堤不断朝东在它们面前长起来。肖勒姆河即阿杜尔河的口门，自诺曼征服到内战之间朝东游了三英里还多，直到最后在紧挨霍夫的地方出海。后来一场大风暴冲破沙堤，造出现在那个口门，在半路上。
乌斯河的事一模一样。它的砾石堤朝东爬，中世纪时口门在西福德——所以西福德叫“海”津，所以法国人总攻海津，所以当他们进攻时，“这位佩勒姆把他们打回船上”，这话你在刘易斯教堂里可以读到。此后一场风暴冲破砾石堤，在西边老远开出一个新口门，于是有了至今叫作“新港”的地方。

---

===Original===

I have just said that the Looe Stream is the gate into the Sussex seas; and so it is, unless you go outside those rocks called Owers, which is a long way round: for the Owers run miles out to sea.
The Looe Stream runs like a river in the midst of the sea between the long-concealed flats and little rocks just under water at low tide of Selsea Bill and the reefs of the Owers outside. There is nearly three fathom in it at low spring tides, and the entry is marked by a couple of buoys just as one might mark the entry to a harbour: also, as with the entry to a harbour, you had best take the Looe Stream on the flood. So did we on that morning after the storm make our way against the ebb until we had Selsea Bill close before us, and we caught the flood through the Looe by the afternoon.
There I saw, upon the left, the pole and basket of the Mixen, that sea-mark which is my Sussex gatepost. And from that point, taking a course just south of east, I carried on for Beachy all through the night. The wind held, strangely unchanged, and no more rain fell. It was one of the longest spells of similar weather, exactly suited for my little boat under all plain sail, that I had known. For when the dull morning broke (and it was hazy) she still carried the same wind in her sails.
Indeed, it was so thick that I was a little anxious to catch the chalk precipice against the east and know where I was. There is a little inset making into the Bight of Sussex once one is beyond the Owers, and I might have been carried﻿—especially with some little leeway﻿—inside the point.
When I did see land it was not Beachy at first, but, a good way off on the port bow, a murky gleam, from the Seven Sisters: but then, quickly after, I saw dimly on a grim sky the sharp, perpendicular line of the Head and the stump of the lighthouse at its foot. My course had been well kept: I was heading well outside.

---

===Chinese===

我刚说过，卢水流道是萨塞克斯诸海的大门；确实如此，除非你从叫作奥厄斯的那群礁外头走，那要绕很远：因为奥厄斯礁向海里伸出去好几英里。
卢水流道像一条河在海上流，一边是塞尔西角低潮时刚好没在水下的长暗滩与小礁，一边是外面的奥厄斯礁丛。大低潮时里面也差不多有三英寻深，进口由一对浮标标出，正如人们标出一个港口的进口那样；而且同港口的进口一样，你最好赶在涨水上走卢水。风暴过后的那个上午，我们就这样顶住落潮走到了塞尔西角近前，到下午赶上了穿过卢水的涨潮。
在那里，我望见左舷的米克森标杆与标篮，那座海标是我萨塞克斯的门柱。从那里取正南偏东的航向，我朝比奇整夜行驶。风保持着，出奇地不变，雨也没有再下。那是我记忆中最长的一段同质天气之一，恰好配我这条张满通帆的小船：因为当钝白的清晨破开时（天有薄雾），她的帆里还是那同一阵风。
雾实在厚，我有点急着要望见东面的白垩绝壁、弄清自己的位置。一过奥厄斯，萨塞克斯湾里有一股小小的内插流，我也许已经被带着——尤其再加上一点风压差——进到角的内侧去了。
等我看见陆地时，起初并不是比奇，而是左舷船首老远处、七姐妹崖的一道幽光；紧接着，我在铅灰的天上隐隐看见岬头笔直陡峭的线条和它脚下灯塔的残墩。我的航向保持得好：我正走在足够靠外的位置。

---

===Original===

There is little to tell of that run, for it was uneventful. We had that day, somewhere about the Royal Sovereign Shoals, a dropping of the wind and its rising again from offshore, north of west, but nothing heavy in it, serving us well for our course up-Channel, the weather still too thick to see much of the land between Beachy and Dungeness. It was in full darkness that we made and passed that light and, with the next day, we still carried on to Dover.
I have a grudge against that great harbour of Dover: mainly, I suppose, because I have had bad luck in it, but also because it seems to me so wasted an effort of human energy. It has founded one of our new peerages, and while it was being made there was any amount of political talk about it, and also about the electoral conditions of its town. Those who know assure me that it was of vast service in the war, but I have read recently, I know not on what authority, that the Navy has abandoned it. It must always have been a difficult place to enter, with its two narrow gates and the violent tide across them. There is nearly always a lump outside, where the sea shows its annoyance at having this obstacle thrust into it; and trying to get in with a small boat like mine and in a light wind is a trial. There is peril of missing the entry in the rush of the tide, unless one comes close up to the breakwater-heads. If one does this, one never knows but that some great death-ship, on its way to Calais or Ostend, will leap out suddenly upon one as one opens the harbour. Moreover, there are all manner of regulations which I do not understand, as to where you may drop anchor, and as to where you may not, and once, when I left the boat for some weeks in secure care, I found her on my return completely covered with the soot of His Majesty’s coal.
We put in that day for water and to provision. We stayed but a little while, coming in by the southern and going out by the eastern gate, and passing as we did so that sad wreck of munitions wherein lay, I believe, the bodies of men entombed.
My companion took the helm up past the Foreland in a rather fresher breeze than we had yet had, and with more north in it, and I looked back along our wake and thought how here our time has managed to change too thoroughly what was the most famous sight of western Europe, the entry into England.

===Chinese===

那一程没什么可讲的，因为风平浪静。那天，大约在皇家君主浅滩一带，风落了一阵，又从离岸的西北偏西方向起起来，但不重，正合我们沿海峡上行的航向；天气仍厚，比奇与邓杰内斯之间的陆地看不大清。到天全黑我们才望见并驶过那座灯塔；第二天，我们继续赶往多佛。
我对多佛这座大港有一份积怨：主要我想是因为我在那里倒过霉，但也因为它在我看来是人类精力那样一场被浪费的功夫。它成就了我们的一门新贵族爵位；修它的时候，关于它的政论滔滔不绝，关于它那个镇的选举状况也一样。知情的人向我保证，大战里它帮了大忙；可我最近读到——不知凭的什么权威——海军已经弃用了它。它必定从来都是个难进的港，两道窄门，横着猛烈的潮。门外几乎总有一堆浪，那是海对被塞进这障碍物表示的恼怒；乘我这样的小船、顶着一丝轻风想进去，是一场考验。潮的冲急里有错过进口之虞，除非你贴到防波堤头跟前。可你真贴过去，谁也保不定哪艘开往加来或奥斯坦德的巨大死船，会趁你敞开港口的一瞬猛扑到你身上。何况还有种种我不明白的规矩：哪里可抛锚，哪里不可；有一回我把船留下几星期托人照管，回来发现她从头到尾盖满了国王陛下煤炭的烟灰。
那天我们进去补水上粮。只待了片刻，从南门进，东门出，路过时还经过那艘沉没的军火船的残骸——我相信里面还躺着被封在舱里的尸首。
同伴掌舵驶过福尔兰角，风比先前劲了些，也更偏北；我顺着航迹回望，心想：我们这个时代竟这样彻底地改掉了西欧最著名的一景——进入英格兰的门户。

===Original===

Although the old harbour (which lay between the hills﻿—where the gasworks are today) had long ago gone dry, yet the marks of it and much human ingenuity added, maintained the port of Dover; and through that port went the prodigious traffic which bound this province of Christendom to the rest. Here, throughout the intense activity of the Middle Ages, passed and repassed the blood of the State, and the life running outwards from the heart of Europe at Rome. During all that time, on to days I can myself remember, this main majestic entry into England presented something of the same aspect to the traveller coming in from the sea. There was the town in its cleft; the masts in the little harbour; the castle on its height. Now, to my eye, the huge new haven, with its miles of wall, has changed and spoilt all this.
But, alas, something worse, more dreadful and more definite has come by way of change. Something which cannot be undone and which has subtly stolen their meaning not only from old Dover, but from the great keels of the fleet and its guns.
You may see the memorial of it marked upon the turf: the memorial of the place where the first man (and he a Frenchman), having flown the Channel, landed upon the sacred island, without leave of the sea.
That was a date for you, if you like! That was a year to remember! In history perhaps, many a generation hence, it will be made a dividing point between the old and the new relations of the peoples of Europe.

===Chinese===

旧港（它躺在两山之间——如今的煤气厂那里）虽早已干涸，但它的残迹加上许多人类的巧思，一直维持着多佛港；经由这座港，流动着把基督教世界这个行省与其余部分捆在一起的巨大交通。整个中世纪炽烈的活力里，国家的血脉在这里往返，从欧洲的心脏罗马向外奔涌的生命在这里流过。在那全部岁月里，直到我自己记得的日子，这道进入英格兰的庄严正门，对从海上进来的旅人呈现的始终是大致同一副面貌：山谷夹缝里的小城；小港里的桅樯；高处的城堡。如今在我眼里，这座巨大的新港，连同它几英里的墙，把这一切改变并精蹋尽了。
可是唉，更糟、更可怕、更确定的东西随着变化来了。一样无法挽回的东西，不仅悄悄偷走了旧多佛的意义，也偷走了舰队的大龙骨与它们的大炮的意义。
你可以看见它的纪念碑标在草皮上：纪念第一个飞越海峡的人（而且是个法国人）未经海的许可、降落在这神圣岛屿上的地点。
那才是一个日子，如果你要日子的话！那才是一个值得记住的年份！或许，多少代以后，历史会拿它作一个分界点，划分欧洲各民族新旧关系的分界点。

===Original===

It is in the irony of Providence that the more man comes to control the material world about him, the more does he lose control over the effects of his action; and it is when he is remaking the world most speedily that he knows least whither he is driving.
It is absorbing to watch change coming upon a whole civilisation. The thirty years of active manhood, the forty years of consecutive experience and memory which a man of my age can remember, the years 1884﻿–﻿1894﻿–﻿1924 form, perhaps, the best period for watching such things that we Europeans of the West have had since the Renaissance.
That may sound extravagant. The very great change introduced by the use of coal, and the machinery dependent upon coal, one hundred and fifty years ago, produced, I suppose, greater outward effects; but the spiritual fruits of the change were not ripe till our day.
The characteristic of our time is the crisis in the *philosophy* of Western Christendom: the thing began with Voltaire, but it has come to a head somewhat suddenly, following long after the material change.
For the great characteristic of the mental change between, say, 1885 and the present day, is the final segregation of Catholic philosophy from an opponent mood. That is the formula now applying to all the Occident, to all the one group of human beings who are variously called the Spaniards, the French, the Italians, the English, the more southern Germans of the Rhine and the Danube, the Dutch, the Scandinavians, the Irish, and the Scotch.
It is this group of human beings which conducts the world and will probably continue to conduct it; for it has the best physical stock, the greatest energy, the greatest intelligence, and the strongest and most continuous will: and its colonies can never replace it. It has always been at the head of humanity, even before it acquired, through its conversion to Catholicism fifteen hundred years ago, those vast new powers which have since distinguished it. Before Western Europe had opportunities for conducting the world as a whole, it was still manifestly the greatest thing in the world, and when its soldierly adventurers and seamen, and its growing mastery over physical things gave it opportunity it took a place from which I do not think it can be dislodged.

===Chinese===
