Those patriots who rightly think they serve the country by strengthening the authority of even nominal governors, but who are persuaded that the toleration of political vice strengthens such authority, make in this second postulate of theirs a very obvious error. It is an error which they would not make, I think, if they lived the life of their fellow citizens and knew the average Englishmen better than they do.
All politicians live a life apart from the general mass of the less wealthy, less privileged classes, though they themselves are so largely drawn from these strata. It is astonishing when one hears them talking among themselves to discover how remote they are from the general interests of the public, and how seriously they take their own sham divisions and sham battles, and with what strange simplicity they believe that these sham battles are of vast moment to the electorate. The public as a whole is now fully acquainted with the nastiness and degradation of public life.

===Chinese===

一个人会为投政府的票收取金钱或荣衔，但政府成员自己不会惯常地收钱，去便利那些想宰割公众的发起人。偶尔有过这类例子，但不是常态。你想像不出沃波尔，或那个时期另一头的小皮特，会从一个撞上门来的金融冒险家手里收钱——那冒险家想让他添一道新税、免一道旧税、或发明一种新专营，好填满自己的腰包。
我想像不出有人去找小皮特说：“令兄与您同在内阁，我们兄弟有个项目，要给帆船装上某种装置。您若帮我们立个法，规定所有英国船都得装这装置、都得向我们购买，我们就分给您一份收益。”我们现代的政客就是这么干的；而且——对政府的权威或许更糟——他们干的样子让自己滑稽：被揭发的前夜还高声自证清白，永远生活在某种惴惴之中，与那些只要揪住他们这些小习惯不放、就能叫他们生不如死的人进行着一场无休止的角力。
我想，这里就是整桩事情的核心。那些爱国者们——他们以为加强哪怕名义上的执政者的权威就是报效国家，想法本对——却相信容忍政治恶行能加强这种权威；在这个第二前提上，他们犯了一个极明显的错误。倘若他们过一过同胞的日子，多了解一点普通的英国人，我想他们就不会犯这个错。
政客统统过着一种游离于不那么有钱、不那么有特权的广大阶层之外的生活，尽管他们自己多半正是从这些阶层里拔出来的。听他们彼此交谈，你会惊讶地发现他们离公众的普遍利益多么远，他们把自己的假表决、假战斗看得多么郑重，又以怎样古怪的天真相信这些假战斗对选民关系重大。公众整体如今已充分领教公共生活的龌龊与堕落。

===Original===

It certainly does not appreciate the ultimate consequences of these, but at any rate it is no longer under any illusions. Indeed, my experience is that the ordinary member of the public, the average man in the liberal professions and in commerce, rather exaggerates the evil than otherwise, although he does not recognise its danger. He is ready to believe that pretty well any politician may be found taking money, and he does not distinguish as he should between the two sharply different types of Parliamentarians: those with a dirty record of their own, and those with a clean record who permit and support the dirt in the midst of which they live. For it must be admitted that public life is full of men who would never dream of themselves doing such actions as have come to mark their profession as a whole; but these men do not on that account stand excused. They support the nastiness in the midst of which they live; they refuse to correct it; they even, as a rule, join those who would prevent its exposure. They are accessories to all those acts which they not only personally despise and are disgusted with, but which in private they denounce. For they could put an end to the system if they preferred justice to their own little careers and the general good of England to their personal ease or notoriety.
Sometimes they try to salve their consciences by substituting a general for a particular condemnation﻿—the oldest device known to governing man for shirking the duties of government.
I remember an excellent comedy of this sort in which I myself took part. The Admiralty had given an order for the making of an important part of a man-of-war to a small company (what is called a “one-man” company) run for the profit of a friend of the Prime Minister of the day.
There was nothing excessive about that bit of favouritism. It was natural that a politician should help a friend: someone must have the order, and there was no great harm done to the State. But the next step was of real importance.
It turned out that the part supplied was defective. Those who supplied it might plead that they did not know the defect to exist, nor indeed would it be to their advantage to have allowed such a defect. It was probably an accident, though it did point to the work having been given to people inadequate to its gravity. The part was sent back to be put right.

===Chinese===

公众当然掠不出这些事情的最终后果，但至少不再抱幻想。而且我的经验是，公众的普通一员——自由职业与商业中的普通人——多半把这恶估计得过了头，而非不及，尽管他并不认识其危险。他随时相信随便哪个政客都可能被抓到收钱，也不像应该的那样区分两类截然不同的议员：一类自己有脏记录，一类记录干净、却容许并支持自己身处其中的污秽。因为必须承认，公共生活里到处是不肯梦想亲自去做那些已经给整个行当打上印记的事的人；可这些人并不因此得赦。他们支持自己身处其中的污秽；他们拒绝纠正它；他们通常甚至还加入阻挠揭发的一伙。他们是这一切行为的从犯——那些行为他们不但个人鄙夷、憎恶，私下里还痛斥。因为他们本可以终结这套制度，只要他们把正义摆得比自己那点小小前程更高，把英格兰的公益摆得比个人的安逸或名头更高。
有时他们用泛泛的谴责顶替具体的谴责，来安抚良心——这是掌权的人逃避治理职责的最古老的法门。
我记得一出这样的好喜剧，我自己还参与了。海军部把一纸重要舰件订单发给了一家小公司（所谓“一人”公司），它是替当时的首相的一位朋友营利的。
那点偏私本不过分。政客帮朋友一把，情理之中；订单总得有人接，国家也没吃多大的亏。但下一步才真正要紧。
供货的那件舰件原来有缺陷。供货的人可以辩称不知道缺陷存在，而且容许这种缺陷也实在于他们无利。多半是意外，尽管它确实说明活儿交给了担不起其分量的人。舰件被退回返修。

===Original===

Instead of being put right, as it should have been, an operation was performed with the effect of concealing the defect, instead of remedying it. The part was sent back in such a condition that, had the flaw not been discovered, the great man-of-war itself and its crew might have perished. This was obviously a very serious matter indeed. If there is one thing the politicians must not be allowed to play tricks with, it is the Navy.
Now, the Parliamentarians appointed to look into public accounts issued a fine bombastic denunciation of the awful business, and used such words as would have satisfied Jeremiah. *But they were very careful not to mention a single name.*
That seemed to me something of a defect. I was in Parliament at the time, and I set out to find out who were the actual human beings responsible for this crime. Unless one got on to them and punished them, one might as well have done nothing in the matter. If things of this sort can be done with impunity, they will certainly be done again.
I found the greatest difficulty in getting any single one of those six hundred-odd men in the Commons to act with me in the matter. I did not, of course, approach more than a few, because there were not more than a few who would even consider the public good; but by one of those few I was told that it was not usual to act in this fashion; that such action was regarded as “personal”; that the thing was now some months concluded and ought to be buried; that it was difficult to arrive at the ultimate responsibility in these affairs﻿—and so on. They all applied to a principal unit of the British Navy, to an English man-of-war and a thousand English lives, the principle that the politician was more important than ship or crew, and that their own Parliamentary skins were more important than the country.
The individual private member of Parliament has no power of action in Parliament except the chance of the ballot or the putting-down of a question. I put down my question. It was answered in the most pathetic manner by the politician directly responsible, who called Heaven to witness that I had traduced by my question an old and valued friend of his own whom he loved as a brother. That the fault was that of “a subordinate.” The House murmured its sympathy.

===Chinese===

该修的没修，倒是做了一场手术，效果是掩盖缺陷，而非消除。舰件被退回时处于这样一种状态：倘若那瑕疵没有被发现，那艘大战舰连同它的舰员可能就毁掉了。这显然是一桩非常严重的事。政客们最不能被允许耍花招的东西，如果还有一样，那就是海军。
于是，受命审查公共账目的议员们对这桩可怕的事发了一通漂亮的浮夸的谴责，用词之重足以让耶利米满意。可他们非常小心，一个名字也不提。
这在我看来未免是个缺陷。我当时在议会里，便着手去查出这桩罪行究竟由哪些活人负责。除非揪住他们、惩办他们，在这件事上等于什么也没做。这类事若能不受惩罚，将来必定重演。
我发现在下议院那六百多人里，极难找到哪怕一个人肯同我一起行动。我当然只找了不多几个，因为肯把公益放在心上的本来就不多几个；而那不多几个里的一位告诉我：照这样的方式行事不合惯例；这种行为被视为“针对个人”；事情已过去几个月，该埋掉了；这类事情很难追到最终责任——等等。他们全都把一条原则用在英国海军的一个主力部件、一艘英国战舰和一千条英国人命上：政客比军舰或舰员重要，他们自己的议会皮比国家重要。
普通的下院议员在议会里没有行动的权力，只有抽签的机会或提出质询。我提出了我的质询。直接负责的那位政客以最动人的方式作了答复：他请上苍作证，说我的质询诽谤了他自己的一位敬爱如手足的老朋友；过错属于“一名下属”。全院低声同情。

===Original===

I pointed out that I had traduced no one, that I had only asked for the names of the responsible people and for their prosecution. Whereupon, as another journalistic phrase goes, “the matter was allowed to drop.” But I need hardly add that the person nominally responsible was promoted to a public honour, and that no “subordinate” appeared.
It was only a small episode, though it would not have seemed small to the officers or crew if their ship had struck in the middle of the night owing to a defect in the steering gear﻿—still, honestly, it was a small matter as politics go. There are hosts of much graver instances, but it was a characteristic one.
The argument that this now universal taint covering public life is tolerable because the same thing goes on in every country where there is a Parliament, and therefore does not weaken us in the face of our rivals, has two bad flaws in it.
The first is a neglect of the fact that the cancer called Parliament is no longer universal. Some of our rivals are already rid of it: all are disgusted with it; most will probably have cut it out within a generation.
The second is that nowhere is Parliament bound up with the Nation as it is in England.
The French parliamentary system is at least as corrupt as the English: from the little I know of it, I should say it was worse. The American, I understand, is not so bad; but it is still sufficiently corrupt to excite the perpetual vigilance of the American electorate and press, which are as ruthless in exposing such evils as we are resolute in concealing them. Of the Polish and German systems, which have just come into existence, I know nothing. The Italian was contemptible, and the Spanish a joke. They have both been happily kicked into the street, and I trust we shall hear of them no more.
But the parliamentary corruption of other countries is attached to an institution which in those countries is subsidiary to the national life. No one in France, for instance, regards the Parliament as a necessary, or even a properly working, part of the national existence; it is a modern experiment, very unpopular, and standing in some dread of far more permanent and deep-rooted institutions, such as the University, the Army, the Church, and the landed system, with which it has to work as best it may.

===Chinese===

我指出我没有诽谤任何人，我只是要求公布责任者的名字并予起诉。于是，用另一句新闻套话说，“此事就此作罢”。但不必说，那位名义上负责的人随后荣升了一个公共荣衔，而任何“下属”都没有露面。
这只是一个小插曲——虽然倘若他们的军舰因转向装置的缺陷在半夜触礁，这在军官或舰员看来不会小——不过，按政治的标准，老实说，这确实是小事。类似的事多得多、严重得多，但这件很有代表性。
说这层如今笼罩公共生活的普染之污可以容忍，因为凡有议会的国家都一样、因而无碍我们面对对手——这个论证有两个大漏洞。
其一，它忽视了这样的事实：名为议会的这块癌瘤已不再普世。我们的对手有的已经摘掉了它；个个都厌恶它；多半会在一代人之内把它割掉。
其二，没有一处，议会像英格兰这样与国家血肉相连。
法国的议会制度至少同英国的一样腐败：凭我对它那一点了解，我还要说更糟。美国的，据我所知没那么坏；但仍腐败得足以让美国的选民和报刊永远警觉，他们揭露这类恶疾的无情，一如我们掩饰它的坚决。波兰与德国刚建立的制度，我一无所知。意大利的曾可鄙，西班牙的是笑话。两台货色都已快活地被踢到街上，但愿我们再不要听说它们。
但别国的议会腐败，挂在一个从属于民族生活的机构上。比如在法国，没有人把议会看作民族生存必要的、甚至正常运转的一部分；它是一场很不得人心的现代实验，还得战战兢兢伺候着一些远更持久、扎根更深的建制——大学、军队、教会、土地制度——勉力与它们共事。

===Original===

The politicians in Paris can and do work great harm in all departments, but they do not form the traditional organ of government, rooted in the country, as the Commons do here. They live under perpetual protest.
In the United States, as everybody knows, the central representative assembly is altogether subordinate. The Senate is far more powerful, and far more respected. The President is high above both. He has monarchic powers superior to anything of the kind in Europe﻿—superior by far to those exercised by the King of Prussia in the days when that individual was also military head of the Reich.
But in England Parliament is the great national institution. It is bound up with the whole life of the country. A disease attacking it is a national disease. The lawyers’ guild, which is in England far, far more powerful than in any other country, is inextricably bound up with Parliament. There can in England never be any question of the armed forces acting apart from Parliament; nor can there be apparently (and unfortunately) as yet a kingly power, like that of the President of the United States. A King of England would today find ample popular support against Parliament; but how is he to move?
Therein lies the gravity of the situation. Parliament will not reform itself, and yet there is no power external to Parliament capable of reforming it. Though the nation were in the last extremity of peril, we have not at hand some force external to Parliament capable of pushing aside its maleficent impotence and taking over the reins. Indeed, when certain politicians were secretly trying to make peace behind the back of the Allies during the crisis of the Great War, no one moved﻿—except a few humble seamen, who stopped one important voyage of their own accord. Should we live to see an invasion of this country, or a shameful capitulation, I fear that the disaster and the shame would still be presided over by the politician. During the Great War we owed half a dozen insanities to the fact that with us a politician can order the movements of armies, in spite of the soldiers, and affect﻿—though he can hardly direct﻿—the foreign policy of the country, in spite of its competent permanent officials.

===Chinese===

巴黎的政客们能够、也确实在各处为害，但他们不像这里的下议院那样，是这个国家扎根的传统的治理机关。他们生活在无休止的抗议之下。
在美国，众所周知，中央代议机关完全居于从属。参议院远更有力、更受尊敬。总统高高在两者之上。他握有的君王般的权力，超过欧洲任何同类——远超过普鲁士国王身兼帝国军事首脑那个年代所行使的。
可在英格兰，议会就是那伟大的国家建制。它与国家的全部生活血肉相连。袭向它的病是一场国病。律师行会——它在英格兰比在任何别国都远远更有力——与议会难解难分。在英格兰永远谈不上武装力量脱离议会单独行动；也显然（不幸地）至今产生不出一个像美国总统那样的王权。英格兰国王今天若与议会作对，会赢得广大的民心；可他怎么动得了手呢？
局势的严重在此。议会不会自我改革，而议会之外又没有一个有能力改造它的力量。哪怕国家陷于最后的绝境，我们手边也没有一个议会之外的力量，能推开它那作恶的无能、接过缰绳。真的，当年大战危机中某些政客背着盟友偷偷求和时，没有一个人动——除了几名低微的海员，他们自发地拦下了一次重要的航程。倘若我们活着看到这个国家遭到入侵、或一场可耻的投降，我恐怕那灾难与耻辱仍将由政客主持。大战期间我们吃的半打疯账，一半是因为在我们这里，政客能违背军人调遣军队的行动，还能——虽谈不上主导——违背称职的常任文官影响这个国家的外交。

===Original===

It is true that the millionaire newspaper-owner, especially if he be devoid of instruction, and therefore of all responsibility, has more to do with the framing of a policy than has the politician, and it is true that the great and weighty financial interests are much more powerful. But Parliament is the door through which everything must pass﻿—or, to change the metaphor, it is the instrument which alone can be used for direct effect; and its decline is on that account of very grave moment to the future of the country. It is therefore a despairing thing to say that the decline cannot be checked, that the disease must run its course; yet I fear there is nothing else to say. It is manifestly too late to set things right. There came one moment after another in which a remedy could have been applied; but each opportunity was refused, and I do not see how any sufficient one can arise in the future.
To have checked the rot at the very beginning, as, for instance, during the telephone scandal, would have been easy enough (already, a few years before, Mundella had been thrown out of public life for what was then thought a monstrous lapse, but what would now be thought hardly an irregularity). It was even possible to turn the current as late as the Marconi scandal. One virile decision in that crisis might still have saved Parliament. But I do not see, nor do I think anyone else can tell me, how it can be saved now.
The test is very simple. On the day when a Cabinet Minister is heavily fined or imprisoned for irregular action in connection with money the tide will have turned. Does anyone believe that such a day will come? I for my part certainly do not. Therefore the decay will continue. The system, already deep-rooted and established, will become a matter of course, and there will happen in the long run to the House of Commons what has happened to every institution which gets past praying for; but, meanwhile, what of us who have no other instrument? What of the nation?
One point of view was presented to me by a wise old gentleman who had spent about thirty years (doing nothing) in the House of Commons and then wangled a peerage (for nothing) out of his colleagues; for he desired to live on under the same roof and to spend the last of his days amusing himself﻿—remaining an indulgent spectator of what was still, to him, a slightly interesting affair.

===Chinese===

的确，百万富翁报纸老板——尤其假如他既无教养亦因而毫无责任心——对政策制定的影响比政客还大；那些巨大而沉重的金融利益，也确实更有力。但议会是万物必经之门——或者换个比喻，它是唯一能用来直接施加作用的工具；因此它的衰落，关乎这个国家未来的极重大处。所以说一句绝望的话——衰落挡不住、病必须走完它的病程——是令人绝望的；可我怕此外无话可说。拨乱反正，明摆着已太迟。一个又一个可以下药的时机都来过；每一个都被拒绝了，我看不出将来还能有什么充分的时机。
在最初就遏制朽坏——比如电话丑闻期间——本不费事（更早几年，蒙德拉还因一桩当时视为骇人过失的事被逐出公共生活，那事放到今天连个违纪都算不上）。迟至马可尼丑闻，都还来得及扭转潮流。那场危机里一次有丈夫气的决断，本还可以救下议会。但我不明白——我想也没人能告诉我——如今还怎么救。
检验的标准很简单。哪一天一位内阁大臣因金钱上的不端行为被重罚或监禁，潮流就算转了。谁相信会有那一天？我反正不信。所以朽坏将继续。这制度——已根深蒂固——将成为理所当然，下议院长此以往，将遭遇每一家病入膏肓的建制所遭遇的一切；可是与此同时，我们这些没有别的工具的人怎么办？民族怎么办？
有一位睿智的老先生向我摆过一种观点。他在下议院消磨了约三十年（无所事事），随后从同僚们那里（无缘无故地）骗得一个贵族爵位；因为他想在同一座屋顶下活下去，把最后的日子用来取乐——继续做一个宽厚的看客，看这场在他看来仍算稍有兴趣的戏。

===Original===

It was on the evening of a day when I had been trying hard to dig one of the more noisome of the politicians out of the earth in which he had taken refuge after a particularly unpleasant affair. The old fellow had watched the process with an active amusement, and I think he was grateful to me for having provided him with an entertaining day. He had been watching from the gallery, but that evening, as I dined with him, he warned me against wasting too much energy in such a pastime.
I assured him that it was no pastime﻿—that I took it very seriously indeed.
“I know you do,” he answered, “and that is just what I am warning you against. The country is carried on not by these fellows,” jerking his thumb towards the door, “but by the general activity of its people: that people is sound and will come well out of any ordeal.”
The war proved him right.
“What the mass of the people do not do for themselves,” he said, “is done by a rather too large but very conscientious body of permanent officials, underpaid, industrious, and exceedingly competent. The politicians are only the scum on the top; they have very little to do with the happiness or the unhappiness of the nation, and not much to do with its strength. Even the points of policy are, as a rule, decided outside their circle in the weightier financial world. It is a waste of energy to draw that covert too long.”
There is a great deal to be said for the old boy’s attitude. But I could not help asking him what Mr. Gladstone would have done if he had been faced with a Chancellor of the Exchequer taking shares over the breakfast-table; for he remembered Mr. Gladstone well, and had for him that odd half-worship which so many men who knew that powerful, short, oratorical genius of a better past were smitten with. I said, “In Mr. Gladstone’s time the Secretaries of State had more privacy than our modern politicians, and their actions interfered less with the general life of England than they do today: with the permanent officials far less; yet you know as well as I do what Mr. Gladstone would have done if he had found a member of his Government taking shares from a company promoter over the breakfast-table.”

===Chinese===

那是一天的傍晚，我刚费了大劲，想把一个特别难看的政客从一桩特别不愉快的事后藏身的土里刨出来。老头兴致勃勃地看了全程，我想他还感激我给了他一个开怀的日子。他一直从旁听廊上看，可那天晚上同我吃饭时，他警告我别在这种消遣上耗太多力气。
我向他保证这不是消遣——我当它是顶顶正经的事。
“我知道你当真，”他答道，“我警告你的正是这个。国家不是靠这帮家伙维持的，”他朝门那边竖了竖拇指，“而是靠它人民的总体活力：那人民是健全的，经得起任何考验。”
大战证明他对。
“大众替自己不做的事，”他说，“由一支人数过多、却极尽职的常任文官做了——薪俸微薄、勤勉、极为称职。政客只是浮在上头的一层沫子；同民族的苦乐关系很小，同它的力量关系也不大。连政策的要点，通常也在他们那个圈子之外的、更有分量的金融世界里决定。这窝鸟打得太久，是白费力气。”
老头子的态度大有可说之处。可我忍不住问他：倘若格莱斯顿先生面对一位在早餐桌上收股票的财政大臣，他会怎么办？因为他记得格莱斯顿先生，而且对他怀着那种奇怪的半个偶像崇拜——认识那个强毅、矮小、善辩的、来自更好时代的天才的人，许多都染上这种崇拜。我说：“格莱斯顿时代的国务大臣们比我们现代政客享有更多私密，他们的行为对英格兰总体生活的干扰也比今天小：对常任文官更小；可你同我一样清楚，格莱斯顿先生若发现自己政府里的一名成员在早餐桌上从公司发起人手里收股票，他会怎么办。”

===Original===

The old man answered to this that the standard was altogether higher in his own youth than it was now, and that though he could not deny that Cabinet Ministers’ present habit of taking money was a weakness to the country, yet he thought it a small weakness. He added a thing which I have since discovered to be profoundly true, “At any rate, it cannot be remedied, and so you are wasting your time.”
In all the discussions﻿—far too many!﻿—in which I have engaged upon this subject I found only one really enthusiastic defender of the system, and that was a wealthy young Anglo-American who either just had taken up, or was just about to take up, a place in public life. To him our present way of going on had a sort of special excellence peculiar to this country and worthy of Bacchic praise. He spoke like a man who has come into a promised land, and he was moved to indignation by my criticisms against certain recent events. He applauded a Cabinet Minister who had just given a valuable monopoly to a company promoter and then resigned, and accepted a permanent highly salaried post in that company: for he repudiated any connection between the selling of the monopoly and the immediate subsequent acceptation of the salary. He was indignant that such a connection should be dreamt of. They were separate acts.
First the politician, in his capacity of public servant, honestly weighing all the pros and cons, decides that a company promoter is the right man to have a great public monopoly﻿—worth millions. He gives him that monopoly. There the matter ends.
Then begins quite another set of actions. The politician as a private individual accepts a highly salaried post in the pay of the said company promoter and lives on the profits of the monopoly.
Only insane suspicion could connect the two acts.
It was a startling theory to hear: it was also completely new to me. But it had this advantage: it made him a contented supporter of that which others either laughed at cynically or held their tongues about.
I shall be interested to see, as I grow older, whether this type of defence develops, and whether we shall find the taking of bribes and the levying of blackmail a matter, not for commonplace acceptance, but for indignant praise. I do not think that we shall have much of that argument. It is exotic. No man of our blood is really comfortable in the presence of financial corruption.

===Chinese===

老人回答说，他自己青年时代的标准完全比现在高，而且虽然他不能否认内阁大臣们如今收钱的习气是国家的一个弱点，他却认为那是小小的弱点。他补了一句我后来发现极其真实的话：“无论如何，这是没法补救的，所以你在浪费时间。”
在我就此题目参加过的全部讨论里——太多了！——我只遇到过一个真正热诚为这制度辩护的人：一个有钱的年轻的英美混血，他不是刚在公共生活中谋到一席，就是正要去谋。在他看来，我们如今的这套行径有一种本国独有的优异，值得酒神式的赞颂。他说话像进了应许之地的人，我批评最近几件事，他还动了气。他称赞一位刚把值钱的专营权批给一位公司发起人、随即辞职、到那家公司就任高薪长缺的内阁大臣：因为他否认出卖专营权与紧接着接受那份薪俸之间有任何联系。有人竟会梦想出这种联系，令他愤慨。那是两码事。
先是这位政客，以公仆的身份，诚实地权衡一切利弊，断定某公司发起人是获得一项数百万价值的公共专营权的合适人选。他把专营权给了他。事情到此为止。
然后开始完全另一套行为。这位政客作为私人，受聘于上述公司发起人，就任高薪职位，靠专营权的利润生活。
只有疯狂的疑心才会把两件事连起来。
这是一套听起来让人吃惊的理论：对我也是全新的。但它有一样好处：它使他成了一个心满意足的拥护者，拥护那别人要么冷笑、要么闭口的东西。
将来年纪更大些，我会很有兴味地看这一类辩护会不会发展起来，看我们会不会见到受贿与讹诈不但被平常地接受，还被愤慨地赞扬。我想这样的论调不会有太多。它是异货。没有哪个我们血统的人，面对金钱腐败真正自在。

===Original===

He may tolerate it: he may practise it; but it does not fit in with his conscience, still less excite his enthusiasm.
However, one never knows. There are very vile commercial transactions which certainly excite admiration today as surely as they would have excited in our fathers the determination to hang the culprit. Laxity in the relations between men and women which our fathers would at least have condemned is today not so much condoned as defended and made into a sort of religion. Direct treason in time of war merits a few months in the second division. So it is on the cards that I shall live to hear men say in awestruck tones, “What a fellow Binks is! The Prime Minister’s agent offered him ten thousand pounds to clear out, but he got one hundred thousand pounds in solid cash before he had done with him. He frightened the old fool to death by showing he knew all about Chapel Street.”
After all, I have heard much the same tone of religious veneration in those describing some stock-exchange swindle of our day, and a fortune made in a few weeks by the deception of the multitude and the ruin of a thousand families.
So there is still hope.
The authority and prestige of Parliament have sunk like water in a river lock, sogging away rapidly before our eyes.
The process should normally have been more rapid, and the ultimate crisis to which it should surely lead was due to come long before this.
It would have been much better for the country if it *had* so come. The Marconi scandal was an excellent opportunity for an explosion and a thorough clearing of the air. The far worse Dope scandal, taking place when the nation was fighting for its life, was perhaps the last chance offered. If half a dozen of the politicians and company promoters compromised in the Dope scandal had been openly tried and sent to penal servitude during the war, we should have been the better for it today. Instead of that the whole thing was hushed up, and the loot remained in the hands of the looters: an example followed by I know not how many others in the short seven years which have elapsed since that worst of abominations was condoned.

===Chinese===

他可以容忍它：他可以实践它；但它与他的良心不合，更谈不上激发他的热忱。
不过，谁也说不准。有些极卑劣的商业交易，今天确实赢得钦佩，正如它们在我们父辈那里会激起把罪人送上绞架的决心。男女关系上的放荡，我们父辈至少会谴责，今天不但被宽宥，还被辩护、被立成一种宗教。战时的直接叛国，判几个月二等监禁了事。所以完全可能，我会活着听见人们敬畏地说：“宾克斯这家伙真行！首相的说客出价一万镑要他滚蛋，可他到收场时从那老傻瓜手里拿到了十万镑硬邦邦的现钱。他把教堂街的事全知道了，把那老东西活活吓死。”
毕竟，我听过几乎同样的宗教般崇敬的口吻，出自那些描写我们这个时代某场交易所骗局、几个星期里靠欺骗大众、毁掉一千个家庭发了大财的人之口。
所以还是有希望的。
议会的权威与威望，已像船闸里的水，在我们眼前迅速泄尽。
这过程本该更快，它必然引向的最终危机，也早该到来了。
它若真早来了，对这个国家会好得多。马可尼丑闻是爆发一场、彻底换气的绝好机会。远更恶劣的“杜佩”丑闻，发生在民族生死存亡之战中，也许是给出的最后一次机会。倘若杜佩丑闻里牵连的那半打政客与公司发起人在战时被公开审判、判处苦役，我们今天会好得多。非但如此，整件事被据了下来，赃物留在劫掠者手里：自那桩最坏的丑恶得到宽赦以来的短短七年里，不知多少人效法了这个榜样。

===Original===

Perhaps the strongest brake that was put upon the pace downhill, the strongest force restraining a final collapse, and thereby condemning the country to the gradual and certain extinction of Parliamentary authority, lay in the momentum of the old Parliamentary prestige.
To an older generation, of whom some few survive, the idea of a seat in Parliament was connected with honour and a social position worthy of ambition. That generation carried on an admixture of the old tradition which only slowly died, and while it lasted supported the collapsing fabric with which it was intertwined.
Such men, before the present nastiness began, had already acquired a habit of the House of Commons, they lived in the thoughts and customs of an older and much better time, and lent to the diseased institution of their age the memories of its health during their own early youth and manhood.
Some very few still survive to lend the corruption of professional politicians a remnant of moral support. When they are gone we shall have the problem to face in full as we have not yet had to face it.
Of those men who had thus obtained a habit of the House of Commons, as it were, and who for long lent it a fictitious value which it had long ceased to possess, I would particularly recall﻿—for I followed his public action closely and often discussed it with him﻿—Sir Charles Dilke.
He belonged, of course, to an older time, when politics were still respectable and even dignified, and, having begun to take root in them much earlier in life, he felt acutely the wastage of his powers﻿—for he could never have office. But he was treated by the Chair as a sort of Elder Statesman, and I shall always remember the solidity and detail of his experience in foreign affairs. He was one of the very few men of my time (overlapping from an older time) who knew what he was talking about when he discussed the Continent, and who followed the change which was taking place over all Western Europe; and he could value the degree and the quality of it in each country, or at least in the Germanies, in France, in Italy, and in Belgium. Spain he discussed less, and I do not know what his measure of acquaintance was with Spanish affairs. I always count his evidence as valuable in the interesting discussion upon the origin of Gambetta. He knew Gambetta well, and did not despise him.

===Chinese===

也许，刹住下坡车速最强的闸、遏止最终崩塌最强的力——并因此注定了这个国家只能眼看着议会权威逐渐而然地熄灭——存在于旧议会威望的惯性之中。
对更老的一代——尚有寥寥几人健在——议会的一个议席，连着体面，连着值得追求的社会地位。那一代人延续着旧传统的一种掺合物，它死得很慢，而只要它还在，就支撑着与它缠结在一起的正在坍塌的构造。
这些人，在如今的龌龊开始之前，已经养成了下议院的习惯，他们活在一个更老、好得多的时代的思想与习俗里，把那个制度在他们青春与壮年时期的健康的记忆，借给了他们时代的病态建制。
极少数如今仍活着的人，还在给职业政客的腐败垫着一星半点的道义支持。他们一去，我们就得全面面对这个问题，像至今还不曾不得不面对过的那样。
在那些就这样养成了一种下议院的习惯、并长期借给她一种她久已不配的虚假价值的人里，我要特别记起查尔斯·迪尔克爵士——因为我紧随过他的公开言行，也常同他讨论。
他当然属于更早的时代，那时政治还体面、甚至尊严；又因他年纪轻轻就在政治里扎了根，他痛感自己力量的荒耗——因为他永远当不了官。但议长把他当作一种“元老”对待，而我将永远记得他外交经验的扎实与细密。他是我这个时代（从更早的时代延展过来）极少数谈论大陆时真正懂得自己在谈什么的人之一，他跟踪着整个西欧正在发生的变动，并能掂出它在每个国家、至少在德意志诸邦、法国、意大利和比利时的程度与性质。西班牙他谈得少，我不知道他与西班牙事务的交情深浅。在关于甘必大出身的那场有趣的讨论里，我总把他的证词算作有价值。他熟识甘必大，也不轻看他。

===Original===

He said that he was a Jew, and that, therefore, he was quite alien to the French temper. That, of course, was also the thesis of many of Gambetta’s enemies (for Liberals always seem to think that they are saying something unpleasant about a man when they say that he is of Jewish race), but it was not the testimony of most of his friends. Two of Gambetta’s friends I knew well enough: one was a relative of mine, the other was that very great man, Déroulède. I do not think Déroulède was a very accurate observer, but at any rate he testified to the intensely national quality of Gambetta, and swore that there was nothing Oriental about him at all. My own relative, who was of a more cynical temper, said that he was certain of the same thing from careful observation. Gambetta seems to have been a man who commanded real and profound affection, but his legend will be distorted, for it is one of the curses of the modern political chaos that those who by accident or by their desire enter its whirlpool of personal competition and consequent advertisement must inevitably be betrayed by fate. Not one of them can appear as he is, and not one of them will ever be taken by posterity to have anything of greatness about him, however great he may be, unless he can exercise some talent outside the wretched parliamentary business into which he has been caught up.
There is in that life such an admixture of violence, cunning, and bombast, quite apart from the money taint, that a man’s name becomes a mere label for hatred and ridicule in places where the struggle is taken seriously, as it is in France or in Ireland. It becomes a mere label for playacting and make-believe in a country such as ours, where politics are not taken seriously.
No English Parliamentarian could suffer the legacy of hatred, virulent contempt, and documented, detailed, evidenced accusation which will prevent the name of Gambetta from taking an honourable place in history. But, on the other hand, no English politician, since the nineties, will cut any figure for posterity unless he can do something outside his profession. There is nothing to prevent the Postmaster-General, or the Leader of the Opposition, or any other of the circus, from writing a good poem, or painting a good picture; but, unless he does something of the sort, his name will only be one of a hundred names, half comic and all wearisome.

===Chinese===

他说他是犹太人，因此与法兰西气质全然隔膜。这当然也是甘必大的许多论敌的论调（因为自由派说起一个人是犹太种族时，总以为自己在说此人的坏话），但他多数朋友的证词并非如此。甘必大的朋友里，有两位我够熟：一位是我的亲戚，另一位是那位非常伟大的人物德鲁莱德。我不认为德鲁莱德是很精确的观察者，但无论如何，他证明了甘必大身上那种炽烈的民族品质，并起誓说他身上没有半点东方气。我那位气质更冷请的亲戚说，他经细心观察确信同样的事。甘必大似乎是一个赢得过真实而深厚爱戴的人；但他的传说将被歪曲，因为现代政治混乱的诅咒之一就是：那些因偶然或因自愿进入它那个人竞逐与随之而来的扬名的漩涡的人，必被命运出卖。他们没有一个能以本来面目示人，而且除非能在把他卷进去的那套可鄙的议会营生之外施展某种才具，无论他多伟大，后世都不会觉得他有任何伟大之处。
那样的生活里，撇开金钱的污渍不谈，掺着那样多的暴戾、狡诈与浮夸，以致在把这场争斗当真的地方——如法国、如爱尔兰——一个人的名字沦为仇恨与嘲笑的一个标签；而在我们这样一个不把政治当真的国家，它不过是装腔作势、逢场作戏的一个标签。
没有哪位英国议员会承受得住那一笔遗产——仇恨、恶毒的蔑视、以及有档有据、细节俱全、证据确凿的指控；正是它将阻止甘必大的名字在历史上占据一个体面的位置。但另一方面，自九十年代以来，也没有哪位英国政客能在后世留下任何身影，除非他能做出本行之外的东西。没有什么拦着邮政总长、或反对党领袖、或马戏班里的任何一位去写一首好诗、画一幅好画；可除非他干出点这类东西，他的名字将只是一百个名字中的一个，一半滑稽，全部乏味。

===Original===

For the trade has lost its dignity. It is found out.
I have delayed on this subject too long. It is public property, and filth at that, and people are tired of it, for in their heart of hearts they know that nothing more can be done, that public life in its Parliamentary form is past saving.
It is possible that I exaggerate the effect of the evil; it is possible that some novel remedy besides the obvious one of increasing the power of the Crown may set things right. It has certainly done so in the past; the breakup of one institution apparently necessary to society has discovered the importance of other forces which have saved society by bringing up new forces unsuspected. It is possible. Perhaps it is probable. It is to be hoped. And that is all one can say.

===Chinese===

因为这行当丢了它的尊严。它被戳穿了。
这个题目我耽搁得太久。它已是公众的产业，而且是污秽的产业，人们已经腻了；因为他们心底里知道，再无能为力了，议会形态的公共生活已不可救药。
也可能我夸大了这恶的后果；也可能除开那个显而易见的加强王权之法，还有某种新药能拨乱反正。过去确实发生过这样的事：一个表面上是社会必需的建制解体了，反而显出了其他力量的重要，它们靠唤起没人疑心的新力量拯救了社会。这是可能的。也许还是或然的。但愿如此。而人所能说的，也就到此为止。

===Original===

Such thoughts are suitable for a drizzling calm, and for wet canvas hanging useless and dead in a hopeless dawn upon a sea without life. But with the coming of a little wind, a little northeasterly wind in the growing light; with its blowing away of the misty rain, and its bringing a heartening into the morning, I prefer to turn to better things, to the realities: to the English water and to the English land.
The mist scudded off from the Folkestone waters, the light not only brightened, but grew coloured, until at last there was a fine, flaming dawn over the Kentish chalk and the Bastion cliffs of the Kentish salient, and very soon she was running to it prettily, her wet canvas holding the wind well, and, if anything, pressing her a little too hard. My companion came on deck with the morning, woken by the new life in the hull, and by the run of the sea along the sides. As yet the water was smooth, for the wind was a trifle off shore, and would so remain until we had come into the hollow bend of the flat coast, the last of which runs almost due south, and which ends in the shingle of Dungeness. Also we were still on the ebb and the wind followed the stream.
After the sun had risen the breeze grew stronger, and even violent; but as I greatly desired to round the Ness before the tide should catch me and raise a sea, I would not take in a reef. So I held on, though with the more southerly course we were setting, and with the wind still rising, it was not over safe; for that wind was now coming almost due aft, her boom was right out, and I feared a gybe. To take every advantage of any slight northing of the wind that might come, I ran closer into the coast at a still greater risk of gybe, of course, if the wind should hold where it did when we came to the sharp turn of the Ness. We were now running for it very hard indeed, and the *Nona*, splendidly as she lifts when properly trimmed, was burying a little owing to too great press of mainsail, and with head sails doing no work to support her bows. A slight sea had risen, although the tide was still with us and with the wind, and once or twice she took it green as she plunged forward.

===Chinese===

这样的念头，配得上一个飘着毛毛雨的死寂，配得上无用的湿帆在无望的黎明里死沉沉垂在一片没有生气的海上。可风一来——渐渐亮起来的天色里一小股东北风——它吹走迷蒙的雨，给清晨带来一点振作，我便宁愿转向更好的东西，转向实在之物：英格兰的水和英格兰的陆地。

雾被扫出福克斯通的海面；天光不但亮了，而且有了颜色，到末了，肯特白垩和肯特突出部堡崖的顶上烧起一场明亮炽烈的朝霞；不多时她已朝它欣然驶去，湿帆兜风兜得很好，甚至把她压得略略过沉。同伴随晨光上了甲板，被船壳里新的生命、被沿船舷奔流的海水唤醒。水面还平，因为风略偏岸外；在驶入平坦海岸的凹弯之前，风会一直这样——那道海岸的末一段几乎正南走向，尽头便是邓杰内斯的卵石滩。而且我们仍在落潮上，风顺着流水。

日出之后风势愈壮，甚至狂暴起来；可我一心要在潮头追上我、掀起海浪之前绕过邓杰内斯岬，便不肯收帆结。就这样硬撑着，虽然照我们愈偏南的航向、照还在上涨的风，这不算安稳；因为风如今几乎是正后方，桁完全放出，我怕换舷。为占尽风向略略偏北的每一点便宜，我更贴着海岸跑——当然，若风在抵岬尖急转弯处维持原样，换舷的危险就更大。此刻我们确实在拼命赶路，“诺娜”号虽在调校得当时昂首漂亮，此刻却因主帆受压过重、前帆又不出力扶起船头，微微向下扎。虽然潮仍顺着我们、顺着风，一片小海已经掀起来；她向前扑去时，有一两回整片绿水漫过了船头。

===Original===

Then it was, as I held the *Nona* with difficulty thus, right in front of a wind that was now much too strong for her canvas, that I understood what had long puzzled me, and that is, why the ancients, down to the end of the Dark Ages in the Mediterranean, and to, perhaps, the fourteenth century in the North, continued to step the masts of their square rig so far aft. It was to prevent burying; to give the bow its chance of lifting to the sea. For this rig sailed at its best with the wind aft, and when their boats were running so with the great square sail driving them into the seas, had their mast been stepped forward, they would have buried dangerously.
And why do you think men were so long in adapting the fore-and-aft to larger boats?
But I cannot delay to consider such things of the past, for I am at the edge of Dungeness, and with my heart in my mouth lest at any moment she should gybe over.
I handed the helm to my companion, who had a better eye than I had, and a firmer hold, and he, with great skill, did just save the gybe. We had not the luck to hold the tide; we caught the turn of it and the early flood nearly two miles from Dungeness Point, and, as you may imagine, it raised a short and buffeting sea, which made our task the more perilous. My companion handled her beautifully, until I told him he could go as near as he liked to the end of the Ness, because the land shelves down here so steeply as to give one deep water right up against the shore. We were, perhaps, five minutes in the worst of the ordeal just opposite the lighthouse before we could put the helm a trifle over and get the wind fairly on our quarter and breathe again. It seemed about half an hour, for the strength of the tide, even against that wind, made us forge very slowly past the land, and it was astonishing to see the white foam racing by while the shingle, a stone’s throw away, barely crept past us; but save the gybe he did, as I said, and there came that placid moment when she would take a turn a little west of south, and then fairly west of south, and the roar of the wind was at her quarter and I had no excuse for an excessive nervousness.

===Chinese===

就在那时——正当我这样费力地把“诺娜”号顶着对她帆力来说过强的风——我懂得了一直让我困惑的事：为什么古人，在地中海直到黑暗时代之末、在北方也许直到十四世纪，一直把横帆的桅杆立得那样靠后。正是为了防扎首；为了让船头有抬头迎浪的机会。因为这种帆装在风从正后来时航行最佳，而他们的船这样跑着、大横帆把它们往浪里驱时，桅杆若立得靠前，就会危险地扎首。

可你想，人为什么那么久才把纵帆装推广到大船上？

这类往昔之事我顾不上细想，因为我已到了邓杰内斯的边沿，提心吊胆，唯恐她随时换舷翻过去。

我把舵交给同伴，他的眼比我尖，手比我稳；他凭着高超的技术，恰恰把那次换舷救了下来。我们的运气没能保住潮；离邓杰内斯角还有将近两英里，我们就赶上了转潮和初涨；你们可以想象，那掀起一片短促而乱撞的海，使我们的差事愈发凶险。同伴操纵得漂亮，直到我告诉他，尽可随意贴近岬的尽头，因为这里陆地陡深直下，贴着岸边就是深水。大约五分钟，我们就在灯塔正对面熬着那最凶的一段，才把舵略略一转，让风安安稳稳吃在船尾侧，喘过气来。感觉足有半个钟头：因为潮的力量即便逆着那样的风，也只让我们极慢地蹭过陆地——白沫在旁边飞驰，而一掷之遥的卵石滩几乎爬不过我们，看得人瞠目；可他到底把换舷救下了，如我所说；随后来了那个安然的时刻：她可以转偏南一点西，然后稳稳偏南西，风的吼声落在了船尾侧，我便再没有理由过度神经紧张。

===Original===

But she was pressing too hard altogether, so now we threw her round (as I ought to have done long before), took in two reefs, and then were able to set an easy and comfortable line for the doubtful harbour of Rye, which neither of us had yet made. We feared we might touch at the entry, but we had at least the comfort of a wind off the land, yet far round enough to take us in without beating, and we were on a rising tide.
I have often thought that a boat running snug and properly reefed to suit the wind is a model of the virtue which the theologians called temperance, and which has nothing to do with the historical dread of wine bred in those who have themselves suffered from, or have seen in their families, the disease called dipsomania. For temperance does not mean doing things half-heartedly, still less does it mean doing or not doing things extremely. It means suiting your implements to your motive power, and not carrying on at a risk. It is not unconnected with dignity, and there is something profound about it; I will call it the contralto among the virtues, and leave it at that.
We sped on (the stream against us, very slight now that we were inside the Ness); we saw before us the broomstick, or whatever you like to call it, which marks (or does not mark) the entry into Rye Haven, and the last bare remnant of what was once the crowded fairway, a market for the nations﻿—the harbour of Rye.
The little town stood up inland, neat and beautiful, red and grey, a pyramid thrown up towards its squat steeple; for under that keen northeasterly air all was clear and well cut. The pilot book gave us no comfort; it only told us that the deep changes with every gale, and, as for the lead, there was no use for it, the shore would come too steep; what we had to do was to look out for the outer buoy, and round it, hoping that as we turned we should neither touch nor get into irons, but still have the wind easterly enough to round to take us in.
On the plan I had by me the fairway was marked dangerously near the point from which the wind was coming, but I trusted to luck, and still kept her to her course.

===Chinese===

可她终究压得过沉，于是我们把她转过来（这本该早就做），收了两个帆结，然后得以定下一条从容安适的线，奔拉伊那个靠不住的港去——我们俩谁也没进过它。我们怕进港时擦底，可至少有一桩安慰：风离岸来，偏得又够圆，不必抢风便能送我们进去；而且我们在涨潮上。

我常常想，一条收帆收得妥帖合风的船，是神学家们所谓“节制”之德的模型——这节制与那种历史性的怕酒毫无干系；那种怕，生在自己患过、或在家里见过嗜酒症的人心里。节制不是做事三心二意，更不是把事做得或不做得极端。节制是让器械合乎动力，不冒险硬撑。它与尊严并非无关，其中有某种深的东西；我称它为诸德中的女中音，就说到这里。

我们疾行（潮流顶着我们，进了岬内已极微弱）；望见前方那根扫帚柄——你爱叫什么都行——它标着（或标不着）拉伊港的进口，标着那曾经船挤船的航道、万国的市场——拉伊港——如今光秃秃的最后残迹。

小镇在内地立起，整洁而美丽，红与灰，一座朝它矮墩墩的尖塔堆上去的金字塔；因为在那股凛冽的东北空气下，一切清晰而有棱角。航路指南不给我们安慰；它只说深水随每场大风变，至于测深锤，用了也白用，岸陡得太快；我们要做的是找到外浮标，绕过它，只求转向时既不擦底、也不顶风失速，而风还偏东得够我们绕进去。

在我手边的平面图上，航道标得离来风的岬危险地近，可我把命交给运气，仍然让她走她的航线。

===Original===

Any man making Rye Haven must first resign himself to the will of God, and consider, especially if the boat is running and a little over-canvassed, that death is but a mighty transition; that it is all sand hereabouts, with no cruel rocks to tear the tender body with their horrid fangs; that nothing is worth calculating in life, because things happen by fate anyhow, or by chance, but certainly not by our direction; and that if, or when, she strikes, it will not be his fault. There is no man living that can ever tell you the deep into Rye Harbour, for it shifts with every wind, and at the best it is of the narrowest. As for me, I have made it four times in my life, each time I have touched and never have I struck, and how the thing was done no one knows. Nevertheless, they still build ships in Rye, and the tradition of the sea is all about it, though what used to be the old haven is now a field.
I remember sitting up to a very late and clear sunset of a Whit-Monday, gazing, under the sharp northeast breeze (the *Nona* moored at last to the wooden quay), at the pyramid of red roofs, and the highly successful walls and battlements of Rye, and considering within my mind many things.
First I considered within my own dear mind how marvellously this place had been preserved, seeing what modern England is, and what modern travel is. It is, of course, a stage-scenery town, such as I have heard that Rothenburg is in Germany, and, therefore, there is an interest in preserving it; but I never heard that any such pains were taken to prevent its destruction by increase or rebuilding, or by any of those courses which bring death to beauty, such as a good train service, clubs, large and torturing hotels. Rye manages to live on. I suppose there is a spell. At any moment it may be broken, and the flood will pour in.
Being what it is, you cannot begin thinking about Rye without finding yourself dragged on to consider preciosity, and from that to the business of letters is but half a step.
Here at Rye did I meet and speak with Henry James, who was in the very heart of letters.

===Chinese===

凡进拉伊港的人，必须先把自己交托给天主的旨意，并且想一想——尤其当船是跑顺风、又略微超帆的时候——死不过是一场重大的过渡；这一带全是沙，没有残忍的礁石用可怕的獠牙撕扯娇嫩的船身；人生没有一件事值得计算，因为事情横竖凭命运发生，或凭偶然，断不凭我们的指挥；而她若当真搁底——如果、当真搁了——那不是他的错。世上没有一个活人能告诉你拉伊港的深水在哪儿，因为它随风变，至多时也窄得可怜。至于我，平生进过它四次，每次都擦了底，从没有搁死；这事是怎么办成的，无人知晓。尽管如此，拉伊仍在造船，海的传统在它四下皆是，虽然当年的老港如今成了一片田。

我记得一个圣灵降临节后的星期一，坐守到极晚极清的日落，就着凛冽的东北风（“诺娜”号终于系上了木码头），凝望那红屋顶的金字塔、拉伊那些极见成效的城墙和雉堞，心里盘算许多事情。

首先我在我可爱的心里盘算：这地方保存得何等奇妙——想想现代英格兰是什么，现代旅行是什么。它当然是一座布景镇，如我听说德意志的罗滕堡那样；因此保存它自有其趣味；可我从未听说有谁下过这样的功夫，防止它毁于扩建或重建，毁于那一切给美带来死亡的手法——比如便捷的火车、俱乐部、大而折磨人的旅馆。拉伊居然活得下去。我想必有一道符咒。它随时可能被打破，洪水随时会灌进来。

既是这么个所在，你一想拉伊，就免不了被拖去想“精致”，而从精致到文字这一行，只有半步。

就在拉伊，我结识并交谈过亨利·詹姆斯——文字这行当正中心的人物。

===Original===

Here also did I meet his brother, William James, whose business in life it was to write about philosophy, but whose conversation when I met him turned principally upon the subtle diplomatic genius of the King of England then reigning; upon the stability of English institutions; the excellence of the English police, and the singular faculty shown by our institutions of permitting differences without conflict. Also the piercing wit of the paper *Punch*, and the profound wisdom of *The Times*. I had just come back with a companion from foreign parts; I had been sleeping rough, and I was in no condition to take part in polite conversation, so I did all the listening, and, as I listened, I still considered how literary was Rye. And so now, years after, looking at Rye from my boat, was I led on to consider the trade or business of letters.
When a writing man approaches that subject, he feels﻿—at least I feel﻿—like a man before a jumbled heap of material of all kinds﻿—one of those refuse-heaps in which you may pick up stray bits of machinery, or salve a little fuel, or find what you will. Or again, it is like being in one of those shops﻿—there are few of them left, alas!﻿—where an old half-wit has collected all manner of things, so that one knows not whether to begin by the furniture, or the glass, or the pictures, or the secondhand telescopes, or the books. For there is an indefinite number of ways of considering letters.
To those who have had to pursue letters as a trade (and to this I have been condemned all my life since my twenty-fifth year) it certainly is the hardest and the most capricious, and, indeed, the most abominable of trades, for the simple reason that it was never meant to be a trade.
A man is no more meant to live by writing than he is meant to live by conversation, or by dressing, or by walking about and seeing the world. For there is no relation between the function of letters and the economic effect of letters; there is no relation between the goodness and the badness of the work, or the usefulness of the work, or the magnitude of the work, and the sums paid for the work. It would not be natural that there should be such a relation, and, in fact, there is none.
This truth is missed by people who say that good writing has no market. That is not the point. Good writing sometimes has a market, and very bad writing sometimes has a market.

===Chinese===

也在这里，我结识了他的兄弟威廉·詹姆斯。此人生涯以论哲学为业，可我遇见他时，他的谈话主要围绕当时在位的英王的精明外交天才；围绕英格兰制度的稳固；英国警察的优良；以及我们的制度那种容许歧见而不起冲突的特异才具。还有《笨拙》周刊的锋利机智，《泰晤士报》的深沉智慧。我刚同一位伙伴从外国回来；一路风餐露宿，实在没有体面谈话的模样，便只管听；一面听，一面仍在想，拉伊是何等地“文”。于是几年之后的今天，从我的船上望着拉伊，我又被引去想文字这门生意。

一个弄笔墨的人捱近这个题目，便觉得——至少我觉得——像一个人站在一堆各色杂物跟前：那种垃圾堆，你可以在里面捡到零星的机器零件，捞一点燃料，找到随便什么。又像进了那种铺子——唉，剩得不多了！——一个老半痴收罗了五花八门的货色，叫人不知先看家具、先看玻璃、先看画、先看二手望远镜，还是先看书。因为考虑文字的方式有无限多种。

对那些不得不把文字当生意做的人（我从二十五岁起就被判了这一行），它确乎是行业中至难、至任性、说实在至可憎的一行，理由很单纯：它从来就不是被当作行业发明出来的。

人以写作为生，不比以谈话为生、以穿衣为生、以游荡看世界为生更合于天性。因为文字的功能与文字的经济效果之间，没有关系；作品的好坏、用处、大小，与所得的款项之间，没有关系。有这种关系反倒不自然；事实上也没有。

说“好文字没有市场”的人，错过了这条真理。要点不在此。好文字有时有市场，很坏的文字有时也有市场。

===Original===

Useful writing sometimes has a market, and writing of no use whatsoever, even as recreation, sometimes has a market. Writing important truths sometimes has a market. Writing the most ridiculous errors and false judgments sometimes has a market. The point is that the market has nothing to do with the qualities attached to writing. It never had and it never will. There is no injustice about it, any more than there is an injustice in the survival of beauty or ugliness in human beings, or the early death of the beautiful or the ugly. There is no more injustice about it than there is in a dry year hurting a root crop, and a wet harvest hurting a corn crop. The relationship between the excellence or the usefulness of a piece of literature, and the number of those who will buy it in a particular form, is not a causal relationship, it is a purely capricious one; and I, for my part, have never complained of the absence of a market, nor flattered myself upon the presence of one when a market turned up, though I have certainly been astonished at the way the market would behave.
For some few months thousands upon thousands﻿—at one moment sixty thousand a week﻿—of the articles I wrote upon the war were demanded by the public. Some months later the same articles, of the same value, or lack of value, were no longer demanded. There was no particular reason, the articles were clear, and based upon such insufficient knowledge as I shared with all my fellow citizens. I had no private information save upon the point of numbers, and that was the very point upon which the public grew most easily fatigued, and were at last most sceptical.
Mr. Hutchinson, for whose work I personally have (though I am afraid my judgment is not worth much in these things) a great liking, wrote among other books a book called *Once Aboard the Lugger*. It was exceedingly amusing, fresh, and, as I should have said, well worth anybody’s buying. I do not know how many copies he sold of it, but I should imagine nothing very astonishing. A little while after he wrote a book called *If Winter Comes*, and it sold by the stack, and by the ton. One could see it piled up man-high upon the counters of shops in America, and it sold, and it sold, and it sold.
Mr. Wells wrote a book which is most remarkable, and will probably make a mark in English literature. It was called *The Time Machine*.

===Chinese===

有用的文字有时有市场；毫无用处、连消遣之用都没有的文字，有时也有市场。写重要真理的文字有时有市场；写最可笑的错误和虚妄判断的文字，有时也有市场。要点在于：市场同文字附着的品质毫无关系。从来没有，永远没有。这里面没有不公，正如人的美或丑的存殁没有不公，美的或丑的早夭没有不公。旱年伤了块根作物、湿收成伤了谷类，其中也没有不公。一件文学作品之卓越或有用，同以某种形态购买它的人数，其间不是因果关系，是纯粹任性的关系；至于我，市场不来，我从不抱怨，市场来了，我也从不自鸣得意，虽然市场的行径确实屡屡叫我惊讶。

有那么几个月，公众要我的战争文字，成千上万——有一阵一星期六万份。几个月后，同样的文字，同样的价值或无价值，没人要了。没有什么特别的缘故；那些文字是清楚的，依据的是我同全体同胞共享的那点不充足的知识。我没有私密的情报，唯独人数这一点除外——而恰恰在那一点上，公众最容易疲倦，最后也最怀疑。

哈钦森先生——他的作品我个人很喜欢（虽然怕我这类判断不值什么）——写过一本书，叫《上了小帆船》。极有趣，清新，照我看谁买都值。他卖了多少本我不知道，可我想数字平平。过了不久他写了一本《如果冬天来了》，成垛成吨地卖。在美利坚的铺子里，可以看见它堆得齐人高，卖了又卖，卖了又卖。

威尔斯先生写过一本极出色的书，多半会在英国文学里留名。它叫《时间机器》。

===Original===

When Henley (I think it was) was bringing it out by instalments, the appearance of each was, for me at least, and for very many others, the chief event of the month. I do not know how many copies he sold, but there again I will swear nothing comparable to the numbers he has sold of later books which certainly will not stand out in English literature. That one which I hear has sold most largely has always seemed to me to be a mere repetition of what his audience already thought they knew, and of a philosophy which they already held: I mean his *Outline of History*. It was mainly a repetition of the popular mythology of today, of antiquated scientific jargon, full of the popular errors, but it was clear, well-proportioned and vivacious, showing a rare economy in the use and placing of words. It was also (and that is high minor praise for any book purporting to deal with history) astonishingly accurate in details. The names were properly spelt; south was never written for north, nor east for west; the dates were all right. Anyone who has attempted the writing of history will not belittle such a feat: I, least of all, because I am constitutionally inaccurate in such things, and I will write left for right and north for south as a matter of course, and the word “Tyre” for the word “Acre,” and so on. At any rate, the book, with all its good and bad qualities, is obviously not of the first class, and equally obviously is not destined to endure, while *The Time Machine* is quite certainly of the first class, and is quite likely to endure. But I believe that the huge ephemeral demand for the *Outline of History* created no special, certainly no very great, demand for the more permanent *The Time Machine*. In general, I am assured by publishers, that when there is a sudden boom in a writer’s work, they hardly ever manage to float on that boom a resale of earlier works; while if these earlier works are unknown, it is almost impossible to give them a popular fame on the strength of the later works. Witness the late Mr. Hudson’s *Crystal Age*. I came across it as a young man in a bookshop in the Euston Road on a winter evening. I took it up and began reading it, and I read it so long that the bookseller told me I must buy it, which I did. Now, towards the end of Mr.

===Chinese===

亨利（我记得是他）分期刊出它的时候，每一期的出现，至少对我、对许多许多人，是当月头一件大事。他卖了多少本我不知道；可我仍敢断言，比不上他后来几本书的数目——那几本断然不会在英国文学里出头。听说卖得最广的那本，在我看始终只是把读者自以为已知的东西、把他们本已持有的哲学重复了一遍：我指他的《历史纲要》。它主要是把今天的流行神话、过时的科学套话重复一遍，满是流行的错误，但写得清楚、匀称、有生气，用字措置见出罕见的节俭。它还（对任何自命处理历史的书，这已是很高的次等赞誉）在细节上惊人地准确。名字拼写无误；南没有写成北，东没有写成西；日期都对。写过历史的人不会小看这样的功夫：我尤其不会，因为我天生在这类事上没准头，左写成右、北写成南是家常便饭，“提尔”写成“阿卡”等等。总之那本书，好坏不论，显然不是第一流，也同样显然传不下去；而《时间机器》千真万确是第一流，极可能传下去。可我相信，《历史纲要》那阵转瞬即逝的巨大需求，并没有给更经久的《时间机器》带来什么特别、更说不上很大的需求。出版商们一致告诉我：一位作家的作品突然走俏时，他们几乎从来搭不上这股风，把他早先的作品重卖出去；而早先的作品若原本无名，想凭后来的作品给它们挣一份大众声名，几乎不可能。已故赫德森先生的《水晶时代》便是明证。我年轻时一个冬夜，在尤斯顿路一家书铺撞见它。拿起来一读，读得太久，书商说我非买不可，我便买了。后来，赫德森先生

===Original===

Hudson’s life, he became deservedly famous as a writer upon the things of the natural world, and especially upon animals. But what made him famous was not his subject, though he was both accurate and learned; it was his manner of writing. One would have expected, therefore, that the *Crystal Age* would, upon his fame tardily arising, have a quiet and equal fame. It did not. I say that there are no laws governing these movements. They are capricious and incalculable.
Sometimes one hears that a great demand for a book, or for a set of newspaper articles, is due to something touching a common feeling in a particular fashion, something which great bodies of men necessarily feel together. There is a sense, of course, in which that is a mere truism, for it is clear that obscure stuff, or stuff dealing with matters that could not interest great numbers, will never sell. Why should it? But if the statement is intended to mean (as it usually is) that whatever sells in great numbers does so through this quality of a common appeal, that anything having this quality will always sell in great numbers, and that this quality is in some way a rare thing discoverable only in “bestsellers,” then the statement is manifestly false. A great deal of matter which possesses this appeal﻿—enormously the greater part of such matter﻿—remains unknown, and that which is best known is often rather weak in such an appeal. There are a great many factors entering into the result of a great popular demand which people forget because they are irrational: fashion is one, routine is another, inertia is a third. I know one man whose name, for the honour I bear it (he is now dead), I would not publish, since what I am about to say of his work is not flattering. He wrote a great number of stories, all of them with exactly the same plot and exactly the same characters. There was a beautiful chocolate-box girl, there was a villain, there was a hero who became rich after being poor, there were parents who objected to the match, and there was a racehorse. Had he departed from the formula, he would not have sold. As it was, he sold by the million. He was not only as modest a man as you could find, but exceedingly generous. He put on no airs; he made no error as to the nature of his success; he is already forgotten.

===Chinese===

晚年，赫德森先生作为描写自然界、尤其动物的作家，声誉日隆，得当其所。可成全他名声的不是他的题材，虽然他既精确又有学养；是他行文的方式。因此人们本可指望，《水晶时代》会随着他姗姗来迟的成名，得一份安静而相当的名声。没有。我说，支配这些动静的没有定律。它们任性而不可测。

有时听人说，一本书、一组报纸文章的大需求，是缘于某样东西碰巧触动了共同的情感，某种大群人必然共感的东西。这话在某种意义上当然是老生常谈，因为晦涩的东西、或处理大众不会关心之事的东西，显然永远卖不动。何必卖得动？可这话若意在（通常正是此意）说：凡大量销售之物皆凭这共同感染力；凡有这感染力的东西必大量销售；且这感染力是某种只有“畅销书”里才找得到的稀罕物——那这话就分明是假的。具有这种感染力的东西——其中绝大部分——依然无人知晓；而最广为人知的东西，感染力往往相当贫弱。促成一场大众需求的因素很多，人们因为它们不讲道理就把它们忘了：时尚是一个，惯例是一个，惰性是第三个。我认识一个人，出于我对他的敬意（他已故去），我不愿公布他的名字，因为我将要说的他的作品并不好听。他写过一大堆小说，情节一模一样，人物一模一样。有一个糖果盒封面式的美人，有一个恶棍，有一个先贫后富的主人公，有反对婚事的父母，有一匹赛马。他若偏离这个配方，就卖不掉。正因如此，他百万百万地卖。他不仅是你能找到的最谦逊的人，还极其慷慨。他不摆架子；对自己成功的性质没有半点误解；他已经被遗忘了。

===Original===

Nothing shall persuade me that any one of many thousand men could not have written exactly the same; but a certain vast public got used to that particular insipid food, and wanted it unchanged, just as you and I get used to a particular kind of breakfast, and eat it every day of our lives.
Of course, there is a different set of factors operating in different phases of society. The popular bestseller of today depends upon the existence of our great towns with their machine-made citizens, all working blindly in little grooves, lacking any common direction﻿—a dust of individuals. He depends also on that newfangled, mechanical, and, let us hope, ephemeral, institution called “popular education.” If you tell millions of boys and girls (nearly all the citizens of the state), at the age when they receive unquestioning any dogmatic teaching, that their ancestors were repulsive savages, first actually bestial, later exceedingly vile, and that these ancestors passed through certain stages, and were “Cavemen,” and men of the “Stone Age,” and of the “Bronze Age,” and so forth; then, obviously, writers using such terms, and taking for granted such a mythology, will fit in with the popular market. The demand for books, or for writing in any form, is created by minds already moulded, and any kind of writing which tends to break the mould is resented and left on one side. In older phases of society, judgment upon writing was passed by a few well-trained and leisured people, who handed down their judgments to a larger class, but still a small class compared with the whole state; and it is clear that in such a condition of society﻿—which was that of England from the last third of the eighteenth century to the last third of the nineteenth﻿—there would be no bestsellers in our modern sense, and that there would be a nearer approach between the demand for a book and its excellence. But there also the mould had to be considered. Macaulay’s false rhetoric sold well, because it agreed with the religion of his day. No historical work telling the truth about James II would have sold in his day, nor for that matter would any such matter sell in ours.
For religion is here, as elsewhere, at the root of effect. Robert Louis Stevenson was an excellent writer, but his popularity reposed not upon his excellence alone, but also upon the consonance of his religion with that of his readers.

谁也不能说服我：几千人里的任何一个都写不出一模一样的东西；只是一大群公众惯了那一种寡淡的饭食，要它一成不变，正如你我惯了某一种早饭，一辈子天天吃它。

当然，社会不同的阶段有不同的因素组合在起作用。今天的畅销书，靠的是我们大城的存在，和城里机器造出的公民——全都在小槽子里盲目做工，缺乏任何共同的方向——一撮个人的尘沙。它还靠那个新式的、机械的、我们但愿短命的制度，所谓“大众教育”。你在千百万男孩女孩（几乎是国家的全体公民）无条件接受一切教条教导的年纪，告诉他们：他们的祖先是可憎的野蛮人，起初十足兽性，后来极端卑劣；这些祖先经过某些阶段，是“穴居人”，是“石器时代”的人，是“青铜时代”的人，等等——那么显然，使用这套字眼、默认这套神话的作家，就合大众市场的口径。对书、对任何形态文字的需求，是已被铸好的心智造出来的；任何倾向于打破这个铸模的文字，都遭怨恨，被撇在一旁。在社会较早的诸阶段，对文字的评骘出自少数受过良好训练、有闲暇的人，他们把判断传给一个较大的阶级，但同整个国家相比仍是小阶级；显然，在那样一种社会状况下——英格兰从十八世纪后三分之一到十九世纪后三分之一正是那样——不会有我们现代意义的畅销书，书的需求与书的卓越之间会接近得多。但那里的铸模也得考虑。麦考莱的伪雄辩卖得好，因为它合乎他那时代的宗教。一部对詹姆斯二世讲真话的史书，在他那时代卖不动；这样东西在我们这时代也一样卖不动。

因为宗教在这里，同在别处一样，是效果之根。罗伯特·路易斯·史蒂文森是出色的作家，可他的盛名所倚，不单是他的出色，还有他的宗教与读者宗教的谐和。

A man who was perpetually telling you that it was your duty to be cheerful, that you must take pleasure in God’s creation, that you had a “task of happiness” would cut no ice with the Irish, or with the Italians, or, in general, with the Catholic culture of Europe, but he rings true in the ears of the Protestant culture. Conversely, what the French powerfully call the “bitter taste of reality” has very little chance with the crowd of the North. It is a spur and a relish to the crowd of the South. You could not sell Barrie in Arles nor Baudelaire in Pudsey.
The truth remains that the relation, or the lack of relation, between the economic result of a literary effort and its success as a piece of craft is of very much less importance than the consideration of the canon, the attempt to achieve a standard. To get a canon is the first requirement of letters. For though it is said that time will test literature for us in any case, this is not universally true; and, even in so far as it is true, it is better to have the good things to our hand than to wait for time to establish them. Such a canon requires not only intelligent analysis, but a common philosophy into which our rules must fit. It is, therefore, with great difficulty arrived at, or perhaps not to be arrived at at all, in a period of confusion like ours. But the attempt to arrive at it never loses interest﻿—no, not even today.
One of the things which always seem to me best worth saying in connection with letters is that prose should be distinguished, not only from verse, but also from rhetoric; and that prose in itself, mere lucid, economic, unornamented prose, is the foundation of good letters.
If the national prose is sound, the rest of national expression will come of itself. But this matter of prose has today been nearly forgotten. For most of us perhaps the phrase “good prose,” or “fine prose,” really means rhetoric. It means a passage in which the soul is stirred by a choice of rhythm and sound, and a mystical connotation of words in some passage not definitely reduced to versification.

一个人不停地告诉你：快活是你的本分，你必须在天主的造物中取乐，你有一桩“幸福的功课”——这样的人，在爱尔兰人那里、在意大利人那里、总在欧洲天主教文明那里，行不通；可在新教文明的耳朵里，他字字入心。反过来，法兰西人强有力的说法——“现实之苦味”——在北方大众那里几乎无缘分。它是南方大众的马刺与美味。你在阿尔勒卖不动巴里，在普德西也卖不动波德莱尔。

真理依然是：一件文字劳作的经济结果同它作为手艺的成败，其间关系之有无，远不如考虑典则、追求一个标准来得重要。得到一部典则，是文字的第一需要。因为虽说时间自会替我们检验文学，这并不处处为真；即便在其为真的范围内，好东西就在手边，也胜过坐等时间去确立它们。这样一部典则，不但需要聪明的分析，还需要一套我们的规则能够嵌进去的共同哲学。因此，在像我们这样混乱的时期，它极难达成，或者根本达不成。但求达成的努力从不失其趣味——不，今天也不失。

依我看，关于文字，有一件事永远最值得说：散文不但要与诗分清，也要与雄辩分清；散文本身——单纯明白、节俭、不加雕饰的散文——是好文字的根基。

民族散文健全，民族表达的其余自会随之而来。可散文这桩事，今天几乎被忘了。对我们多数人来说，“好散文”或“美文”这说法，实际指的就是雄辩。指一段文字，灵魂被节奏与音响的拣选、被某段文字中词语的神秘暗示所激动——那段文字并未明确归为诗行。

===Original===

Thus, all the great passages from the Jacobean translation of the Hebrew Scriptures which are familiar as examples to our generation are essentially rhetoric; so are the excerpts quoted from Bunyan, such as the fine closing passage of the “Pilgrim’s Progress”; so is every single passage quoted from Carlyle; so are even those quoted from Newman and (I am sorry to say) those quoted from the greatest of our modern prose writers, Huxley.
But prose is rightly to be distinguished from anything of that kind. The excellence of prose lies in its adaptation to the function of intelligent expression or of narration. It is a statement; the end of it is not the exciting of emotion but the clear presentation, whether of a record (in fiction or fact) or of an idea, which the writer desires to communicate to his fellows.
He that succeeds in presenting the idea lucidly, so that no modification or subsidiary idea confuses the process; he that succeeds in presenting vividly to the reader an image of particular fact, or of imagined fiction, vividly arisen in his own mind, is a writer of good prose; and the better he succeeds in this faculty of mere exposition, the more he excels in the supreme character of lucidity, the better his prose. Prose which is redundant is worse than prose which is economic in the use of words, simply because economic prose is the better statement. Exactitude of word and idea makes better prose than the use of many synonyms and of vague terms, simply because it ministers to lucidity. A mass of adjectives or adverbs is usually (not always) ill in prose, because usually (not always) the idea which the writer has to express does not involve all these modifications; they only slip from his pen through laziness or through his having heard them before. It is obviously better prose to say, “I was tired out,” than to say, “I was wholly tired out,” unless for any particular reason you wish to express complete exhaustion. It is obviously better prose to say “the sky” than “the blue sky,” unless for any particular reason you are concerned in your description with the colour of the sky.
But the worst enemy of prose today is the snobbishness of rules and forms, the Mumbo-Jumbo of hieratic prescription.

===Chinese===

于是，詹姆士王译本希伯来经卷里那些被我们这代人当作范例念熟了的伟大段落，本质上是雄辩；从班扬那里摘引的段落，如《天路历程》精彩的收尾，也是；从卡莱尔那里摘引的每一段都是；连从纽曼那里、（说来遗憾）从我们现代最伟大的散文家赫胥黎那里摘引的段落，也是。

可散文理应同这一切区别开来。散文之卓越，在于它契合智能表达或叙述的功能。它是一种陈述；它的目的不是激发情感，而是清楚呈现——无论呈现一段记事（虚构的或事实的），还是呈现一个观念——作者想要传达给同侪的东西。

谁能把观念呈现得明白，不使任何修饰或次要观念搅浑这个过程；谁能在读者心中生动唤起一桩特定事实、或一段虚构想象的图像，同它在自己心中升起的一样生动，谁就是好散文家；他在这份单纯的陈述之才上愈成功，愈长于清明这一至高的品格，他的散文就愈好。冗赘的散文劣于用字节俭的散文，只因节俭的散文是更好的陈述。字与义的精确，胜过堆砌许多同义词和含糊字眼，只因它效力于清明。一大堆形容词或副词在散文里通常（不是总是）是病，因为通常（不是总是）作者要表达的意思并不包含这么多修饰；它们不过出于懒惰、出于耳熟才溜下他的笔。“我累极了”显然比“我完全彻底累极了”是好散文，除非你出于什么特别的理由要表达彻底的耗竭。“天空”显然比“蔚蓝的天空”是好散文，除非你的描写恰恰关心天空的颜色。

可散文今天最大的敌人，是对规则与格式的势利，是圣职人员式条规的蒙昧法。

===Original===

The influence of these is a very good example of that excellent rule laid down by Saint Thomas Aquinas that all evil exists in mistaking, or misusing, the means for the end. This plague of pedantry does not rage quite as severely as it did when I was young, but it is still pretty severe. You are told that it is good prose, for instance, to have as few adjectives as possible. That is nonsense. It is good prose to have as many adjectives as you need, and *no more*. You are told, as a sort of eleventh commandment, never to split an infinitive; the rule is that of an excellent master of English, the late Professor Ker, who adds, “except when euphony demands it.” I would go further; I would say that when the spoken language has arrived, as ours has today, at the universal use of the split infinitive, the written language may follow at some little distance behind. We are told that prose must never be emphatic, or excited, or this, or that, or the other. All that is nonsense. Prose must be emphatic when special emphasis is to be expressed, excited when excitement is to be expressed, and so forth. But certainly the character of good prose is the subordination of everything in it to the end, which is expression of what you have to say; and that is the distinction between good prose and rhetoric and verse. For in rhetoric and in verse the end is the emotion you desire, non-rationally, to excite; but in prose the end is rational presentation, narration, or statement of thesis: nothing more.
Judged by such standards, the two best prose writers in English are, I think, Dean Inge and Mr. Gosse; for they, each of them, write on subjects where they have much to say, with the use of no words other than those needed for such expression, and they put those words invariably in the right order. Cecil Chesterton is dead.
I had almost written that there is no such thing as “great” prose, in the sense in which there is “great” rhetoric or “great” verse. The very best prose may be dull if the subject expressed is dull to the reader. You will get no better prose, for instance, than Newman’s *Arians of the Fourth Century*. It is not dull to me, because I happen to be interested in that bit of history, but it would be fiercely dull to any reader who was not. Nevertheless it is first-rate prose.

===Chinese===

这些东西的影响，正好例证圣托马斯·阿奎那立下的那条卓越规则：一切恶，都生于把手段错认或误用为目的。这场迂腐的瘟疫不比我年轻时那样猖獗了，但仍然相当猖獗。譬如有人告诉你：形容词越少越是好散文。那是胡说。好散文是形容词要用多少有多少，一个不多。有人告诫你，如同第十一诫：永不拆分不定式；这规则出自一位卓越的英语大师、已故的克尔教授，他补了一句，“除非音韵需要”。我还要更进一步；我说，当口头语言——如我们今天的语言——已达到拆分不定式的普遍使用，书面语不妨在后面隔着一小段距离跟上。有人告诉我们，散文绝不可有力、绝不可激动，不可这样、不可那样。全是胡说。散文在要表达特别的强调时就该有力，在要表达激动时就该激动，如此等等。但好散文的品格确实是：其中一切都从属于目的，即把你不得不说的话表达出来；这就是好散文同雄辩、同诗的区别。因为在雄辩与诗里，目的在于你渴望非理性地激起的情感；而在散文里，目的是理性的呈现、叙述或论题的陈述：仅此而已。

照这样的标准，英语里最好的两位散文家，我想是英奇院长和戈斯先生；因为他们俩，在有许多话可说的题目上写，用的字不多于表达所需，而且那些字的次序永远摆得对。塞西尔·切斯特顿死了。

我几乎要写下：不存在“伟大”的散文这种东西——在存在“伟大”的雄辩或“伟大”的诗的那种意义上。最好的散文，倘若它表达的题目对读者乏味，也可能乏味。你举不出比纽曼的《第四世纪的亚流派》更好的散文了。它于我不乏味，因为我恰好对那一段历史感兴趣；可对不感兴趣的读者，它会乏味得要命。然而它是第一流的散文。

===Original===

Newman, having to write about a particular thing upon which he had made himself immensely learned, having to tell a certain number of facts, and to express a certain number of ideas, does so with the best choice of words in the best order﻿—and that is prose.
The very fact that any sound definition of prose involves many negative and only one positive (to wit, the positive need for lucid expression) makes it true that good prose is a common art. Excellence in prose is not common to all: far from it; but the faculties whereby excellence is reached are common to all. A man who is a first-rate walker, who carries his body with an exact poise, who can walk far without fatigue, and at a good pace, is a rare man; but we can all walk, and we all approach in various degrees the standard that man has reached. So it is with prose. Those who think that good prose must have something odd about it﻿—a few archaic words, little eighteenth-century tricks here and there, subjunctives popped in, like currants into a cake﻿—these resemble men who in walking every now and then in high spirits do a little dance for fun. They are free to do so; we are not displeased. We cannot help noticing them (which is, perhaps, their object), and no one is the worse for the *gambado*. But in so far as they indulge in the fantasy, they are not walking; and if they do it the least bit too much, they are playing the fool. A wise man said to me once, “Don’t go and get your words from the artist colourman’s.” Would that I had remembered his words in youth as I now humbly acknowledge them in age.
But the rhetorician wisely indulges in extravagance, and as for the poet, he may use what words he likes, and go to any extreme, or to any excess, so long only as he hits the mark. For it is with poetry as with love and with singing in tune. It is with poetry as with the sense of reality. It is with poetry as with the toothache. Either you have it, or you have it not.
There is no getting near poetry; and though there are degrees in it, the boundary between its being and its not being is as sharp as a razor: on the one side is It, and on the other is nothingness.

===Chinese===

纽曼要写一件他已使自己学识浩瀚的特定事情，要讲一定数量的事实，表达一定数量的观念；他用最好的字、按最好的次序去做——这便是散文。

任何健全的散文定义都含许多否定、只有一个肯定（即清明表达这一必需）——正是这一事实，使好散文成为一门公共的技艺。散文的卓越不是人人共有的：远非如此；但达到卓越所凭的官能，是人人共有的。一个人是第一流的步行者，身体带着恰好的平衡，能走远而不倦，步子又快——这样的人是罕见的；可我们人人都会走路，都在不同程度上接近那人达到的标准。散文正是如此。那些以为好散文必得带点古怪——几个古字，这里那里一点十八世纪的小巧，虚拟语气像蛋糕里的葡萄干一样撒进去——的人，正像步行的人兴之所至，时不时蹦一小段舞取乐。他们尽可以这样；我们不恼。我们免不了注意到他们（也许那正是他们的目的），谁也没因这蹦跳吃亏。可就其纵情于这份奇想而言，他们不是在走路；做得稍稍过头，便是在出洋相。一位智者曾对我说：“别去画材商那里买你的词。”但愿我年轻时记住了这句话，如我今日在暮年谦卑地承认它。

可修辞家放纵于铺张是明智的；至于诗人，他爱用什么词就用什么词，走得多远、使到多过都随他，只要他中的。因为诗之为物，如同爱情，如同唱得准调。诗如同实在之感。诗如同牙疼。你要么有，要么没有。

诗没有“接近”可言；诗中虽有程度之差，它在与不在之间的界线却利如剃刀：一边是它，一边是虚无。

===Original===

By which I do not mean to say that poetry is only found in certain violent stabs of emotion such as Shakespeare and Keats can launch, for it often inhabits page upon page, as throughout (I will maintain) all the Fourth Book of “Paradise Lost,” and countless other great achievements of the past in every tongue. But I mean that, in a long flight or a short one, immediate or continuous, poetry is poetry, and not to be mistaken for anything else. Charles Kingsley (I ought to have dragged this in at Appledore, and not have waited for Rye) said to a woman, “Madam, there is poetry and there is verse; and verse is divided into two kinds﻿—good verse and bad verse. What you have here shown me is not poetry, it is verse. It is not good verse, it is bad verse.”

===Chinese===

我这样说，并不是说诗只存在于莎士比亚和济慈所能发出的那些暴烈的情感猛刺里；因为它常常栖居于连绵的许多页，如（我始终坚持）《失乐园》整个第四卷，如往昔每一种文字里无数别的伟大成就。我的意思是：不论长飞短飞，不论转瞬还是绵延，诗就是诗，不会被误认作别的东西。查尔斯·金斯利（这话我本该在阿普尔多尔就扯进来，不该拖到拉伊）对一位女士说：“夫人，世上有诗，也有韵文；韵文分两种——好韵文和坏韵文。您方才给我看的这个，不是诗，是韵文。而且不是好韵文，是坏韵文。”

===Original===

But, really, if I go on in this endless way of discussing the difficult trade of letters, I shall never get her nose round for the sea again. I had almost forgot I was sailing. What! Of two such occupations, the benediction of dealing with the sea and the degradation of scribbling, can you forget the better for the worse? I can. I am of a fallen race. I follow downwards.
But I am aboard the *Nona* again, and must put her out down-channel. This will I now do, and sail out of Rye by the first of the ebb under too strong a northeast wind at two o’clock in the morning with the waning moon just rising, the sky clear, two reefs in my mainsail, the second jib, and a reef in my foresail, knowing well that I must watch all night, and that I shall get no sleep till morning. For it is one of the glories of sailing that you are under the authority of the heavens, and must submit to the whole world of water and of air, of which you are a part, not making laws to yourself capriciously, but acting as servant or brother of universal things.
It would be no bad thing if someone (I am not competent) were to draw up a list of advices for the brutish man who is too poor to sail a big boat, or is not such a fool as to desire one: I mean a list of advices for a man who sails such a boat as the *Nona*: say, boats from seven tons to twenty.
The first article in such a list would be this: *Cruising is not racing.*
But on this one could write a whole volume, with digressions upon the general evils and corruptions of these our latter times when the Beast has most certainly been let loose out of the Pit, and is going about the world breathing smoke out of seven mouths, and stamping 666, with his hoof, on the foreheads of all his elect.
For no one can doubt that the practice of sailing, which renews in us all the past of our blood, has been abominably corrupted by racing. I do not know whence the evil came, but I suppose it came like most evils, from a love of money. The love of money made men admire the possessors of it, and so they came to think of sailing as they do of riding horses, or of any other sport﻿—as something to be tested by what the rich man could do. And clearly, when it came to making machines for going fast through the water by the aid of the wind alone, the rich man had his advantage.

===Chinese===

可是说真的，再这样没完没了地讨论文字这门艰难的行当，我就再也没法把她的船头转回海上去了。我几乎忘了自己正在航行。什么！在这样两种营生之间——与海打交道的祝福，和涂鸦的堕落——你竟会为次的忘了好的么？我会。我属于一个堕落的种族。我随波向下。

可我又上了“诺娜”号，必须把她放出港去下海峡。这就去办：凌晨两点，趁落潮的头一段，顶着过强的东北风驶出拉伊，残月刚起，天宇澄澈，主帆收着两个结，挂着二号前帆，前桅帆也收了一结——心里明白必须整夜守望，天亮前休想合眼。因为航海的荣耀之一，正在你受天象的管辖，必须顺服于水与风的整个世界——你是它的一部分——不是任性给自己立法，而是充当万物之仆、万物之弟。

倘若有人（我不胜任）肯替那种穷得养不起大船、也没傻到想要大船的粗人拟一张须知——我是说，替驾驶“诺娜”号这样船的人拟一张须知：譬如七吨到二十吨的船——那倒不是坏事。

这样一张单子的头一条会是：巡航不是竞赛。

单这一条就能写整整一卷书，还要岔出去谈谈我们这末世种种的弊恶与败坏——兽确确实实已从坑里放了出来，正走遍世界，七张口喷着烟，用蹄子把六六六盖在它全体选民的前额上。

因为谁也不能怀疑，航海这门重续我们血脉全部往昔的实践，已被竞赛败坏得不成样子。这恶从何来我不知道，但我想它同多数恶一样，从爱钱来。爱钱使人艳羡有钱的人，于是人们看航海，就像看骑马、看别的运动一样——当作一件拿富人做得到什么来检验的东西。而显然，一旦论到造凭风快驶水上的机器，富人是占便宜的。

===Original===

Then I suppose there came in also that craze for measurable things which has done us all so much harm in the last lifetime and a half. When I say of the *Nona*, “She is a good boat,” when I stroke her and she purrs, there is nothing here measurable. When I say of that really abominable boat [*name censored*] (well worthy of her name)﻿—that I had rather she had been sunk at birth than continue to sail the seas, you cannot apply a single measurable test to prove that the solid, noble *Nona* is good and the flimsy, whimsy [*name censored*] is bad; but in racing you can. Boat A gets in front of Boat B. Any fool can test that, and today was made for fools.
Well, then, whatever the root of the evil, evil it is, and it has corrupted our way of living in happy little boats. Remember, and write it down: “*Cruising is not racing.*” If your boat is a home and a companion, and at the same time a genius that takes you from place to place and, what is much more, a good angel, revealing unexpected things, and a comforter and an introducer to the Infinite Verities﻿—and my boat is all these things﻿—then you must put away from yourself altogether the idea of racing, as much as, when you consider a wife, you must put away the intellectual woman and the waxwork girl﻿—both of whom may have their place for all I know. The cruiser, the strong little, deep little boat, is all I have called it. It is a complete satisfaction for man; but if you let in racing you are letting in the serpent.
Here you will say, “Have you then never raced?” Never, sweetheart, except to get away from danger, which I loathe. I have never raced another boat in my life, although I have been on other men’s boats when they were racing, and admired their special talent, just as I would admire the talent of a man who successfully climbed the greasy pole. You must expect of your boat﻿—your companion boat﻿—a rational behaviour. You must say to yourself that when she is doing seven knots, she is doing well, and when she is doing nine, she is excited, and will be the better for a night’s rest.
When you are on a long passage, even with steady weather, you had better bank on three to four knots and no more. For what with beating, fishing perhaps, the falling of wind, over-reefing in terror, and the rest of it, you will not do more.

===Chinese===

随后我想，又捻进来那股对可测量之物的狂热——近一个半世纪以来它害我们害得好苦。当我说“诺娜”号“是条好船”、我抚着她、她呜呜地响，这里没有任何可测的东西。当我说起那条真正可憎的船〔船名从略〕（人如其名，名副其实）——说她不如一出世就沉了，也别再在海面上晃——你拿不出一条可测的检验，来证明敦实高贵的“诺娜”号好、轻浮善变的〔船名从略〕坏；可竞赛里有。甲船跑在乙船前面。任何傻瓜都验得了这个，而今天正是为傻瓜造的。

那么，无论这恶的根在哪儿，它就是恶，而且它败坏了我们在快乐小船里的生活方式。记住，写下来：“巡航不是竞赛。”倘若你的船是一个家、一位伴侣，同时又是一个带你四处去的精魂，更是一位好天使，为你揭示意想不到的事物，一位安慰者、一位把你引见给无限真理的引路人——我的船就是这一切——那你就必须把竞赛的念头整个抛开，正如你考虑妻子时必须抛开女学究和蜡人姑娘——这两者或许也自有其位置，我无从知道。巡航艇，这结实的小、深沉的小船，就是我说的这一切。它给人完全的满足；可你一旦放竞赛进来，就是放蛇进来。

这里你要说了：“那你自己从没赛过？”从没有过，亲爱的，除了逃离危险时——那是我痛恨的。我平生从未同别的船赛过，虽然我搭过别人赛船的船，也钦佩他们那份特别的才具，正如我会钦佩谁利索地爬上一根抹油的杆子。你必须对你的船——你的伴侣船——期待一种理性的行为。你必须对自己说：她跑七节时，是跑得好；跑九节时，是兴奋了，歇一夜会对她有好处。

跑长程时，哪怕天气稳定，你也最好按三四节盘算，多则不必。因为又要抢风，也许还要钓钓鱼，风又会落，又会吓得以至过度收帆，等等，你走不出更多。

===Original===

I have always thought to myself, knocking up and down the coasts of dear England, that if I did a hundred miles in my twenty-four hours, I was doing very well indeed.
Once I spent a whole day drifting with the tide from the two Etaples Lights to the Dune, and very nearly all the way back, but even that did not persuade me to a motor, for, of all things abominable to God and His Saints, I know of nothing more abominable than machinery and petrol and the rest on board a little cruising boat.
I would rather die of thirst, ten miles off the headlands in a brazen calm, having lost my dinghy in the previous storm, than have on board what is monstrously called today an “auxiliary.” The name is worthy of the thing. By auxiliaries the Roman army perished. Further, it is a nasty foreign sort of term. Call it the machine and tell the truth. I am told by those who use the abomination that it is ashamed of itself, and often will not start, as though to say, “You came out to sail the seas, and I am reluctant to cheat wind, weather, and tide in your favour.” I will not deny that mechanism is valuable to those who conduct huge hordes, the rich and the poor, from Europe to the New World, but in a little cruising boat I will have none of it. It would be foolish to be rid of it in armament, for there it does give one’s country a great advantage, but in a little cruising boat it is as much out of place as is electric light where one should use candles, or as are motorcars in the hunting of wild beasts. But I must return to things more practical.
My next rule would be: *Get everything shipshape and, so far as you can, keep it shipshape.*
That is, see that the falls are clear, that everything is properly stowed away; that you know where everything is to be got at in any emergency; that you have to look for nothing; that you make the most of every space﻿—and the rest of it.
All this I say knowing full well that I myself am incapable of it; but I am giving advice, I am not practising. I am expending myself for the good of others, and what I myself do is no affair of yours. The best known and the most tragic tag descended to us from antiquity is, “*Video meliora proboque, deteriora sequor.

===Chinese===

沿着亲爱的英格兰的海岸上上下下，我一向心里盘算：二十四小时里能跑一百英里，就实在干得不错了。

有一回，我随潮漂了整整一天，从埃塔普勒的两盏灯漂到迪纳沙丘，又几乎一路漂回来；可就连那样也没劝动我装马达。因为凡天主及其诸圣所憎之物，我不知道还有什么比机器和汽油之类更可憎——在小巡航船上。

我宁可渴死在离岬十英里的铜色死寂里——上一场风暴里又丢了小划艇——也不带那件今天被荒唐地叫作“辅机”的东西。这名字配这东西。罗马军团就亡于辅军。再者，这是个下等的外来词。就叫它机器，说真话。用这可憎之物的人告诉我，它自己害臊，常常不肯发动，仿佛在说：“你出来是航海的，我不愿替你骗风、骗天、骗潮。”我不否认机械对那些把大群的人——富人和穷人——从欧洲运往新世界的人有价值；可小巡航船上，我一点也不要它。在军备里去掉它是蠢的，因为在那儿它确实给国家大便宜；可小巡航船上，它之不合于位置，正如该用蜡烛处用电灯，正如猎野兽用汽车。可我得回到更实际的事上来。

我的下一条规矩是：把一切收拾得井井有条，并尽力保持井井有条。

也就是说，查看索绳都理顺，一切妥善收置；确保急难时什么都伸手即得；什么都不必现找；把每一寸空间用到十足——等等。

说这一切时我心知肚明：我自己做不到；可我是在给忠告，不是在践行。我在为他人之好耗着自己，我自己干什么不干你们的事。从古代传下来的那句最著名也最悲惨的老话说得好：“我见善而赞之，择恶而随之。”

===Original===

*” Some would say that they are not only the most tragic words the great pagans bequeathed to us﻿—the pagans upon whom we all repose﻿—but would add that they are also the most ironic; for if you stop a little after the *proboque*, and add in another voice the following two words, you will appreciate the sardonic accent.
My own boat has usually come into port more like the disturbed nest of a dormouse than like the spick and span arrangement which I advise.
